Isotropy, anisotropies and non-Gaussianity in the scalar-induced gravitational-wave background: diagrammatic approach for primordial non-Gaussianity up to arbitrary order
Jun-Peng Li, Sai Wang, Zhi-Chao Zhao, Kazunori Kohri

Abstract
Produced nonlinearly by the enhanced linear cosmological curvature perturbations, the scalar-induced gravitational waves (SIGWs) can serve as a potentially powerful probe of primordial non-Gaussianity (PNG) in the early Universe. In this work, we comprehensively investigate the imprints of local-type PNG on the SIGW background beyond the widely used quadratic and cubic approximations. We extend the diagrammatic approach to simplify the calculation of the SIGW energy density spectrum with high-order PNG, thereby facilitating systematic analysis for PNG up to arbitrary order. Following this approach, we derive semi-analytic formulas for the energy-density fraction spectrum, the angular power spectrum, and the angular bispectrum and trispectrum to describe the isotropic component, anisotropies, and non-Gaussianity of the SIGW background, respectively. Particularly, focusing on PNG up to quartic approximation (parameterized by f NL f_{\mathrm{NL}} , g NL g_{\mathrm{NL}} , and h NL h_{\mathrm{NL}} ), we numerically compute all contributions to these SIGW spectra. We find that PNG can significantly alter the magnitude of the SIGW energy-density spectrum, and can generate substantial anisotropies through the initial inhomogeneities in the SIGW distribution. Furthermore, we observe that the SIGW angular bispectrum and trispectrum always vanish when the primordial curvature perturbations are Gaussian; otherwise, they do not, indicating their potential utility as probes of PNG. Therefore, we anticipate that the SIGW background will provide essential information about the early Universe.



arXiv:
2505.16820

Introduction

Gravitational waves arising from the nonlinear interactions of linear cosmological curvature perturbations at horizon reentry, dubbed scalar-induced gravitational waves
Ananda et al. (2007)
;
Baumann et al. (2007)
;
Espinosa et al. (2018)
;
Kohri and Terada (2018)
;
Tomita (1967)
;
Matarrese et al. (1993)
;
Matarrese et al. (1994)
;
Matarrese et al. (1998)
;
Mollerach et al. (2004)
;
Carbone and Matarrese (2005)
;
Assadullahi and Wands (2010)
;
Domènech (2021)
, emerge as a promising avenue for exploring the early Universe.
It is well known that prior to the last-scattering surface, the Universe exhibited opacity to electromagnetic waves while remaining transparent to gravitational waves
Bartolo et al. (2018)
;
Flauger and Weinberg (2019)
.
Consequently, scalar-induced gravitational waves produced in the early Universe play a pivotal role in conveying valuable information about epochs characterized by a minuscule comoving Hubble horizon, which are inaccessible to observations of the cosmic microwave background and large-scale structure.
The detection of scalar-induced gravitational waves establishes a groundbreaking avenue for understanding the properties and evolution of cosmological scalar metric perturbations at small scales.
On one hand, it provides insights into the dynamics during the later stages of inflation which predicts enhanced curvature power spectra at small scales (see Refs.
Özsoy and Tasinato (2023)
;
Gammal and others (2025)
and the references therein).
On the other hand, the detection of scalar-induced gravitational waves sheds light on the nature of cosmological fluids in the early Universe, such as the equation of state
Kohri and Terada (2018)
;
Inomata et al. (2019b)
;
Inomata et al. (2019a)
;
Hajkarim and Schaffner-Bielich (2020)
;
Domènech (2020)
;
Domènech et al. (2020)
;
Abe et al. (2021)
;
Domènech (2021)
;
Escriva et al. (2024)
;
Escrivà et al. (2025)
, adiabaticity
Domènech et al. (2022)
;
Domènech (2024)
;
Chen and Liu (2025)
;
Yuan et al. (2024)
;
Luo et al. (2025)
;
Yu and Wang (2025a)
, dissipative effects
Yu and Wang (2025b)
;
Domènech and Chluba (2025)
, and more.
In particular, scalar-induced gravitational waves can serve as valuable tools in the search for primordial black holes, which have been proposed as possible constituents of dark matter
Carr and Kuhnel (2020)
;
Green and Kavanagh (2021)
;
Carr and Kuhnel (2022)
;
Carr et al. (2024)
;
Escrivà et al. (2022a)
.
The formation of primordial black holes can occur concurrently with the production of scalar-induced gravitational waves, stemming from the gravitational collapse of large overdensities where scalar perturbations achieve sufficient amplitudes
Hawking (1971)
;
Carr and Hawking (1974)
;
Carr (1975)
;
Chapline (1975)
.
Therefore, the detection of scalar-induced gravitational waves has the potential to impose constraints on the mass distribution of primordial black holes and vice versa
Sasaki et al. (2018)
;
Bagui and others (2025)
;
Gammal and others (2025)
.
Nonetheless, the scalar-induced gravitational wave background is mixed with other stochastic gravitational-wave backgrounds, such as the gravitational waves emitted from binary black holes
Abbott and others (2016)
, posing challenges in distinguishing scalar-induced gravitational wave signals from these superposed stochastic gravitational-wave backgrounds
Christensen (2019)
.
A comprehensive analysis of the statistics of the scalar-induced gravitational wave background is imperative, encompassing the energy-density fraction spectrum, angular power spectrum, angular bispectrum, and trispectrum of scalar-induced gravitational waves, which characterize the isotropic component, anisotropies, and non-Gaussianity of scalar-induced gravitational wave energy density, respectively.

Scalar-induced gravitational waves sourced from enhanced and non-Gaussian curvature perturbations at small scales have sparked great interest due to their association with the formation of primordial black holes.
Existing studies have demonstrated that the presence of primordial non-Gaussianity
Maldacena (2003)
;
Bartolo et al. (2004)
;
Allen et al. (1987)
;
Bartolo et al. (2002)
;
Acquaviva et al. (2003)
;
Bernardeau and Uzan (2002)
;
Chen et al. (2007)
;
Byrnes and Choi (2010)
;
Wands (2010)
;
Meerburg and others (2019)
;
Achúcarro and others (2022)
not only has a substantial impact on the abundance of primordial black holes significantly
Bullock and Primack (1997)
;
Byrnes et al. (2012)
;
Young and Byrnes (2013)
;

;

, but also leaves remarkable imprints on the scalar-induced gravitational wave background

;

;

;
Rey (2024)
;
Pi (2024)
;
Zhou et al. (2025)
.
These studies predominantly concentrate on local-type primordial non-Gaussianity within the quadratic and cubic approximations, characterized by the non-linear parameters
f_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
g_{\mathrm{NL}}
, respectively.
Here, we present a concise overview on these studies according to three aspects, i.e., the isotropic component, anisotropies, and non-Gaussianity of scalar-induced gravitational wave energy density, respectively.
Regarding the isotropic component of scalar-induced gravitational waves, contributions from local-type primordial non-Gaussianity at
f_{\mathrm{NL}}
order to the energy-density fraction spectrum have been shown in Refs.
Adshead et al. (2021b)
;
Ragavendra (2022)
;
Li et al. (2023a)
, and further investigations for primordial non-Gaussianity up to
g_{\mathrm{NL}}
order have been carried out in Refs.
Yuan et al. (2023)
;
Li et al. (2024b)
;
Ruiz and Rey (2025)
.
Refs.
Abe et al. (2023)
;
Perna et al. (2024)
have investigated the contributions associated with primordial non-Gaussianity up to quintic approximations, retaining the terms up to quartic order in the amplitude of the primordial curvature power spectrum.
These studies have illustrated that the consideration of
f_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
g_{\mathrm{NL}}
can lead to significant alterations in the energy-density fraction spectrum by orders of magnitude, particularly within the anticipated sensitivity ranges of upcoming or futuristic gravitational wave detection initiatives
Baker and others (2019)
;
Smith et al. (2019)
;
Hu and Wu (2017)
;
Wang and Han (2021)
;
Ren et al. (2023)
;
Luo and others (2016)
;
Mei and others (2021)
;
Seto et al. (2001)
;
Kawamura and others (2021)
;
Crowder and Cornish (2005)
;

;
Capurri et al. (2023)
;
Hobbs and others (2010)
;
Demorest and others (2013)
;
Kramer and Champion (2013)
;
Manchester and others (2013)
;
Sesana et al. (2008)
;
Thrane and Romano (2013)
;
Janssen and others (2015)
;
Dewdney et al. (2009)
;
Weltman and others (2020)
;
Moore et al. (2015)
;
Abbott and others (2018)
;
Aasi and others (2015)
;
Chen et al. (2022b)
.
Other related works exploring the effects of primordial non-Gaussianity on the energy-density fraction spectrum of scalar-induced gravitational waves have been shown in Refs.
Wang et al. (2024a)
;
Yu and Wang (2024)
;
Iovino et al. (2024a)
;
Zeng et al. (2024)
;
Cai et al. (2019a)
;
Unal (2019)
;
Atal and Domènech (2021)
;
Yuan and Huang (2021)
;
Chang et al. (2024)
;
Nakama et al. (2017)
;
Garcia-Bellido et al. (2017)
;
Ragavendra et al. (2021)
;
Zhang (2022)
;
Lin et al. (2023)
;
Chen et al. (2022a)
;
Cai et al. (2019b)
;
Garcia-Saenz et al. (2023)
;
Pi (2024)
;
Papanikolaou et al. (2024)
;
He et al. (2024)
.
Moreover, the presence of local-type primordial non-Gaussianity induces large-scale initial inhomogeneities at the scalar-induced gravitational wave emission, resulting in the anisotropies on the skymap that may surpass the propagation effects.
Refs.
Bartolo et al. (2020a)
;
Li et al. (2023a)
have demonstrated that the primordial non-Gaussianity at
f_{\mathrm{NL}}
order can induce significant anisotropies in the scalar-induced gravitational wave background, with further investigations extending to primordial non-Gaussianity at
g_{\mathrm{NL}}
order in Refs.
Li et al. (2024b)
;
Ruiz and Rey (2025)
.
Regarding non-Gaussianity in the scalar-induced gravitational wave background, Ref.
Bartolo et al. (2020a)
provided an approximate expression for the angular bispectrum associated with primordial non-Gaussianity at
f_{\mathrm{NL}}
order, while a comprehensive analysis of the angular bispectrum and trispectrum for primordial non-Gaussianity up to
g_{\mathrm{NL}}
order was presented in Ref.
Li et al. (2024a)
.
These studies underscore that the scalar-induced gravitational wave non-Gaussianity results from the squeezed primordial non-Gaussianity, suggesting that the angular bispectrum and trispectrum of scalar-induced gravitational waves could serve as probes of the primordial non-Gaussianity.
However, primordial non-Gaussianity resulting from distinct physical mechanisms exhibits significant features.
The nonlinear parameters
f_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
g_{\mathrm{NL}}
are insufficient for a comprehensive characterization of primordial non-Gaussianity.
A recent study
Iovino et al. (2024a)
argues that for logarithmic primordial non-Gaussianity, the amplitude of the scalar-induced gravitational wave energy-density spectrum may significantly deviate from predictions made based on the quadratic approximation.

The imprints of a more general form of primordial non-Gaussianity on the scalar-induced gravitational wave background warrants particular attention, particularly in scenarios where the primordial curvature perturbation
\zeta
can be represented as a function of its Gaussian component
\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}
.
Our objective is to incorporate primordial non-Gaussianity beyond the commonly employed quadratic (
f_{\mathrm{NL}}
order) and cubic (
g_{\mathrm{NL}}
order) approximations, and develop a methodology capable of encompassing a full power-series expansion to all orders based on the diagrammatic approach
Byrnes et al. (2007)
.
This endeavor is essential given the applicability of the
\delta N
formalism
Sasaki and Stewart (1996)
;
Sasaki and Tanaka (1998)
;
Lyth et al. (2005)
;
Lyth and Rodriguez (2005)
in expressing the primordial curvature perturbation
\zeta
as a series expansion in terms of its Gaussian component
\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}
in various inflationary models, such as single-field inflation with an ultra-slow-roll phase
Atal et al. (2019)
and two-field curvaton models
Sasaki et al. (2006)
;
Pi and Sasaki (2023)
.
As long as the deviation from the distribution of
\zeta
from a Gaussian statistics remains within the perturbative regime, the discrepancy put forward in Ref.
Iovino et al. (2024a)
is expected to be eliminated in sufficiently high-order series expansion of
\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}
.
Although our present study does not explore concrete scenarios, our methodology is readily applicable to specific inflation models, including cases where the scale dependencies of non-linear parameters are non-negligible.
In particular, we will present a detailed example of primordial non-Gaussianity up to quartic approximation (i.e.,
h_{\mathrm{NL}}
order), with explicit expressions and numerical results for the energy density spectrum and angular correlation functions provided for analysis.

The paper is structured as follows.
In Section
2
, we review the energy density of scalar-induced gravitational waves, both during production and in the present day, with a basic diagrammatic approach introduced subsequently.
Section
3
delves into the energy-density fraction spectra of scalar-induced gravitational waves at the isotropic background level in the presence of primordial non-Gaussianity by developing the “renormalized” diagrammatic approach.
The angular power spectrum for the anisotropies in scalar-induced gravitational waves is analyzed in Section
4
, considering contributions from initial inhomogeneities and propagation effects at the perturbation level.
Section
5
explores the angular bispectrum and trispectrum of scalar-induced gravitational waves in a manner similar to the angular power spectrum.
In Section
6
, we broaden the scope of this study to investigate the general infrared behaviors of the scalar-induced gravitational wave energy-density spectra, impacts of the trispectrum of primordial curvature perturbations on scalar-induced gravitational waves, the applicability of our methodology to scenarios involving scale-dependent primordial non-Gaussianity parameters, and the relationship between the primordial black hole formation and the scalar-induced gravitational wave background.
Finally, our conclusions are presented in Section
7
.
In addition, supplementary materials providing a brief summary of Wigner symbols are presented in Appendix
A
, while further details on the theoretical fundamentals of scalar-induced gravitational waves production can be found in Appendix
B
.
Appendix
C
provides a detailed list of specific diagrams contributing to the energy-density fraction spectrum and primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneities.
Appendix
D
illustrates the relevant numerical integration techniques, while Appendix
F
offers specifics on the Boltzmann equation of cosmological gravitational waves.

2
Energy density of SIGWs

In this section, we review the energy density of scalar-induced gravitational waves, both at the time of production and in the present day.
In particular, we introduce a diagrammatic approach for computing the energy density of scalar-induced gravitational waves in the presence of primordial non-Gaussianity, with detailed calculations and results elaborated in subsequent sections.

In order to characterize the scalar-induced gravitational wave background, we define the energy-density full spectrum
\omega_{\mathrm{gw}}
through the energy density
\rho_{\mathrm{gw}}
as follows

\rho_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,\mathbf{x})=\rho_{\mathrm{c}}(\eta)\int\mathrm{d}\ln q\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{n}\,\omega_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})\ ,

(1)

where
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}=3\mathcal{H}^{2}m_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}/a^{2}
represents the critical energy density of the Universe with
m_{\mathrm{Pl}}=1/\sqrt{8\pi G}
denoting the Planck mass and
\mathcal{H}(\eta)
representing the conformal Hubble parameter at conformal time
\eta
.
Moreover, we use
\mathbf{q}=q\mathbf{n}
to represent the 3-momentum of the gravitons, where
q
is the comoving scalar-induced gravitational wave wavenumber and
\mathbf{n}
stands for the scalar-induced gravitational wave propagation direction.
To relate
\omega_{\mathrm{gw}}
to the observable scalar-induced gravitational wave strain
h_{ij}
, we recall the energy density of scalar-induced gravitational waves on subhorizon scales as presented in Ref.
Maggiore (2000)
, i.e.,

\rho_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,\mathbf{x})=\frac{m_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}{16a^{2}(\eta)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\partial_{l}h_{ij}(\eta,\mathbf{x})\partial_{l}h_{ij}(\eta,\mathbf{x})\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\ ,

(2)

where
a(\eta)
is the scale factor of the Universe and the long overbar labels a spacetime average over a region large compared to the gravitational wave wavelength but small compared to the horizon scales.
Furthermore, it is useful to decompose
h_{ij}
in Fourier modes with different polarization tensors as

\displaystyle h_{ij}(\eta,\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{q}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}e^{i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{x}}h_{ij}(\eta,\mathbf{q})

(3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda=+,\times}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{q}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}e^{i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\epsilon_{ij}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{q})h_{\lambda}(\eta,\mathbf{q})\ ,

where
\epsilon^{+}_{ij}(\mathbf{q})=\left(\epsilon_{i}(\mathbf{q})\epsilon_{j}(\mathbf{q})-\bar{\epsilon}_{i}(\mathbf{q})\bar{\epsilon}_{j}(\mathbf{q})\right)/\sqrt{2}
and
\epsilon^{\times}_{ij}(\mathbf{q})=\left(\epsilon_{i}(\mathbf{q})\bar{\epsilon}_{j}(\mathbf{q})+\bar{\epsilon}_{i}(\mathbf{q})\epsilon_{j}(\mathbf{q})\right)/\sqrt{2}
denote the polarization tensors, with the vectors
\epsilon_{i}(\mathbf{q})
,
\bar{\epsilon}_{i}(\mathbf{q})
, and
\mathbf{n}
forming an orthonormal basis in the three-dimensional momentum space, i.e.,
\epsilon_{ij}^{\lambda}\epsilon_{ij}^{\lambda^{\prime}}=\delta^{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}
and
q^{i}\epsilon_{i}(\mathbf{q})=q^{i}\bar{\epsilon}_{i}(\mathbf{q})=0
by construction.
The power spectrum of scalar-induced gravitational waves
P_{h_{\lambda}}
is then defined as the two-point correlator of
h_{\lambda}
, i.e.,

\langle h_{\lambda}(\eta,\mathbf{q})h_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\eta,\mathbf{q}^{\prime})\rangle=\delta_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}\delta^{(3)}(\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{q}^{\prime})P_{h_{\lambda}}(\eta,q)\ .

(4)

By combining the above formulae Eqs. (
1
,
2
,
3
),
\omega_{\mathrm{gw}}
can be written in the form of

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})=-\frac{q^{3}}{48\mathcal{H}^{2}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\left[\left(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}\right)\cdot\mathbf{q}\right]\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muh_{ij}(\eta,\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})h_{ij}(\eta,\mathbf{q})\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\ .

(5)

Notably, here and hereinafter, we use
\mathbf{k}
as the Fourier counterpart of the position
\mathbf{x}
.

2.1
Production of SIGWs

First of all, we revisit the calculation of
\omega_{\mathrm{gw}}
at the initial moment
\eta_{\mathrm{in}}
, namely the time of scalar-induced gravitational wave production.
Arising from the non-linear interactions of curvature perturbations, scalar-induced gravitational waves are associated with the linear primordial curvature perturbations
\zeta
through
Ananda et al. (2007)
;
Baumann et al. (2007)
;
Espinosa et al. (2018)
;
Kohri and Terada (2018)

\displaystyle h_{\lambda}(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{q}_{a}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}\zeta(\mathbf{q}_{a})\zeta(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{a})Q_{\lambda}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{q}_{a})\hat{I}({\left|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{a}\right|},q,\eta_{\mathrm{in}})\ ,

(6)

where
Q_{\lambda}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{q}_{a})
denotes the projection factor,
\hat{I}(|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{a}|,q,\eta_{\mathrm{in}})
represents the kernel function, and the subscript
in
indicates the production time.
A concise derivation of this formula can be found in Refs.
Ananda et al. (2007)
;
Baumann et al. (2007)
;
Espinosa et al. (2018)
;
Kohri and Terada (2018)
, with the same conventions as in Refs.
Li et al. (2023a)
;
Li et al. (2024b)
;
Li et al. (2024a)
.
The specific expressions for
Q_{\lambda}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{q}_{a})
and
\hat{I}(|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{a}|,q,\eta_{\mathrm{in}})
are provided in Eq. (
136
) and Eqs. (
148
,
149
), respectively.
The preceding expressions, Eqs. (
3
,
5
,
6
), suggest that the initial energy-density full spectrum, denoted as
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})=\omega_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{q})
, can be schematically represented as
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})\sim\langle\zeta^{4}\rangle_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}}}
.
Here, the angle brackets with the subscript
{}_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}}}
denote an ensemble average over a coarse-grained region centered at the initial position
\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}}
.
Such coarse graining is necessary because the scalar-induced gravitational waves of interest are emitted at an extremely high redshift (
z>10^{10}
), meaning the Hubble-horizon is very small at the initial moment.
Consequently, due to the finite angular resolution of gravitational wave detectors, any signal observed along a given line-of-sight originates from a superposition of scalar-induced gravitational waves emitted from numerous initial Hubble-horizon patches.
This implies that the scale of the averaged region is larger than the gravitational wave wavelength yet significantly smaller than cosmic microwave background scales.
Furthermore, the initial anisotropies vanish upon averaging, as necessitated by the cosmological principle and the assumption of a statistically isotropic Universe.
Given that
\langle\zeta^{4}\rangle_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}}}
encompasses the connected trispectrum of
\zeta
, the relation between
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})
and
\langle\zeta^{4}\rangle_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}}}
implies that
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})
is affected by primordial non-Gaussianity.

In order to analyze the impacts of primordial non-Gaussianity on
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})
,
we consider the primordial curvature perturbations
\zeta(\mathbf{x})
with small local-type primordial non-Gaussianity, which can generally be expanded around their Gaussian components
\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}
as
Komatsu and Spergel (2001)
;
Okamoto and Hu (2002)
;
Smidt et al. (2010)

\zeta(\mathbf{x})=\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{x})+\frac{3}{5}f_{\mathrm{NL}}\left(\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}(\mathbf{x})-\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}\rangle\right)+\frac{9}{25}g_{\mathrm{NL}}\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}^{3}(\mathbf{x})+\frac{27}{125}h_{\mathrm{NL}}\left(\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}^{4}(\mathbf{x})-3\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}\rangle^{2}\right)+\cdots\ .

(7)

For convenience, we recast the non-linear parameters as

F_{\mathrm{NL}}=\frac{3}{5}f_{\mathrm{NL}}\ ,\ G_{\mathrm{NL}}=\frac{9}{25}g_{\mathrm{NL}}\ ,\ H_{\mathrm{NL}}=\frac{27}{125}h_{\mathrm{NL}}\ ,

and rewrite the expression of Eq. (
7
) as follows

\zeta(\mathbf{x})=\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{x})+F_{\mathrm{NL}}\left(\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}(\mathbf{x})-\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}\rangle\right)+G_{\mathrm{NL}}\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}^{3}(\mathbf{x})+H_{\mathrm{NL}}\left(\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}^{4}(\mathbf{x})-3\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}\rangle^{2}\right)+\cdots\ .

(8)

Note that this form of expansion has ensured
\langle\zeta\rangle=0
.
Consequently, the statistics of
\zeta
can be expressed in terms of the two-point correlators of
\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}
.

We are intrigued by scalar-induced gravitational waves produced during the radiation-dominated era, assuming an enhancement of the primordial curvature power spectrum on the Hubble horizon scales at that epoch.
To study the statistics of
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
on the cosmic microwave background scale,
we can further decompose
\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}
into short-wavelength mode
\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}
and long-wavelength mode
\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}

Tada and Yokoyama (2015)
, i.e.,

\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}=\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}+\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}\ ,

(9)

where
\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}
denotes the enhanced curvature perturbations at the horizon scale, while
\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}
represents the mode extrapolated from the cosmic microwave background scale.
Further, the two-point correlators of
\zeta_{\mathrm{gX}}(\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{S},\mathrm{L})
define their respective dimensionless power spectra, namely,

\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gX}}(\mathbf{q})\zeta_{\mathrm{gX}}(\mathbf{q}^{\prime})\rangle=\delta^{(3)}(\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{q}^{\prime})\frac{2\pi^{2}}{q^{3}}\Delta^{2}_{\mathrm{X}}(q)\ .

(10)

For simplicity, we adopt a scale-invariant spectrum for
\Delta_{\mathrm{L}}^{2}

Aghanim and others (2020)
and a normal function with respect to
\ln q
for
\Delta^{2}_{\mathrm{S}}
, expressed as

\displaystyle\Delta^{2}_{\mathrm{L}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{\mathrm{L}}\simeq 2.1\times 10^{-9}\ ,

(11)

\displaystyle\Delta^{2}_{\mathrm{S}}(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{A_{\mathrm{S}}}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{\ln^{2}(q/q_{\ast})}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)\ ,

(12)

where
\sigma
denotes the spectral width and
A_{\mathrm{S}}
represents the spectral amplitude at the peak wavenumber
q_{\ast}
for
\Delta^{2}_{\mathrm{S}}(q)
.
In this study, we consider values of
A_{\mathrm{S}}
ranging from
10^{-4}
to
10^{-1}
, corresponding to scenarios of primordial black hole formation (see reviews in Refs.
Green and Kavanagh (2021)
;
Carr et al. (2021)
;
Escrivà et al. (2022a)
and references therein).
Furthermore, the primordial non-Gaussianity parameters in Eq. (
8
), proposed to be scale-independent for the sake of simplicity, are subject to perturbativity conditions. These conditions are specifically expressed as
|F_{\mathrm{NL}}|\sqrt{A_{\mathrm{S}}}+|G_{\mathrm{NL}}|A_{\mathrm{S}}+|H_{\mathrm{NL}}|\sqrt{A_{\mathrm{S}}^{3}}<1
and
|F_{\mathrm{NL}}|\sqrt{A_{\mathrm{S}}}>|G_{\mathrm{NL}}|A_{\mathrm{S}}>|H_{\mathrm{NL}}|\sqrt{A_{\mathrm{S}}^{3}}
.

By substituting Eqs. (
8
,
9
) into
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})\sim\langle\zeta^{4}\rangle_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}}}
, we can express
\langle\zeta^{4}\rangle_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}}}
as a polynomial in terms of
\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}^{m}\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}^{n}\rangle_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}}}
, where
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
m+n\leq 16
for primordial non-Gaussianity up to
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
order.
The coefficients of this polynomial consist of primordial non-Gaussianity parameters.
Since the wavelength of
\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}
is significantly larger than the scale of the averaged region, we can extract
\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}
from the average around
\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}}
when analyzing
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
alone.
In this process, we neglect the wavenumber of
\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}
, as it is insignificant compared to that of
\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}
.
Consequently, the sum of all momenta of
\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}
within any averages of this polynomial results in zero due to momentum conservation.
Notably, we will omit the subscript
{}_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}}}
from the averages associated with
\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}
, because its wavelength is comparable to the comoving Hubble horizon
\mathcal{H}^{-1}
and considerably smaller than the averaged region.
Owing to
A_{\mathrm{S}}\gg A_{\mathrm{L}}
, we can regard the terms involving
\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}
as corrections to those determined by
\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}
, which corresponds to the large-scale inhomogeneities superimposed on the homogeneous component of scalar-induced gravitational waves.
This will be delineated subsequently.
After extracting
\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}
and the primordial non-Gaussianity parameters from the correlators, we can approximately expand
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})\sim\langle\zeta^{4}\rangle_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}}}
up to third order in
\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}
in a schematic manner, as shown by

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})\sim\langle\zeta^{4}\rangle_{\mathbf{x}}

(13)

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}^{4}\rangle+\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}\bigl(\mathcal{O}(F_{\mathrm{NL}})\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}^{3}\rangle+\mathcal{O}(G_{\mathrm{NL}})\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}^{2}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}^{3}\rangle+\mathcal{O}(H_{\mathrm{NL}})\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}^{3}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}^{3}\rangle\bigr)

\displaystyle+\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}^{2}\bigl[\mathcal{O}(F_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2})\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}^{2}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}^{2}\rangle+\mathcal{O}(F_{\mathrm{NL}}G_{\mathrm{NL}})\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}^{3}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}^{2}\rangle+\bigl(\mathcal{O}(G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(F_{\mathrm{NL}}H_{\mathrm{NL}})\bigr)\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}^{4}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\hphantom{+ \zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}^2 \bigl[}+\mathcal{O}(G_{\mathrm{NL}}H_{\mathrm{NL}})\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}^{5}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}^{2}\rangle+\mathcal{O}(H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2})\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}^{6}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}^{2}\rangle+\mathcal{O}(G_{\mathrm{NL}})\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}^{3}\rangle+\mathcal{O}(H_{\mathrm{NL}})\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}^{2}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}^{3}\rangle\bigr]

\displaystyle+\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}^{3}\bigl[\mathcal{O}(F_{\mathrm{NL}}^{3})\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}^{3}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}\rangle+\mathcal{O}(F_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}G_{\mathrm{NL}})\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}^{4}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}\rangle+\bigl(\mathcal{O}(F_{\mathrm{NL}}G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(F_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}H_{\mathrm{NL}})\bigr)\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}^{5}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}\rangle

\displaystyle\hphantom{+ \zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}^3 \bigl[}+\bigl(\mathcal{O}(G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{3})+\mathcal{O}(F_{\mathrm{NL}}G_{\mathrm{NL}}H_{\mathrm{NL}})\bigr)\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}^{6}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}\rangle+\bigl(\mathcal{O}(G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}H_{\mathrm{NL}})+\mathcal{O}(F_{\mathrm{NL}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2})\bigr)\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}^{7}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}\rangle

\displaystyle\hphantom{+ \zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}^3 \bigl[}+\mathcal{O}(G_{\mathrm{NL}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2})\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}^{8}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}\rangle+\mathcal{O}(H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{3})\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}^{9}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}\rangle+\mathcal{O}(F_{\mathrm{NL}}G_{\mathrm{NL}})\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}^{2}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\hphantom{+ \zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}^3 \bigl[}+\bigl(\mathcal{O}(G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(F_{\mathrm{NL}}H_{\mathrm{NL}})\bigr)\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}^{3}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}^{2}\rangle+\mathcal{O}(G_{\mathrm{NL}}H_{\mathrm{NL}})\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}^{4}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\hphantom{+ \zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}^3 \bigl[}+\mathcal{O}(H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2})\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}^{5}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}^{2}\rangle+\mathcal{O}(H_{\mathrm{NL}})\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}^{3}\rangle\bigr]\ ,

where
\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}
denotes the short-wavelength modes of
\zeta
, encompassing both Gaussian and non-Gaussian components.
Explicitly, the relationship between
\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}
and its Gaussian component
\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}
is identical to that between
\zeta
and
\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}
as outlined in Eq. (
8
).
By utilizing Wick’s theorem in conjunction with Eqs. (
10
,
12
), all multi-point correlators within this polynomial can be contracted and expressed in terms of
\Delta^{2}_{\mathrm{S}}
, which remains independent of the position
\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}}
and the propagation direction
\mathbf{n}
.
Furthermore, by combining Eqs. (
4
,
5
,
6
), we can observe that the spatial and directional dependencies of
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})
are dictated solely by
\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}
.
Thus, the leading-order term in Eq. (
13
) corresponds to the homogeneous component of
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})
, while the remaining terms characterize the 1st-, 2nd-, and 3rd-order primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneities, respectively.
We decompose
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})
into its homogeneous component and primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneities of varying orders in
\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}
, as given by

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)+\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}(\mathbf{q})+\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(2)}(\mathbf{q})+\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}(\mathbf{q})+\cdots\ ,

(14a)

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}(q)\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{k})\ ,

(14b)

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2)}(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(2)}(q)\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{p})\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{p})\ ,

(14c)

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}(q)\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{p}\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{l}}{(2\pi)^{9/2}}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{l})\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{l})\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{p})\ ,

(14d)

where
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)=\langle\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})\rangle
denotes the average energy-density spectrum, while
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}(\mathbf{q})
,
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2)}(\mathbf{q})
, and
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}(\mathbf{q})
represent the 1st-, 2nd-, and 3rd-order primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneities of the scalar-induced gravitational wave energy density, respectively.
Also,
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}(q)
,
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(2)}(q)
, and
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}(q)
correspond to their respective large-scale modulation functions.
Since these large-scale modulation functions are governed by the multi-point correlators of
\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}
, Eqs. (
14b
-
14d
) indicate that the primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneities of scalar-induced gravitational waves at the time of emission can be attributed to the coupling between short- and long-wavelength modes of curvature perturbations.

The expression for
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)
can be derived by calculating the spatial average of Eq. (
5
), which is formulated in terms of the power spectrum, as given by
Inomata et al. (2017)

\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)=\frac{q^{5}}{48\times(2\pi)^{3}\mathcal{H}^{2}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muP_{h}(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},q)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\ ,

(15)

where
P_{h}=\sum_{\lambda=+,\times}P_{h_{\lambda}}
represents the total power spectrum of scalar-induced gravitational waves.
Using Eq. (
6
), we can express
P_{h_{\lambda}}
explicitly in terms of the four-point correlator of
\zeta_{S}
.
As for the large-scale inhoomogeneities, we introduce the density contrast
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})
to better characterize them, as given by

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)\bigl(1+\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})\bigr)\ ,

(16)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}(\mathbf{q})+\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2)}(\mathbf{q})+\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}(\mathbf{q})+\cdots\ ,

(17)

where
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}=\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}/\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}\propto\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}
,
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2)}=\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2)}/\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}\propto\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}^{2}
, and
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}=\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}/\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}\propto\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}^{3}
represent the density contrast of different orders in
\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}
.
The equations presented, namely Eqs. (
13
-
17
), indicate that the short-wavelength mode is responsible for the production of scalar-induced gravitational waves, while the long-wavelength mode modulates the distribution of their energy density on large scales.
Consequently, we can utilize
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
and the correlations of
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
to describe the average and other statistical properties of scalar-induced gravitational waves at their production moment.
Their explicit expressions will be explored in the following sections.

2.2
Present-day energy density of SIGWs

In this subsection, we aim to investigate the transfer of energy density of scalar-induced gravitational waves from the time of emission to the present observation.
Similar to the average energy-density spectrum
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)
and the density contrast
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})
defined at the initial moment
\eta_{\mathrm{in}}
in Eqs. (
14
,
16
), we extend these definitions to any given moment
\eta
as follows

\omega_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})=\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,q)\bigl(1+\delta_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})\bigr)\ .

(18)

Here,
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)
characterizes the homogeneous and isotropic background, while
\delta_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})
measures the fluctuations on this background.
This relationship implies that
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,q)=\langle\omega_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})\rangle
and that the average of the density contrast satisfies
\langle\delta_{\mathrm{gw}}\rangle=0
.
Moreover, the homogeneous and isotropic background is typically described by the energy-density fraction spectrum
\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,q)
.
This spectrum is defined in terms of the spatially-averaged energy density
\bar{\rho}_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta)
.
It is expressed as
\bar{\rho}_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta)=\rho_{\mathrm{c}}(\eta)\int\mathrm{d}\ln q\,\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,q)
, where the overbar signifies spatially-averaged physical quantities.
By comparing this with definition with Eq. (
1
), we have

\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,q)=4\pi\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,q)\ .

(19)

It is essential to investigate the relationship between the present-day energy-density fraction spectrum, denoted as
\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\nu)=\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta_{0},2\pi\nu)
, and the average energy-density spectrum at the time of emission,
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)
.
Here, the subscript
0
denotes the current epoch of observation.
The present-day scalar-induced gravitational wave frequency
\nu
is determined by the comoving momentum
q
(specifically,
\nu=q/(2\pi)
), as
q
is conserved during propagation at the background level—a result derived in Appendix
F
.
Regarding the amplitude, we focus on scalar-induced gravitational waves produced during the radiation-dominated era, as discussed previously.
Accounting for the dilution of radiation components and the evolution of the effective number of relativistic degrees of freedom in the Universe, we combine Eq. (
19
) to yield the expression for
\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\nu)

Wang et al. (2019)

\displaystyle h^{2}\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\nu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\pi h^{2}\Omega_{\mathrm{rad},0}\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)\left(\frac{g_{*,\rho}(T_{\mathrm{in}})}{g_{*,\rho}(T_{0})}\right)\left(\frac{g_{*,s}(T_{0})}{g_{*,s}(T_{\mathrm{in}})}\right)^{4/3}\ ,

(20)

where the relation between the initial temperature
T_{\mathrm{in}}
and the scalar-induced gravitational wave frequency
\nu
is approximately given by
Wang et al. (2019)
;
Zhao and Wang (2023)

\displaystyle\frac{\nu}{\mathrm{nHz}}=26.5\left(\frac{T_{\mathrm{in}}}{\mathrm{GeV}}\right)\left(\frac{g_{*,\rho}(T_{\mathrm{in}})}{106.75}\right)^{1/2}\left(\frac{g_{*,s}(T_{\mathrm{in}})}{106.75}\right)^{-1/3}\ .

(21)

Here,
h^{2}\Omega_{\mathrm{rad},0}=4.2\times 10^{-5}
represents the energy-density fraction of radiation in the present Universe, and
h
denotes the dimensionless Hubble constant
Aghanim and others (2020)
.

g_{*,\rho}
and
g_{*,s}
characterize the effective numbers of relativistic degrees of freedom, tabulated as functions of the cosmic temperature
T
(see Ref.
Saikawa and Shirai (2018)
for concrete tables).

The present-day density contrast, denoted as
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\mathbf{q})=\delta_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta_{0},\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{q})
, can be derived by solving the Boltzmann equation for gravitons
Contaldi (2017)
;
Bartolo et al. (2019)
;
Bartolo et al. (2020b)
;
Schulze et al. (2023)
.
The solution includes both the initial inhomogeneities and propagation effects, as given by

\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\mathbf{q})=\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})+\left(6-n_{\mathrm{gw}}(\nu)\right)\Phi(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})\ ,

(22)

where
\Phi(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})
denotes the Bardeen potential at the time of scalar-induced gravitational waves production, while
n_{\mathrm{gw}}
represents the spectral index of
\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}
.
A brief review of the derivation process of Eq. (
22
) is provided in Appendix
F
.
Notably, in our analysis of propagation effects, we focus exclusively on the Sachs-Wolfe effect, since it is the predominant phenomenon among all propagation effects.
The formula presented here differs slightly from the results reported in Refs.
Bartolo et al. (2020a)
;
Li et al. (2023a)
;
Li et al. (2024b)
;
Wang et al. (2024a)
.
This discrepancy arises from our inclusion of initial inhomogeneity induced by large-scale scalar perturbations, which is referred to as inflationary initial conditions in Refs.
Mierna et al. (2024)
;
Valbusa Dall’Armi et al. (2023)
;
Valbusa Dall’Armi et al. (2024)
.
This is regarded as a general feature of cosmological gravitational waves produced via non-thermal and non-adiabatic mechanisms.

1
1

1

When accounting for gravitational wave energy density modified by large-scale scalar perturbations
Mierna et al. (2024)
;
Valbusa Dall’Armi et al. (2023)
;
Valbusa Dall’Armi et al. (2024)
, the initial energy density of scalar-induced gravitational waves is modified as given in Appendix
F
, i.e.,

\tilde{\rho}_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})=\frac{m_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}(1+2\Phi_{\mathrm{L}}(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}}))}{16a^{2}(\eta_{\mathrm{in}})}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\partial_{l}h_{ij}(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})\partial_{l}h_{ij}(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\ ,

(23)

where
\Phi_{\mathrm{L}}(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})
denotes the large-scale scalar perturbations.
Despite this modification, we continue to use Eq. (
2
) for the calculation of
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})
and
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})
for convenience.
To indicate the inclusion of this modification, we place a tilde above these quantities.

For the first terms in Eq. (
22
), we derive the initial density contrast
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})
based on the framework outlined in Subsection
2.1
, deferring the detailed derivation to later sections.
The second term includes the Sachs-Wolfe effect, expressed as
\left(4-n_{\mathrm{gw}}(\nu)\right)\Phi(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})
, along with the inhomogeneity arising from initial inflationary condition
Mierna et al. (2024)
;
Valbusa Dall’Armi et al. (2023)
;
Valbusa Dall’Armi et al. (2024)
, given by
2\Phi(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})
.
When calculating the correlators of
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}
, we only take into account long-wavelength scalar perturbations that re-entered the Hubble horizon during matter domination, owing to the angular resolution limitations of current and upcoming gravitational wave detectors.
Therefore, the long-wavelength Bardeen potential at the production time,
\Phi(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})
, up to third order in
\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}
, is given by

\displaystyle\Phi(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})=\frac{3}{5}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}

\displaystyle\biggl(

\displaystyle\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{k})+F_{\mathrm{NL}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{p})\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{p})

(24)

\displaystyle+G_{\mathrm{NL}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{p}\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{l}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{l})\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{l})\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{p})+\cdots\biggr)\ .

Moreover, the spectral index of
\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}
is defined as

n_{\mathrm{gw}}(\nu)=\frac{\partial\ln\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\nu)}{\partial\ln\nu}\simeq\frac{\partial\ln\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)}{\partial\ln q}\Big|_{q=2\pi\nu}\ .

(25)

In this analysis, we neglect the time evolution of
n_{\mathrm{gw}}
during propagation, as the influence of the numbers of relativistic species on the frequency remains constant in Eq. (
20
) for the milli-Hertz band under consideration.
These two equations, Eqs. (
24
,
25
), indicate that the Sachs-Wolfe effect can be expressed as a series expansion in terms of
\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}
, with the leading term being of
\mathcal{O}(\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}})
order.
Given that the initial density contrast
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})
is similarly expressed in Eq. (
17
), we can represent the present-day density contrast
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\mathbf{q})
as a power-series expansion in
\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}
, namely

\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\mathbf{q})=\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}^{(1)}(\mathbf{q})+\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}^{(2)}(\mathbf{q})+\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}^{(3)}(\mathbf{q})+\cdots\ ,

(26)

where we introduce three quantities of the form

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}^{(1)}(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\biggl[\frac{\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}(q)}{\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)}+\frac{3}{5}\bigl(6-n_{\mathrm{gw}}(\nu)\bigr)\biggr]\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{k})\ ,

(27)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}^{(2)}(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\biggl[\frac{\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(2)}(q)}{\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)}+\frac{3}{5}F_{\mathrm{NL}}\bigl(6-n_{\mathrm{gw}}(\nu)\bigr)\biggr]\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{p})\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{p})\ ,

(28)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}^{(3)}(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\biggl[\frac{\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}(q)}{\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)}+\frac{9}{25}G_{\mathrm{NL}}\bigl(6-n_{\mathrm{gw}}(\nu)\bigr)\biggr]\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{p}\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{l}}{(2\pi)^{9/2}}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{l})\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{l})\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{p})\ .

Consequently, the angular correlation functions of
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\mathbf{q})
, which encapsulate the statistical information regarding anisotropies in scalar-induced gravitational waves, can be expressed in terms of the correlators of
\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}
.
Furthermore, we offer a supplementary explanation regarding other propagation effects.
The integrated Sachs-Wolfe effect is relatively less significant than the Sachs-Wolfe effect for
\ell\lesssim 10^{2}
, as demonstrated in
Bartolo et al. (2020a)
;
Valbusa Dall’Armi et al. (2021)
.
The gravitational lensing effect is of higher order compared to the Sachs-Wolfe effect
Bartolo et al. (2019)
;
Bartolo et al. (2020b)
and is anticipated to primarily influence
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}
at higher multipoles
Dodelson (2003)
.
If necessary, our analysis can be readily extended to incorporate all these effects.

As a result, we are able to characterize the isotropy of scalar-induced gravitational wave background using
\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}
, while the statistics of the anisotropies in the scalar-induced gravitational wave background are described through the angular correlations of
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}
.
All of these quantities can be expressed in terms of the multi-point correlators of
\zeta
.
Although the physics underlying the production and propagation of scalar-induced gravitational waves has been clarified, a significant challenge remains in calculating the multi-point correlators of
\zeta
for non-Gaussian cases.
Addressing this issue is essential for establishing a association between the scalar-induced gravitational wave background and inflationary physics.
Our proposed solution to this problem will be presented in the following subsection.

2.3
Feynman-like diagrammatic approach

Figure 1
:
Feynman-like rules for evaluation of Eq. (
39
) by incorporating Eq. (
8
). Wavy lines represent GWs, double wavy lines denote
\omega_{\mathrm{gw}}
, dashed lines denote the transfer functions, and (red/blue) solid lines stand for the (long-/short-wavelength) primordial curvature power spectra. Arrows indicate the flow of comoving 3-momenta, which are conserved at each vertex. The total 3-momentum vanishes for each individual diagram, and all the loop 3-momenta should be integrated over.

As elaborated in the previous two subsections, the present-day energy-density fraction spectrum
\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}
of scalar-induced gravitational waves is expressed in terms of
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
in Eq. (
20
), while the present-day density contrast
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}(q)
for scalar-induced gravitational waves is formulated in terms of
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}(q)
,
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(2)}(q)
, and
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}(q)
in Eqs. (
26
-
).
To derive their explicit expressions, the principal challenge lies in evaluating the numerous multi-point correlators of
\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}
, as presented in Eqs. (
13
,
14
).
In the context of local-type primordial non-Gaussianity introduced in Eq. (
8
) up to quartic approximation (i.e.,
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
order), the calculation of
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
necessitates addressing up to 16-point correlators of
\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}
.
Furthermore, the calculation of the angular two-, three-, and four-point correlators of
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
requires up to 14-point correlators of
\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}
as well as 9-point correlators of
\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}
.
Although Wick’s theorem can be applied to facilitate the contractions of these correlators, the large number of two-point correlators involved presents a significant challenge.

Fortunately, the diagrammatic approach has proven to be a helpful tool for addressing this challenge in previous works
Unal (2019)
;
Atal and Domènech (2021)
;
Adshead et al. (2021b)
;
Ragavendra (2022)
;
Abe et al. (2023)
;
Li et al. (2023a)
;
Li et al. (2024b)
;
Perna et al. (2024)
;
Ruiz and Rey (2025)
.
This approach is a classical method for handling multi-point correlation functions of non-Gaussian fields.
Its application to primordial curvature perturbations was initiated by Ref.
Byrnes et al. (2007)
.
Subsequent works, Refs.
Unal (2019)
;
Atal and Domènech (2021)
, began applying it to compute the scalar-induced gravitational wave energy density with primordial non-Gaussianity.
A milestone was Ref.
Adshead et al. (2021b)
, which first established a complete set of Feynman-like rules for calculating the scalar-induced gravitational wave fractional energy spectrum at
f_{\mathrm{NL}}
order.
This framework was subsequently applied by Ref.
Ragavendra (2022)
to specific inflationary models, while Ref.
Li et al. (2024b)
extended the diagrammatic approach to compute the scalar-induced gravitational wave energy density fraction in the presence of
g_{\mathrm{NL}}
-order primordial non-Gaussianity.
Further developments were made in Refs.
Abe et al. (2023)
;
Perna et al. (2024)
, where the diagrammatic approaches were employed to systematically investigate contributions to the scalar-induced gravitational wave energy density fraction up to
\mathcal{O}(A_{S}^{4})
for polynomially expanded primordial non-Gaussianity.
Recently, Ref.
Ruiz and Rey (2025)
adapted these Feynman-like rules to address the renormalization of the one-loop scalar power spectrum in ultra-slow-roll inflation models
Ballesteros and Gambín Egea (2024)
.
A comparison of the number of diagrams involved in these various works is presented in Appendix
G
.
Further, our earlier works
Li et al. (2023a)
;
Li et al. (2024b)
;
Li et al. (2024a)
advanced the diagrammatic approach by extending it to study inhomogeneities induced by primordial non-Gaussianity, enabling the treatment of angular correlations in
\omega_{\mathrm{GW},\mathrm{in}}
.
This extension was conceptually inspired by Ref.
Bartolo et al. (2020a)
, which used Feynman-like diagrams schematically to illustrate how primordial non-Gaussianity may induce angular anisotropies of the scalar-induced gravitational wave background.

Similar to these works, we introduce appropriate Feynman-like rules (see
Fig.

1
), which then enable the diagrammatic representation of
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
across various contraction terms.
In this set of Feynman-like rules, wavy lines denote the gravitational wave strain, while double wavy lines represent
\omega_{\mathrm{gw}}
.
Dashed lines indicate the transfer functions, and (red/blue) solid lines stand for the propagators of (long-/short-wavelength) primordial curvature perturbations, corresponding to their power spectra.
Arrows adjacent to these lines signify the flow of comoving 3-momenta, which are conserved at each vertex.
It is important to note that, as discussed in Subsection
2.1
, we will disregard the 3-momenta contributed by the red lines when calculating the sum of all momenta at any vertex, since they are significantly smaller than those contributed by the blue lines.
The sum of all momenta of
\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}
at any vertex results in zero due to momentum conservation.
Furthermore, the total 3-momentum for each individual diagram vanishes, and all loop 3-momenta should be integrated over.
In particular, while vertices with identical solid-line counts but distinct red-line counts are treated as distinct entities, we assign them the same primordial non-Gaussianity parameters.
This treatment is consistent, as our present analysis is confined to the scale-independent primordial non-Gaussianity scenario.
Specifically, according to
Fig.

1
, while the Gaussian-vertex means
V_{0}^{[1]}=1
by definition, we introduce the
F_{\mathrm{NL}}
-vertices with
V_{0}^{[2]}=V_{1}^{[2]}=F_{\mathrm{NL}}
, the
G_{\mathrm{NL}}
-vertices with
V_{0}^{[3]}=V_{1}^{[3]}=V_{2}^{[3]}=G_{\mathrm{NL}}
, and the
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
-vertices with
V_{0}^{[4]}=V_{1}^{[4]}=V_{2}^{[4]}=V_{3}^{[4]}=H_{\mathrm{NL}}
.
Utilizing these Feynman-like rules, we can draw diagrams that represent
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
and its multi-point correlators.
The various configurations of solid lines correspond to different contraction terms of the correlators of
\zeta
.
This visualization highlights the symmetry among certain contraction terms, which significantly simplifies the computation of numerous contraction terms performed directly using Wick’s theorem.

Figure 2
:
Common structure of the diagrams for the SIGW average energy-density spectrum
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
, where the shaded square should be replaced with one of the panels from
Fig.

26
-
Fig.

34
for a specific diagram.

Figure 3
:

Primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneities of
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})
up to third order in
\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}
, where the shaded squares should be replaced with one of the panels from
Fig.

26
to
Fig.

34
for a specific diagram according to
Table

1
. From left to right and top to bottom, the six panels represent
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}(\mathbf{q})
,
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},1}^{(2)}(\mathbf{q})
,
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},2}^{(2)}(\mathbf{q})
,
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},1}^{(3)}(\mathbf{q})
,
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},2}^{(3)}(\mathbf{q})
, and
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},3}^{(3)}(\mathbf{q})
, respectively.

Utilizing the diagrammatic rules in
Fig.

1
, we construct the common structure of the diagrams for
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
in
Fig.

2
for conciseness, where the shaded square covers the various configurations of the blue solid lines introduced in
Fig.

1
.
The shaded square should be replaced with panels from
Fig.

26
to
Fig.

34
in Appendix
C
, whereas the detailed calculation process for all diagrams pertaining to
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
is presented in Section
3
.
We further present the diagrams for the primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneities of
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})
on large-scales, as shown in
Fig.

3
.
These six diagrams correspond to attaching extensional red solid lines to the diagrams of
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
in
Fig.

2
.
The upper left panel depicts the diagram of the 1st-order primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneity
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}
in
\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}
, as defined in Eq. (
14b
).
The other two upper panels, labeled
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},1}^{(2)}(\mathbf{q})
and
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},2}^{(2)}(\mathbf{q})
from left to right, represent the diagrams of the two components of the 2nd-order primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneity
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2)}
in
\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}
, as defined in Eq. (
14c
).
The three lower panels, labeled
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},1}^{(3)}(\mathbf{q})
,
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},2}^{(3)}(\mathbf{q})
, and
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},3}^{(3)}(\mathbf{q})
from left to right, illustrate the diagrams of the three components of the 3rd-order primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneity
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}
in
\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}
, as defined in Eq. (
14d
).
In other words, the 2nd- and 3rd-order primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneities are the sums of all related components, expressed as

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2)}(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},1}^{(2)}(\mathbf{q})+\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},2}^{(2)}(\mathbf{q})\ ,

(30)

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},1}^{(3)}(\mathbf{q})+\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},2}^{(3)}(\mathbf{q})+\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},3}^{(3)}(\mathbf{q})\ .

(31)

It is important to note that, although the extensional red solid lines are attached to vertices on the same side in the diagrams for
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},2}^{(2)}
and
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},2}^{(3)}
in
Fig.

3
, this is not a necessary requirement.
Any two vertices can be utilized as long as, after attaching the red solid lines, the total number of solid lines attached to these vertices does not exceed four.
Similar to
Fig.

2
, the shaded squares represent the configurations of blue solid lines, which correspond to the correlators of
\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}
in Eq. (
13
).
These shaded squares should be replaced with specific squares (or equivalent ones) after a
90^{\circ}
rotation according to the Gaussian-vertex count,
F_{\mathrm{NL}}
-vertex count,
G_{\mathrm{NL}}
-vertex count, and
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
-vertex count in each panel from
Fig.

26
to
Fig.

34
in Appendix
C
.
Specifically, for any panel from
Fig.

26
to
Fig.

34
, a Gaussian-vertex can attach up to three red solid lines, a
F_{\mathrm{NL}}
-vertex can have two, a
G_{\mathrm{NL}}
-vertex can have one, and a
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
-vertex cannot attach any red solid lines.
Furthermore, we provide a table that indicates whether a certain square from
Fig.

26
to
Fig.

34
can be used to replace these shaded squares.

Calculating the primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneities is quite tedious if we replace the squares individually.
However, based on the results for
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
, we can derive explicit expressions for
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}
,
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(2)}
, and
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}
in a simpler manner.
We leverage the fact that the respective numbers of vertices in a specific diagram
D
correspond to the powers of the primordial non-Gaussianity parameters to which
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{D}
is proportional.
For convenience, we classify
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
into 19 distinct categories based on the powers of the three primordial non-Gaussianity parameters.
Each category, denoted as
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(a,b,c)}
, represents the component proportional to
F_{\mathrm{NL}}^{a}G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{b}H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{c}A^{(a+2b+3c+4)/2}
, where
a
,
b
, and
c
are natural numbers.
Specifically, the classification can be expressed as

\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)=\sum_{(a,b,c)}\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(a,b,c)}(q)\ ,

(32)

where the superscripts
a
,
b
, and
c
satisfy the conditions
a+b+c\leq 4
and
a,b,c,(a+c)/2\in\mathbb{N}
.
The requirement that
(a+c)
is even reflects a sign degeneracy between
F_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
.
We can then ascertain the numbers of each vertex type: the number of
F_{\mathrm{NL}}
-vertices (denoted as
V_{0}^{[2]}
) is
N_{2}=a
, the number of
G_{\mathrm{NL}}
-vertices (denoted as
V_{0}^{[3]}
) is
N_{3}=b
, and the number of
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
-vertices (denoted as
V_{0}^{[4]}
) is
N_{4}=c
.
The relation
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{GW},\mathrm{in}}\sim\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}^{4}\rangle
indicates that a total of four vertices are involved in the propagators of
\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}
, yielding the Gaussian-vertex count
N_{\mathrm{1}}=4-a-b-c
.
With these counts determined, we can assign the corresponding categories to the six replacement squares (shaded) in
Fig.

3
.
For the shaded squares in the diagram of
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}
, the replacement squares must belong to categories where
c\leq 3
.
Similarly, for
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},1}^{(2)}
, it requires
c\leq 2
, and for
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},1}^{(3)}
,
c\leq 1
.
In the case of
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},2}^{(2)}
, the replacement squares should belong to categories where
b+c\leq 3
.
The conditions for the categories that can replace the shaded squares of
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},2}^{(3)}
are
b+c\leq 3
and
c\leq 2
.
It is necessary for the replacement squares related to
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},3}^{(3)}
to contain at least one Gaussian-vertex, associated with categories where
a+b+c\leq 3
.
The specific results for the replacements across all categories are also provided in
Table

1
.
The categories contributing to the large-scale modulations
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}
,
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(2)}
, and
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}
, along with the coefficients for these categories, are discussed in detail in Section
4
and Section
5
.

3
Isotropic component of the SIGW background

In this section, we will provide a detailed derivation of the scalar-induced gravitational wave energy-density fraction spectrum for primordial non-Gaussianity up to all orders.
In particular, we will present the theoretical results for primordial non-Gaussianity up to
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
order and elaborate on the associated numerical findings.

3.1
“Renormalized” Feynman-like rules

In the previous section, we have discussed the calculation of the average energy-density spectrum
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)
using a diagrammatic approach based on the Feynman-like rules illustrated in
Fig.

1
.
Previous studies
Adshead et al. (2021b)
;
Li et al. (2023a)
indicate that for primordial non-Gaussianity up to quadratic approximation in
\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}
(
F_{\mathrm{NL}}
order), a total of 7 diagrams are required to capture all contractions in the calculation of
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)
.
For primordial non-Gaussianity up to cubic approximation (or
G_{\mathrm{NL}}
order), 49 diagrams are necessary
Li et al. (2024b)
.
However, deriving
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)
for primordial non-Gaussianity up to quartic approximation (or
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
order) necessitates considering 236 diagrams, as shown in
Fig.

26
-
Fig.

34
in Appendix
C
.
Should this study be extended to include higher orders of primordial non-Gaussianity, a substantially larger number of diagrams will be required, corresponding to a significantly greater workload.
Therefore, it is crucial to investigate the patterns among the diagrams in
Fig.

26
-
Fig.

34
in Appendix
C
to alleviate this workload.

By comparing these diagrams, we find that among the hundreds of diagrams, they can be classified into nine distinct topologically independent families, which we denote as “
G
-like”, “
C
-like”, “
Z
-like”, “
P
-like”, “
N
-like”, “
CZ
-like”, “
PZ
-like”, “
NC
-like”, and “
PN
-like”, i.e.,

\displaystyle\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)=\sum_{X}\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{X-\mathrm{like}}(q)\ ,

(33)

where
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{X-\mathrm{like}}(q)
denotes the contributions from each family to the average energy-density spectrum, with
X=G,C,Z,P,N,CZ,PZ,NC
, and
PN
.
In the Feynman-like diagrams of the same family, the vertices are connected in similar ways, with differences lying in the loop structures at the vertices or the propagators of
\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}
.
Leveraging this similarity, we propose the “renormalized” Feynman-like rules, which incorporate loop structures into the convolved propagators and the “renormalized” vertices, as illustrated in
Fig.

4
.
The convolved propagator, represented as a thick-dashed line with a Greek letter
\xi
(or
\mu
,
\nu
) adjacent, consists of
\xi
(or
\mu
,
\nu
) solid lines, where the Greek letter denotes a positive integer.
The “renormalized” vertices attached by these thick-dashed lines represent the sum of the vertices that encompass all possible loop structures at both the propagators and the vertices themselves.
Additionally, to represent the four-point correlator
\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{1})\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}(\mathbf{q}_{1})\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{2})\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}(\mathbf{q}_{2})\rangle
, any “renormalized” vertex can be attached by up to three thick-dashed lines.
Hence, there are three distinct types of “renormalized” vertices classified by the number of attached thick-dashed lines, as illustrated in
Fig.

4
.
Introducing these “renormalized” Feynman-like rules allows us to perform the integration over loop momenta in advance, thereby reducing the dimensions of the multiple integrals and simplifying the calculation of symmetric factors.
We will provide detailed explanations regarding these convolved propagators and “renormalized” vertices in the subsequent paragraphs.

Figure 4
:
“Renormalized” Feynman-like rules.

The convolved propagator composed of
\xi
propagators of
\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}
is referred to as a
(\xi-1)
-fold convolved propagator, where
\xi
is a positive integer.
The
(\xi-1)
loops in this convolved propagator correspond to the
(\xi-1)
-fold convolutions of the power spectrum of
\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}
.
To maintain consistency, we introduce
P^{[\xi]}(q)
for
\xi\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
as follows

\displaystyle P^{[1]}(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi^{2}}{q^{3}}\Delta^{2}_{\mathrm{S}}(q)\ ,

(34)

\displaystyle P^{[\xi]}(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\tilde{\mathbf{q}}}{(2\pi)^{3}}P^{[\xi-1]}(\tilde{q})P^{[1]}({\left|\mathbf{q}-\tilde{\mathbf{q}}\right|})\ ,\ \text{where }\xi\geq 2,\xi\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\ .

(35)

As illustrated in
Fig.

4
, the symmetric factor contributed by a
(\xi-1)
-fold convolved propagator is obtained by counting all possible connections of the blue solid lines, which is precisely the permutation of
\xi
.
By multiplying this symmetric factor by
P^{[\xi]}
, we can express the
(\xi-1)
-fold convolved propagator as follows

P_{\mathrm{R},a}^{[\xi]}=\xi!P_{a}^{[\xi]}\ ,

(36)

where the subscript
a
denotes the momentum.
This convention will facilitate the subsequent calculations involving the symmetric factor, in which we will employ identical subscripts to represent the momenta of the symmetric convolved propagators.
Notably, in this context, the symmetry between two convolved propagators within a diagram indicates that an equivalent diagram can be obtained by interchanging these two convolved propagators.

We direct our focus to the “renormalized” vertices, each of which can be decomposed into vertices characterized by different numbers of “self-closed loops”.
For scale-independent primordial non-Gaussianity, the loop integral over the momentum of any involved “self-closed loop” of
\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}
yields

\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\tilde{\mathbf{q}}}{(2\pi)^{3}}P^{[1]}(q)=A_{\mathrm{S}}\ .

(37)

Moreover,
i
“self-closed loops” at the vertex also transform the primordial non-Gaussianity parameter into a
2i
-order higher one and correspondingly alter the symmetric factors.
For example, when evaluating the symmetric factor contributed by the vertex
V_{0}^{[2i+\xi]}
that includes
i
“self-closed loops”, the permutation
(2i)!
arising from the loops must be divided by the overcount
2^{i}i!
.
The overcount of
2^{i}
accounts for coincident endpoints of the
i
“self-closed loops”, while the overcount of
i!
arises from the equivalence among all “self-closed loops”.
Furthermore, if a
(\xi-1)
-fold convolved propagator connects this vertex to another vertex, such as those contributing to the “renormalized” vertex
V_{\mathrm{R},1}^{[\xi]}
in
Fig.

4
, then the selection of
\xi
blue solid lines attached to this vertex from a total of
(2i+\xi)
also contributes to the overall symmetric factor.
Similarly, for the vertex
V_{0}^{[2i+\xi+\mu]}
that contributes to the “renormalized” vertex
V_{\mathrm{R},2}^{[\xi,\mu]}
, a
(\xi-1)
-fold convolved propagator connects this vertex to another vertex, a
(\mu-1)
-fold convolved propagator connects it to yet another vertex, and
2i
solid lines form
i
“self-closed loops”.
The symmetric factor can be obtained by multiplying the symmetric factor of vertex
V_{0}^{[2i+\xi]}
by the number of ways to select
\mu
from a total of
(2i+\xi+\mu)
.
The symmetric factor of vertex
V_{0}^{[2i+\xi+\mu+\nu]}
that contributes to the “renormalized” vertex
V_{\mathrm{R},3}^{[\xi,\mu,\nu]}
, follows a similar pattern and can be obtained by multiplying the symmetric factor of the aforementioned vertex
V_{0}^{[2i+\xi+\mu]}
by the number of ways to select
\nu
from a total of
(2i+\xi+\mu+\nu)
.
Thus, for the four-point correlator
\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{1})\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}(\mathbf{q}_{1})\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{2})\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}(\mathbf{q}_{2})\rangle
, the relevant “renormalized” vertices for primordial non-Gaussianity up to
o
-th order approximation is defined as follows

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{R},1}^{[\xi]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor(o-\xi)/2\rfloor}\frac{(2i)!}{2^{i}i!}\binom{2i+\xi}{\xi}A_{\mathrm{S}}^{i}V_{0}^{[2i+\xi]}\ ,

(38a)

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{R},2}^{[\xi,\mu]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor(o-\xi-\mu)/2\rfloor}\frac{(2i)!}{2^{i}i!}\binom{2i+\xi+\mu}{\mu}\binom{2i+\xi}{\xi}A_{\mathrm{S}}^{i}V_{0}^{[2i+\xi+\mu]}\ ,

(38b)

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{R},3}^{[\xi,\mu,\nu]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor(o-\xi-\mu-\nu)/2\rfloor}\frac{(2i)!}{2^{i}i!}\binom{2i+\xi+\mu+\nu}{\nu}\binom{2i+\xi+\mu}{\mu}\binom{2i+\xi}{\xi}A_{\mathrm{S}}^{i}V_{0}^{[2i+\xi+\mu+\nu]}\ ,

(38c)

where the symbols
\lfloor\cdots\rfloor
represent the floor function, and the parentheses containing two numbers indicate combinations.

We utilize the “renormalized” Feynman-like rules depicted in
Fig.

4
to reconstruct the diagrams corresponding to
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
.
Specifically, we replace the solid lines with thick-dashed lines in the diagrams labeled as “
G
”, “
C
”, “
Z
”, “
P
”, “
N
”, “
CZ
”, “
PZ
”, “
NC
”, and “
PN
” in
Fig.

26
-
Fig.

34
in Appendix
C
, and subsequently substitute these into the shaded squares in
Fig.

2
.
Through this method, we obtain nine “renormalized” diagrams in
Fig.

5
, each encompassing all diagrams within the corresponding family.
By employing the Feynman-like rules presented in
Fig.

1
alongside the “renormalized” rules in
Fig.

4
, in conjunction with Eqs. (
36
,
38
), we can compute the contributions from each family to the average energy-density spectrum based on the respective “renormalized” diagram.
By aggregating their individual contributions as expressed in Eq. (
33
), we can determine the total average energy-density spectrum
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)
.
The detailed calculation process is outlined in the following subsection.

G
-like.

*

C
-like.

*

Z
-like.

*

P
-like.

*

N
-like.

*

CZ
-like.

*
