Figure 5
:
“Renormalized” Feynman-like diagrams for the nine families of
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)
.

3.2
Energy-density fraction spectrum

Based on the common structure of the diagrams representing the scalar-induced gravitational wave average energy-density spectrum
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
, as illustrated in
Fig.

2
, and the Feynman-like rules detailed in
Fig.

1
, we combine Eqs. (
4
,
6
,
15
) to express the contribution to
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
from a certain family designated as
X
-like in the form of

\displaystyle\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{X-\mathrm{like}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{q^{5}}{3(2\pi)^{3}\mathcal{H}^{2}}\Bigg\{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{q}_{i}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\Bigg\}\mathcal{P}^{X-\mathrm{like}}

(39)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\frac{q^{5}}{3(2\pi)^{3} \mathcal{H}^{2}}
\Bigg\lx@text@lbrace\prod_{i=1}^n }\times\Bigg\{\vskip-5.0pt\sum_{\lambda=+,\times}Q_{\lambda}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{q}_{1})Q_{\lambda}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{q}_{j})\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\hat{I}({\left|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{1}\right|},q_{1},\eta_{\mathrm{in}})\hat{I}({\left|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{j}\right|},q_{j},\eta_{\mathrm{in}})\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\Bigg\}\ ,

where we introduce
\mathcal{P}^{X-\mathrm{like}}
to differentiate between the various families, each composed of the convolved propagators and “renormalized” vertices defined in Eqs. (
36
,
38
).
Furthermore,
n
represents the number of undetermined momenta to be integrated over, specifically:
n=1
for the
G
-like family,
n=2
for the
C
-like and
Z
-like families,
n=3
for the
P
-like,
N
-like, and
CZ
-like families,
n=4
for the
PZ
-like and
NC
-like families, and
n=5
for the
PN
-like family.
Notably, for the variables of the projection factor
Q_{\lambda}
and the kernel functions
\hat{I}
associated with the
G
-like family, the subscript
j
is set to
1
, while for the other families,
j
is set to
2
.

Upon observing the “renormalized” diagrams in
Fig.

5
, we find that swapping the Greek letter labels adjacent to specific pairs of convolved propagators within a diagram results in an equivalent diagram as the original.
Propagators meeting this criterion are termed symmetric convolved propagators.
As stated below Eqs. (
36
), we will use the same subscript to represent the momenta of these symmetric convolved propagators, which allows for the ready aggregation of all symmetric factors associated with equivalent diagrams.

For the “renormalized”
G
-like diagram (the upper left panel in
Fig.

5
), we can write down the expression of
\mathcal{P}^{G-\mathrm{like}}
based on the “renormalized” Feynman-like rules in
Fig.

4
, namely,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{G-\mathrm{like}}=2\Biggl(\sum_{\alpha=1}^{o}V_{\mathrm{R},1}^{[\alpha]}P_{\mathrm{R},a}^{[\alpha]}V_{\mathrm{R},1}^{[\alpha]}\Biggr)\Biggl(\sum_{\beta=1}^{o}V_{\mathrm{R},1}^{[\beta]}P_{\mathrm{R},a}^{[\beta]}V_{\mathrm{R},1}^{[\beta]}\Biggr)\ .

(40)

After expanding this expression through Eqs. (
36
,
38
), each term is proportional to
P_{a}^{[\alpha]}P_{a}^{[\beta]}
, where
\alpha,\beta
must satisfy the conditions
(\alpha,\beta\leq o,\text{ and }\alpha\geq\beta)
.
Subsequently, we substitute the two subscripts
a
with the momenta
q_{1}
and
|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{1}|
, transforming
P_{a}^{[\alpha]}P_{a}^{[\beta]}
into
P^{[\alpha]}(q_{1})P^{[\beta]}(|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{1}|)
.
In particular, for scale-independent primordial non-Gaussianity up to
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
order (i.e.,
o=4
), we introduce the shorthand notation
P_{G}^{[\alpha,\beta]}=P^{[\alpha]}(q_{1})P^{[\beta]}(|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{1}|)
for convenience, and
\mathcal{P}^{G-\mathrm{like}}
reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{G-\mathrm{like}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\left(1+3A_{\mathrm{S}}G_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)^{4}P_{G}^{[1,1]}+8\left(1+3A_{\mathrm{S}}G_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)^{2}\left(F_{\mathrm{NL}}+6A_{\mathrm{S}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)^{2}P_{G}^{[2,1]}

(41)

\displaystyle+24G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}\left(1+3A_{\mathrm{S}}G_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)^{2}P_{G}^{[3,1]}+96H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}\left(1+3A_{\mathrm{S}}G_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)^{2}P_{G}^{[4,1]}

\displaystyle+8\left(F_{\mathrm{NL}}+6A_{\mathrm{S}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)^{4}P_{G}^{[2,2]}+48G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}\left(F_{\mathrm{NL}}+6A_{\mathrm{S}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)^{2}P_{G}^{[3,2]}

\displaystyle+192H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}\left(F_{\mathrm{NL}}+6A_{\mathrm{S}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)^{2}P_{G}^{[4,2]}+72G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{4}P_{G}^{[3,3]}+576G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}P_{G}^{[4,3]}

\displaystyle+1152H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{4}P_{G}^{[4,4]}\ .

This expression comprises 10 terms that are proportional to
P_{G}^{[\alpha,\beta]}
, corresponding to 10 sets of diagrams featuring the same convolved propagators.
Within any set of diagrams, one diagram without “self-closed loops” accounts for the portion of the term that is independent of
A_{\mathrm{S}}
, while the other diagrams with
i
“self-closed loops” represent the parts proportional to
A_{\mathrm{S}}^{i}
in that term.
To explore the contribution of each individual diagram in detail, we can further expand this expression and substitute each term into Eq. (
39
) individually.
The symmetric factors for the 39 diagrams shown in
Fig.

26
can be obtained from the prefactor of the corresponding expressions.
Following this, the correspondences between
\mathcal{P}^{X-\mathrm{like}}
for other families and their corresponding diagrams in Appendix
C
are analogous, as showcased in the subsequent paragraphs.

The “renormalized”
C
-like diagram (the upper middle panel in
Fig.

5
) yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{C-\mathrm{like}}=4\sum_{\gamma=1}^{o-1}\sum_{\beta=1}^{o-\gamma}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{o-\gamma}V_{\mathrm{R},1}^{[\alpha]}P_{\mathrm{R},a}^{[\alpha]}V_{\mathrm{R},2}^{[\alpha,\gamma]}P_{\mathrm{R},b}^{[\gamma]}V_{\mathrm{R},2}^{[\gamma,\beta]}P_{\mathrm{R},a}^{[\beta]}V_{\mathrm{R},1}^{[\beta]}\ .

(42)

After expanding this expression through Eqs. (
36
,
38
), each term is proportional to
P_{a}^{[\alpha]}P_{a}^{[\beta]}P_{b}^{[\gamma]}
, where the positive integers
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
must satisfy the conditions
(\alpha+\gamma,\beta+\gamma\leq o,\text{ and }\alpha\geq\beta)
.
Subsequently, we replace the two subscripts
a
with the momenta
q_{2}
and
|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{2}|
, and the two subscripts
b
with the momentum
|\mathbf{q}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{2}|
, transforming
P_{a}^{[\alpha]}P_{a}^{[\beta]}P_{b}^{[\gamma]}
into
P^{[\alpha]}(q_{2})P^{[\beta]}(|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{2}|)P^{[\gamma]}(|\mathbf{q}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{2}|)
.
In particular, for scale-independent primordial non-Gaussianity up to
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
order (i.e.,
o=4
), we introduce the shorthand notation
P_{C}^{[\alpha,\beta;\gamma]}=P^{[\alpha]}(q_{2})P^{[\beta]}(|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{2}|)P^{[\gamma]}(|\mathbf{q}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{2}|)
for convenience, leading to the expression for
\mathcal{P}^{C-\mathrm{like}}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{C-\mathrm{like}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 16\left(1+3A_{\mathrm{S}}G_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)^{2}\left(F_{\mathrm{NL}}+6A_{\mathrm{S}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)^{2}P_{C}^{[1,1;1]}

(43)

\displaystyle+96G_{\mathrm{NL}}\left(1+3A_{\mathrm{S}}G_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)\left(F_{\mathrm{NL}}+6A_{\mathrm{S}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)^{2}P_{C}^{[2,1;1]}

\displaystyle+384G_{\mathrm{NL}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}\left(1+3A_{\mathrm{S}}G_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)\left(F_{\mathrm{NL}}+6A_{\mathrm{S}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)P_{C}^{[3,1;1]}

\displaystyle+144G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}\left(F_{\mathrm{NL}}+6A_{\mathrm{S}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)^{2}P_{C}^{[2,2;1]}+1152G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}H_{\mathrm{NL}}\left(F_{\mathrm{NL}}+6A_{\mathrm{S}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)P_{C}^{[3,2;1]}

\displaystyle+2304G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}P_{C}^{[3,3;1]}+72G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}\left(1+3A_{\mathrm{S}}G_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)^{2}P_{C}^{[1,1;2]}

\displaystyle+576G_{\mathrm{NL}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}\left(1+3A_{\mathrm{S}}G_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)\left(F_{\mathrm{NL}}+6A_{\mathrm{S}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)P_{C}^{[2,1;2]}

\displaystyle+1152H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}\left(F_{\mathrm{NL}}+6A_{\mathrm{S}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)^{2}P_{C}^{[2,2;2]}+384H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}\left(1+3A_{\mathrm{S}}G_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)^{2}P_{C}^{[1,1;3]}\ .

This expression consists of 10 terms that are proportional to
P_{C}^{[\alpha,\beta;\gamma]}
, corresponding to 10 sets of diagrams featuring the same convolved propagators.
The contribution from each diagram can be evaluated by fully expanding this expression and substituting each term into Eq. (
39
) individually, allowing us to deduce the symmetric factors for the 41 diagrams depicted in
Fig.

27
from the prefactors.

-like diagram (the center panel in

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{N-\mathrm{like}}=\sum_{\delta=1}^{o-1}\sum_{\gamma=1}^{o-1}\sum_{\beta=1}^{\min(o-\gamma,o-\delta)}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\min(o-\gamma,o-\delta)}P_{\mathrm{R},a}^{[\alpha]}V_{\mathrm{R},2}^{[\alpha,\gamma]}P_{\mathrm{R},c}^{[\gamma]}V_{\mathrm{R},2}^{[\gamma,\beta]}P_{\mathrm{R},a}^{[\beta]}V_{\mathrm{R},2}^{[\beta,\delta]}P_{\mathrm{R},c}^{[\delta]}V_{\mathrm{R},2}^{[\delta,\alpha]}\ ,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 16\left(F_{\mathrm{NL}}+6A_{\mathrm{S}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)^{4}P_{N}^{[1,1;1,1]}+288G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}\left(F_{\mathrm{NL}}+6A_{\mathrm{S}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)^{2}P_{N}^{[1,2;1,1]}

(49)

\displaystyle+1536H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}\left(F_{\mathrm{NL}}+6A_{\mathrm{S}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)^{2}P_{N}^{[1,3;1,1]}+648G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{4}P_{N}^{[2,2;1,1]}+6912G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}P_{N}^{[2,3;1,1]}

\displaystyle+18432H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{4}P_{N}^{[3,3;1,1]}+1728G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}H_{\mathrm{NL}}\left(F_{\mathrm{NL}}+6A_{\mathrm{S}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}\right)P_{N}^{[1,2;2,1]}

To summarize briefly, for the primordial non-Gaussianity up to
o
-th order approximation, we can derive the integrals for
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{X-\mathrm{like}}
across all nine families by substituting Eqs. (
40
,
42
,
44
,
46
,
48
,
50
,

,

,
56
) into Eq. (

) respectively.
A specific case where
o=4
is detailed in Eqs. (
41
,
43
,
45
,
47
,
49
,
51
,
53
,
55
,
57
).
Furthermore, in this example, in order to analyze the specific contributions from the 236 independent diagrams depicted in
Fig.

26
-
Fig.

34
, we can substitute individual terms from these equations, rather than the complete equations, into Eq. (
39
), with the symmetric factor for each specific diagram being readily inferred from the corresponding coefficients.
Since we have carried out the integrals of the convolutions of the power spectrum for the cases of
\xi=2,3,4
in Eq. (
35
) under the condition of scale-independent primordial non-Gaussianity, this process encompasses a total of 81 integrals.
In this manner,
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(a,b,c)}
, which signifies the component proportional to
F_{\mathrm{NL}}^{a}G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{b}H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{c}A_{\mathrm{S}}^{(a+2b+3c)/2}
as introduced in Eq. (
32
), can be directly obtained using these individual calculations.
Based on the results from these 19 categories, we are able to calculate the homogeneous component and primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneities of the initial scalar-induced gravitational wave energy-density spectrum, which will be presented in the following.

3.3
Numerical results

To analyze the influence of the various model parameters on the isotropic component of the scalar-induced gravitational wave background, we perform numerical calculations of the 81 integrals for all contributing diagrams individually.
Specifically, with the spectral width set at
\sigma=1
and constant coefficients, such as symmetric factors, scale-independent primordial non-Gaussianity parameters, and the spectral amplitude
A_{\mathrm{S}}
, excluded from the integrands, we utilize
vegas
to carry out numerical integration for the unscaled spectra for diagrams that do not contain “self-closed loops”.
While the spectral width
\sigma
is fixed for simplicity, other values can be readily accommodated if required.
Although variations in
\sigma
may affect the numerical results, they do not alter our conclusions.
A detailed explanation of the process for transforming the integrals into a numerically advantageous form is provided in Appendix
D
, with a focus on the radiation-dominated era of the early Universe.
By subsequently multiplying the calculated results by the corresponding constant coefficients, we obtain
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{D}
, where
D
labels the specific diagram as shown in
Fig.

26
-
Fig.

34
.
The sum of
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{D}
within a specific family leads to
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{X-\mathrm{like}}
, while the sum of
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{Y}
within a specific category results in
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(a,b,c)}
.
The total energy-density fraction spectrum
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)
is obtained by summing over all categories of
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(a,b,c)}
or all families of
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{X-\mathrm{like}}
, akin to Eq. (
32
) and Eq. (
33
).
Following this, we apply Eq. (
20
) to derive the present-day energy density fraction spectrum
\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\nu)
.
In this subsection, we will present these numerical results and demonstrate the effects of primordial non-Gaussianity on the isotropic component of the scalar-induced gravitational wave background.

Figure 6
:
Contributions from the nine families of scalar-induced gravitational waves energy-density fraction spectrum. Dotted lines indicate where the values are negative.

To compare the contributions of the nine families to the isotropic component of the scalar-induced gravitational wave background produced during the radiation-dominated era, we present their spectra
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{X-\mathrm{like}}
alongside the total average energy-density spectrum
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
(indicated in black) in
Fig.

6
.
We consider the scenarios where
A_{\mathrm{S}}=3.6\times 10^{-3}
and
|H_{\mathrm{NL}}|A_{\mathrm{S}}^{3/2}=0.3|G_{\mathrm{NL}}|A_{\mathrm{S}}=0.3^{2}|F_{\mathrm{NL}}|A_{\mathrm{S}}^{1/2}=0.3^{3}
.
Here, we specifically focus on the effects of varying the signs of
G_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
, while keep the sign of
F_{\mathrm{NL}}
positive due to the sign degeneracy between
F_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
in
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
.
In more detail, the upper panels correspond to positive values of
F_{\mathrm{NL}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}
, while the left panels depict positive
G_{\mathrm{NL}}
.
Conversely, the lower panels illustrate negative
F_{\mathrm{NL}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}
, and the right panels show negative
G_{\mathrm{NL}}
.
Across all scenarios, the “
G
-like” contributions are generally dominant among the nine families, while contributions from other families related to the connected components of the four-point correlator of
\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}
are typically subdominant.
In fact, the “
G
-like” spectra are significantly larger than their Gaussian counterparts, indicating that loop corrections arising from non-Gaussian effects are substantial.
Excluding the “
G
-like” contributions, the contributions from the “
C
-like”, “
Z
-like”, and “
CZ
-like” families are usually more significant, unless
G_{\mathrm{NL}}
is negative and
F_{\mathrm{NL}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}
is positive; in such cases, the “
P
-like” contribution becomes the most prominent.
Notably, these contributions are more pronounced in the ultraviolet regime compared to the “
G
-like” contributions.
These features underscore the necessity of studying all families, as this may aid in determining the values of primordial non-Gaussianity parameters.
Furthermore, for negative primordial non-Gaussianity parameters, it is important to note that contributions from certain families, such as “
CZ
-like”, “
PZ
-like”, and “
NC
-like”, could also be negative within specific frequency bands, although they are often subdominant relative to contributions from other families in those ranges.
Additionally, the exploration of other scenarios, including various values of
\sigma
and additional epochs in the early Universe, can be readily extended for further investigation.

Figure 7
:
Unscaled (or equivalently,
A_{\mathrm{S}}=1
,
F_{\mathrm{NL}}=1
,
G_{\mathrm{NL}}=1
and
H_{\mathrm{NL}}=1
) components of the energy-density fraction spectra of scalar-induced gravitational waves in powers of the
F_{\mathrm{NL}}
,
G_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
.

In addition to the decomposition into nine families based on the topology of the diagrams shown in
Fig.

26
-
Fig.

34
,
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
is also classified into 19 distinct categories according to the powers of the three primordial non-Gaussianity parameters, as introduced in Eq. (
32
).
The numerical results presented in
Fig.

7
illustrate the unscaled components of the energy-density fraction spectra, denoted as
(F_{\mathrm{NL}}^{a}G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{b}H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{c}A_{S}^{(a+2b+3c+4)/2})^{-1}\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(a,b,c)}
.
Specifically,
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,0,0)}
corresponds to the energy-density fraction spectrum in the Gaussian case, while
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,1,0)}=24A_{\mathrm{S}}G_{\mathrm{NL}}\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,0,0)}
and
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,0,0)}
are the leading-order categories in
A_{\mathrm{S}}
arising from primordial non-Gaussianity.
These results facilitate an efficient scan of the parameter space for
A_{\mathrm{S}}
and primordial non-Gaussianity parameters by allowing for the subsequent rescaling of the unscaled spectra, which is particularly advantageous for isolating the impacts of each parameter on the total spectrum.
The 19 profiles illustrated in the figure illustrate that as the power of
A_{\mathrm{S}}
(i.e.,
(a+2b+3c+4)/2
) increases, the unscaled spectrum
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(a,b,c)}
generally displays larger amplitudes.
For instance, the unscaled spectral amplitudes of
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,0,0)}
and
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,1,0)}
are nearly an order of magnitude greater than that of
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,0,0)}
, suggesting that the individual contributions from
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,0,0)}
and
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,1,0)}
become comparable to the Gaussian component for
F_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}A_{\mathrm{S}}\sim G_{\mathrm{NL}}A_{\mathrm{S}}\sim 1
when
A_{\mathrm{S}}\gtrsim 0.1
.
Likewise, other categories with higher powers of
A_{\mathrm{S}}
cannot be neglected if
A_{\mathrm{S}}
and the primordial non-Gaussianity parameters are sufficiently large.
For categories of the same order in
A_{\mathrm{S}}
, most unscaled spectral amplitudes are comparable, with the exception of
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(4,0,0)}
,
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,4,0)}
, and
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1,1,1)}
.
The unscaled spectral amplitudes of
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(4,0,0)}
and
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,4,0)}
are significantly smaller than those of other categories at
\mathcal{O}(A_{\mathrm{S}}^{6})
, whereas the unscaled spectral amplitude of
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1,1,1)}
is notably larger than the amplitudes of other categories at
\mathcal{O}(A_{\mathrm{S}}^{5})
.
Therefore, when the primordial non-Gaussianity parameters are of the same order of magnitude, the figure clearly illustrates that the largest contribution at the
\mathcal{O}(A_{\mathrm{S}}^{5})
level arises from
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1,1,1)}
.

Figure 8
:
Energy-density fraction spectra of scalar-induced gravitational waves in the present Universe, where
\nu_{\ast}=q_{\ast}/2\pi
.

To investigate the impact of various orders of primordial non-Gaussianity and their respective signs on the energy density of the scalar-induced gravitational wave, we present the present-day energy-density fraction spectrum of scalar-induced gravitational waves, denoted as
h^{2}\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\nu)
, in
Fig.

8
.
The model parameters, including the spectral amplitude
A_{\mathrm{S}}
, the spectral width
\sigma
of the primordial curvature spectrum, and the modes of the primordial non-Gaussianity parameters (when non-zero), are consistent with those from
Fig.

6
.
In comparison to the Gaussian case (depicted in black), it is observed that positive (negative) primordial non-Gaussianity enhances (suppresses) the spectral magnitude of
h^{2}\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\nu)
, which span nearly two orders of magnitude.
The black, brown, violet, and red curves illustrate that the presence of higher orders of positive primordial non-Gaussianity results in an increase in spectral magnitude.
In contrast, the spectral magnitudes featuring negative
G_{\mathrm{NL}}
with positive or zero
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
are smaller than those of the Gaussian case, particularly in the low-frequency range.
This figure reveals that the sign of
G_{\mathrm{NL}}
has a more substantial impact on the energy-density spectrum than the sign of
F_{\mathrm{NL}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}
.
This difference can be understood by referring to
Fig.

7
.
In fact, altering the signs of
G_{\mathrm{NL}}
or
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
only affects the signs of categories with odd
b
or odd
c
, respectively.
As indicated in
Fig.

7
, the dominant contribution from categories with odd
b
is
\omega_{\mathrm{gw}}^{(0,1,0)}
at
\mathcal{O}(A_{\mathrm{S}}^{3})
order, while the dominant contribution from categories with odd
c
is
\omega_{\mathrm{gw}}^{(1,0,1)}
at
\mathcal{O}(A_{\mathrm{S}}^{4})
order.
Given that we have fixed
A_{\mathrm{S}}=3.6\times 10^{-3}\ll 10^{-1}
, the contribution from
\omega_{\mathrm{gw}}^{(0,1,0)}
to the total spectrum is significantly larger than that from
\omega_{\mathrm{gw}}^{(1,0,1)}
.
Additionally, it is noteworthy that the spectral shape of
h^{2}\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\nu)
remains nearly invariant across various levels of primordial non-Gaussianity.
Specifically, as the frequency increases from
\nu/\nu_{\ast}=10^{-4}
to
\nu/\nu_{\ast}=10^{2}
, the spectral index decreases from approximately
3
to
-9
(as will be demonstrated in Subsection
6.1
).
This characteristic will be crucial for investigating the anisotropies in the scalar-induced gravitational wave background in subsequent sections.

Figure 9
:
Present-day energy-density fraction spectrum of SIGWs with respect to the
F_{\mathrm{NL}}
,
G_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
with the frequency set to

. Dotted lines represent

, and dashed lines represent

.

Fig.

9
displays the dependence of the present-day energy-density fraction spectrum of scalar-induced gravitational waves on primordial non-Gaussianity parameters and
A_{\mathrm{S}}
.
A
3\times 4
array of contour plots is presented for
h^{2}\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}
at the frequency
\nu=\nu_{\ast}
in relation to

and

.
From left to right, the values of
A_{\mathrm{S}}
increase from
10^{-4}
to

in each column.
The three rows, arranged from top to bottom, correspond to values of

of 0.3, 0.5, and 0.7, respectively.
Within each row of contour plots, specific values of
G_{\mathrm{NL}}A_{\mathrm{S}}
and

correspond to the same position.
Each contour plot is divided into four triangular regions by dotted lines: the left and right regions indicate where
H_{\mathrm{NL}}\sqrt{A_{\mathrm{S}}^{3}}
is less than

Furthermore, the area enclosed by dashed lines signifies the condition
|F_{\mathrm{NL}}|\sqrt{A_{\mathrm{S}}}+|G_{\mathrm{NL}}|A_{\mathrm{S}}+|H_{\mathrm{NL}}|\sqrt{A_{\mathrm{S}}^{3}}<1
.
It is noteworthy that the plots in the first row do not contain dashed lines as they satisfy this condition at all positions.
The intersection regions of the left and right triangular regions with the area bounded by dashed lines in each panel represent the ranges of primordial non-Gaussianity parameters that meet perturbativity conditions.
As anticipated, the contour plots in each row display similar patterns.
The primary difference lies in the spectral magnitudes of
h^{2}\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\nu)
, which consistently increase with
A_{\mathrm{S}}
.
Additionally, for each contour plot, the spectral magnitude attains its maximum value when

reaches its peak values within the perturbativity ranges, while it reaches the minimum value when
G_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
assume small negative values due to the positive value of
F_{\mathrm{NL}}
.
It is evident that
h^{2}\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\nu)
exhibits greater sensitivity to
H_{\mathrm{NL}}\sqrt{A_{\mathrm{S}}^{3}}
than to
G_{\mathrm{NL}}A_{\mathrm{S}}
, suggesting that the contributions from the
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
order may be more significant than those from the
G_{\mathrm{NL}}
order within the perturbativity ranges.

.

reaches

A_{\mathrm{S}}
.
Notably, detectable scalar-induced gravitational waves by these space-borne detectors may be accompanied by the production of primordial black holes, which could potentially account for all dark matter (see reviews in Refs.
Green and Kavanagh (2021)
;

;
Escrivà et al. (2022a)
and references therein).
Specifically, apart from the amplitude of the primordial curvature power spectrum
A_{\mathrm{S}}
, the abundance of primordial black holes is also significantly influenced by primordial non-Gaussianity
Bullock and Primack (1997)
;

.
Thus, evaluating the model parameters in relation to scalar-induced gravitational wave signals is crucial.
However, it is important to acknowledge the significant degeneracies among the model parameters that affect the energy-density fraction spectra.
The four spectra in
Fig.

10
are closely aligned, yet they correspond to entirely distinct combinations of parameters.
Therefore, the scalar-induced gravitational wave energy-density fraction spectrum characterizing the isotropic component may not sufficiently capture all statistical features of primordial curvature perturbations.
A further analysis on the anisotropies in the scalar-induced gravitational wave background is essential to extract more information about the early Universe.

4
Anisotropies in the SIGW background

In Section
2
, we have conducted an analysis of the energy-density full spectrum of scalar-induced gravitational waves, which includes both the underlying isotropic component and the superimposed fluctuations.
The examination of the isotropic component is comprehensively presented in Section
3
.
Building upon the methodologies and findings of these investigations, we aim to scrutinize the statistics of fluctuations on the scalar-induced gravitational wave background in the subsequent sections.
In this section, we focus on investigating the two-point correlation function of density contrast in scalar-induced gravitational waves, a fundamental quantity for characterizing the anisotropies in the scalar-induced gravitational wave background.

4.1
Angular power spectrum: two-point angular correlation

To characterize the anisotropies in the scalar-induced gravitational wave background, we adopt the standard treatment for cosmic microwave background anisotropies and introduce the reduced angular power spectrum of scalar-induced gravitational waves using the two-point angular correlator of the present-day density contrast
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}
.
This spectrum describes the statistical properties of the scalar-induced gravitational wave energy density from any two directions.
For convenience, we denote
\mathbf{q}=q\mathbf{n}
and utilize spherical harmonics to decompose
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\mathbf{q})
into
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0,\ell m}(2\pi\nu)
, as given by

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\mathbf{q})=\sum_{\ell m}\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0,\ell m}(2\pi\nu)Y_{\ell m}(\mathbf{n})\,,\ \text{where }\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0,\ell m}(2\pi\nu)=\int\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{n}\,\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\mathbf{q})Y_{\ell m}^{\ast}(\mathbf{n})\ .

(58)

By applying the cosmological principle, which assumes statistical homogeneity and isotropy on large scales, we achieve rotation-invariance in the gravitational wave background.
Consequently, the two-point correlator of
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0,\ell m}
defines the reduced angular power spectrum in the form of

\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{2}\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0,\ell_{i}m_{i}}(2\pi\nu)\right\rangle=\delta_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}\delta_{m_{1}m_{2}}(-1)^{m_{1}}\tilde{C}_{\ell_{1}}(\nu)\ .

(59)

Previous works in Refs.
Bartolo et al. (2020a)
;
Li et al. (2023a)
;
Li et al. (2024b)
;
Valbusa Dall’Armi et al. (2021)
;
Dimastrogiovanni et al. (2022)
;
Bartolo and others (2022)
;
Auclair and others (2023)
;
Ünal et al. (2021)
;
Malhotra et al. (2021)
;
Carr et al. (2021)
;
Cui et al. (2023)
;
Malhotra et al. (2023)
;
Valbusa Dall’Armi et al. (2023)
;
Bagui and others (2025)
have extensively explored the reduced angular power spectrum of gravitational waves originating from cosmological sources
Caprini and Figueroa (2018)
.
Due to the limitations in the angular resolutions of ongoing and planned gravitational wave detectors
Baker and others (2021)
;
Capurri et al. (2023)
;
Gair et al. (2015)
;
Romano and Cornish (2017)
;
Bartolo and others (2022)
;
Abbott and others (2017)
;
Abbott and others (2019)
;
Abbott and others (2021)
;
Agazie and others (2023a)
, these investigations primarily focus on the low multipoles
\ell
, which aligns with the focus of our own work.
Nevertheless, the extension of our analysis to higher multipoles
\ell
can be theoretically accomplished with relative ease if necessary.

Figure 11
:
Feynman-like diagram for the two-point correlator of
\omega_{\mathrm{gw}}^{(1)}(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})
.

The derivation process of
\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)
for scalar-induced gravitational wave has been previously discussed in Refs.
Bartolo et al. (2020b)
;
Bartolo et al. (2019)
;
Bartolo et al. (2020a)
;
Li et al. (2023a)
;
Li et al. (2024b)
, paralleling the derivation process for the cosmic microwave background at low multipoles.
We will provide a concise overview of this process.
According to Eqs. (
14a
,
16
,
22
), the calculation of
\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)\sim\langle\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}\rangle
involves the two-point correlator of
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})
.
As emphasized in Section
2
, Eqs. (
14
) are applicable for evaluating this correlator, suggesting that
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}
can be expressed as a polynomial in powers of
\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}
, as indicated in Eq. (
26
).
Thus, the reduced angular power spectrum
\tilde{C}_{\ell}
is a polynomial in powers of
A_{\mathrm{L}}
.
Given that
A_{\mathrm{L}}
is a small quantity of order
\mathcal{O}(10^{-9})
, we focus on the leading order in
A_{\mathrm{L}}
.
We approximate the density contrast as
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}^{(1)}
, which encompasses the initial inhomogeneities and the Sachs-Wolfe effect at order
\mathcal{O}(\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}})
in Eq. (
27
).
The Feynman-like diagram for the associated two-point correlator
\langle\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}\rangle
is illustrated in
Fig.

11
, based on
Fig.

1
and
Fig.

3
.
Below, we briefly review the derivation process for the explicit expression of
\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)
.
Following the decomposition of
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}^{(1)}
using spherical harmonics, as outlined in Eqs. (
58
), we express the two-point correlator
\langle\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0,\ell_{1}m_{1}}^{(1)}\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0,\ell_{2}m_{2}}^{(1)}\rangle
in Eq. (
59
) as an integral, with integration variables
\mathbf{k}_{1},\mathbf{k}_{2},\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}
.
Subsequently, after moving integration-independent factors (variables only dependent on
\eta
and
\nu
) outside the integral, the integrand consists of the spherical harmonics
Y_{\ell m}^{\ast}(\mathbf{n})
and
e^{i\mathbf{k}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{2}}\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{k}_{1})\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{k}_{2})\rangle
.
The latter can be reduced to
\delta^{(3)}(\mathbf{k}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{2})e^{i\mathbf{k}_{1}\cdot(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{2})}2\pi^{2}\Delta^{2}_{\mathrm{L}}/k_{1}^{3}
according to Eqs. (
10
) and (
11
).
Moreover, in the leading-order approximation of the Boltzmann equation, as discussed in Appendix
F
and Refs.
Contaldi (2017)
;
Bartolo et al. (2019)
;
Bartolo et al. (2020b)
, the line-of-sight relation
\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0}=(\eta_{\mathrm{in}}-\eta_{0})\mathbf{n}_{1}
is effective, resulting in
\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{2}=(\eta_{\mathrm{in}}-\eta_{0})(\mathbf{n}_{1}-\mathbf{n}_{2})
.
Therefore, the exponential factor
e^{i\mathbf{k}_{1}\cdot(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{2})}\sim e^{i\mathbf{k}_{1}\cdot(\mathbf{n}_{2}-\mathbf{n}_{1})(\eta_{0}-\eta_{\mathrm{in}})}
can be rewritten in terms of spherical harmonics and spherical Bessel functions using the identity

e^{ir(\mathbf{n}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{2})}=4\pi\sum_{\ell m}i^{\ell}j_{\ell}(r)Y^{\ast}_{\ell m}(\mathbf{n}_{1})Y_{\ell m}(\mathbf{n}_{2})\ ,

(60)

where
\mathbf{n}_{1}
and
\mathbf{n}_{2}
denote unit vectors and
r\in\mathbb{R}
.
It is crucial to note that
j_{\ell}(-\mu)=(-1)^{\ell}j_{\ell}(\mu)
.
By utilizing the properties of spherical harmonics and the integral relation

2
2

2

The properties of spherical harmonics relevant to the derivation processes in this work include the conjugate transformation
(-1)^{\ell}Y_{\ell m}(\mathbf{n})=Y_{\ell m}(-\mathbf{n})=Y_{\ell m}^{\ast}(\mathbf{n})=(-1)^{m}Y_{\ell-m}(\mathbf{n})
, orthogonality
\int\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{n}\,Y_{\ell_{1}m_{1}}(\mathbf{n})Y_{\ell_{2}m_{2}}^{\ast}(\mathbf{n})=\delta_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}\delta_{m_{1}m_{2}}
, and completeness
\sum_{\ell m}Y_{\ell m}^{\ast}(\mathbf{n}_{1})Y_{\ell m}(\mathbf{n}_{2})=\delta^{(2)}(\mathbf{n}_{1}-\mathbf{n}_{2})
.

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\ln k\,j_{\ell}^{2}(k\left(\eta_{0}-\eta_{\mathrm{in}}\right))=\frac{1}{2\ell(\ell+1)}\ ,

(61)

we successfully derive the fundamental formula for the reduced angular power spectrum, leading to

\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)=\frac{2\pi A_{\mathrm{L}}}{\ell(\ell+1)}\left[\frac{\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}(q)}{\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)}+\frac{3}{5}\left(6-n_{\mathrm{gw}}(\nu)\right)\right]^{2}\ ,

(62)

where the relationship between
\nu
and
q
is illustrated in Eq. (
21
) and the subsequent discussion therein.
In this formula, the first term in the square brackets corresponds to the 1st-order primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneity of scalar-induced gravitational waves at the production time, while the second term corresponds to the Sachs-Wolfe effect and the initial inhomogeneity arising from non-adiabaticity due to large-scale scalar perturbations.
Thus, this formula indicates that the anisotropies in scalar-induced gravitational waves are attributed to both the presence of primordial non-Gaussianity and the large-scale inhomogeneity of cosmological fluids during propagation.

While the average energy-density spectrum
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)
has been derived in Section
3
and the spectral index
n_{\mathrm{gw}}
can be obtained directly from Eq. (
25
), the remaining task is to derive the specific expression for the 1st-order large-scale modulation
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}(q)
introduced in Eqs. (
14
).
As illustrated in
Fig.

11
, the connected diagram of
\langle\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}\rangle
consists of two diagrams of
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}
(the first panel of
Fig.

3
), with their extensional red solid lines connected to form a single diagram, referred to as a “non-Gaussian bridge”.
Compared to the diagram of
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
in
Fig.

2
, the inclusion of the “non-Gaussian bridge” in
Fig.

11
results in a transformation of the vertices and symmetry factors.
Due to the symmetry between the two diagrams of
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}
within
Fig.

11
, we can derive the specific expression for
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}
, or equivalently for
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}
according to Eq (
14b
), via the first (upper right) panel of
Fig.

3
.
Based on the Feynman-like rules in
Fig.

1
, the attachment of a red solid line transforms a
V_{0}^{[i]}
-vertex into a
V_{1}^{[i+1]}
-vertex, where
i\leq o-1
.
Furthermore, the attachment also alters the symmetric factor of the diagram, involving a change in the permutation factor from
i!
to
(i+1)!
along with selecting
1
out of
N_{i}

V_{0}^{[i]}
-vertices.
Consequently, for primordial non-Gaussianity up to
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
order (i.e.,
o=4
), the large-scale modulation at
\mathcal{O}(\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}})
order,
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}
, is given by

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{c=0}^{4}\sum_{b=0}^{\lfloor 4-c\rfloor}\sum_{a=0}^{\lfloor 4-b-c\rfloor}\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(a,b,c)}(q)\sum_{i=1}^{o-1=3}\left(\frac{(i+1)!}{i!}\frac{V_{1}^{[i+1]}}{V_{0}^{[i]}}\right)^{1}\binom{N_{i}}{1}\ .

(63)

Recall that
N_{i}
denotes the number of vertices
V_{0}^{[i]}
, where
N_{1}=4-a-b-c
,
N_{2}=a
,
N_{3}=b
, and
N_{4}=c
, as established in Subsection
2.3
.
This formulation is readily extensible to include higher-order primordial non-Gaussianity; one need only expand the superscript
(a,b,c)
to incorporate the powers of higher-order primordial non-Gaussianity parameters and update the counts
N_{i}
accordingly.
In particular, for scale-independent primordial non-Gaussianity parameters, namely,
V_{j}^{[1]}=F_{\mathrm{NL}}
,
V_{j}^{[2]}=G_{\mathrm{NL}}
, and
V_{j}^{[3]}=H_{\mathrm{NL}}
, we have

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2F_{\mathrm{NL}}\Bigl(4\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,0,0)}+2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,0,0)}+3\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,1,0)}+\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,1,0)}+2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,2,0)}+2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1,0,1)}+\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,3,0)}+\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1,1,1)}

\displaystyle\quad+2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,0,2)}+\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,1,2)}\Bigr)+\frac{3G_{\mathrm{NL}}}{F_{\mathrm{NL}}}\Bigl(2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,0,0)}+4\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(4,0,0)}+2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,1,0)}+\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1,0,1)}+2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,2,0)}

\displaystyle\quad+3\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(3,0,1)}+\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1,1,1)}+\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1,2,1)}+2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,0,2)}+\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1,0,3)}\Bigr)+\frac{4H_{\mathrm{NL}}}{G_{\mathrm{NL}}}\Bigl(2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,0,0)}+\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,1,0)}

\displaystyle\quad+\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,1,0)}+2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,2,0)}+3\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,3,0)}+\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1,1,1)}+4\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,4,0)}+2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1,2,1)}+\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,1,2)}+2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,2,2)}\Bigr)\ .

By substituting Eq. (
4.1
) into Eq. (
62
), we obtain the complete expression for the scalar-induced gravitational wave reduced angular power spectrum
\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)
.

Eq. (
62
) demonstrates that the multipole dependence of the reduced angular power spectrum yields
\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)\propto[\ell(\ell+1)]^{-1}
, similar to the behavior observed for the cosmic microwave background at low multipoles, as reported in previous studies
Bartolo et al. (2020a)
;
Li et al. (2023a)
;
Li et al. (2024b)
;
Wang et al. (2024a)
;
Dimastrogiovanni et al. (2023)
.
In contrast, the frequency dependence of
\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)
, described by
\ell(\ell+1)\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)
, is peculiar and primarily due to the initial inhomogeneity arising from primordial non-Gaussianity.
While the frequency dependence of the second term in the square brackets of Eq. (
62
) is entirely determined by the energy-density fraction spectrum, the frequency dependence of the first term,
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}(q)/\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)
, is non-trivial.
Thus, we anticipate that the angular power spectrum will serve as a novel tool for extracting valuable information from the scalar-induced gravitational wave background.
Both the multipole and frequency dependencies are vital for distinguishing scalar-induced gravitational wave signals from various sources
Bartolo and others (2022)
, as gravitational waves produced by different sources may exhibit diverse features in their multipole and frequency dependencies.
For gravitational waves generated by binary black holes, the multipole dependence of angular power spectra is roughly proportional to
(\ell+1/2)^{-1}

Cusin et al. (2018)
;
Cusin et al. (2017)
;
Cusin et al. (2020)
;
Cusin et al. (2019)
;
Jenkins et al. (2019a)
;
Jenkins et al. (2018)
;
Jenkins et al. (2019b)
;
Contaldi (2017)
;
Wang et al. (2022)
;
Mukherjee and Silk (2020)
;
Bavera et al. (2022)
;
Bellomo et al. (2022)
, while for gravitational waves originating from cosmic string loops, it behaves as
\ell^{0}

Jenkins and Sakellariadou (2018)
;
Kuroyanagi et al. (2017)
;
Olmez et al. (2012)
.
However, the multipole dependence of the angular power spectra for certain cosmological gravitational waves is expected to behave similarly to that of scalar-induced gravitational waves, including sources such as inflation
Adshead et al. (2021a)
;
Dimastrogiovanni et al. (2022)
;
Dimastrogiovanni et al. (2020)
;
Jeong and Kamionkowski (2012)
;
Valbusa Dall’Armi et al. (2023)
, domain walls
Liu et al. (2021)
, first-order phase transitions
Li et al. (2023b)
;
Li et al. (2022)
;
Domcke et al. (2020)
;
Jinno et al. (2021)
;
Geller et al. (2018)
;
Kumar et al. (2021)
;
Racco and Poletti (2023)
, and preheating
Bethke et al. (2013)
;
Bethke et al. (2014)
;
Caprini and Figueroa (2018)
.
The common feature among these cosmological gravitational waves is that their density contrasts can be decomposed into direction-independent initial inhomogeneity terms and identical propagation terms.
To distinguish scalar-induced gravitational waves from these sources, in addition to analyzing frequency and multipole dependencies, cross-correlations between gravitational wave background and various observables, such as the cosmic microwave background
Dimastrogiovanni et al. (2022)
;
Cusin et al. (2018)
;
Ricciardone et al. (2021)
;
Malhotra et al. (2021)
;
Braglia and Kuroyanagi (2021)
;
Capurri et al. (2022)
;
Dimastrogiovanni et al. (2023)
;
Galloni et al. (2022)
;
Ding and Tian (2024)
;
Cyr et al. (2024)
;
Zhao et al. (2024)
, large-scale structure
Cusin et al. (2018)
;
Cañas-Herrera et al. (2020)
;
Alonso et al. (2020)
;
Yang et al. (2020)
;
Yang et al. (2023)
;
Libanore et al. (2023)
;
Bosi et al. (2023)
;
Balaudo et al. (2023)
;
Bravo and Riquelme (2025)
, and 21 cm lines
Scelfo et al. (2022)
;
Seto and Cooray (2006)
, are also beneficial.

We direct our attention to the behavior of
\ell(\ell+1)\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)
for various values of the three primordial non-Gaussianity parameters.
First, we consider a scenario where the primordial non-Gaussianity, characterized by
(|F_{\mathrm{NL}}|A_{\mathrm{S}}^{1/2}+|G_{\mathrm{NL}}|A_{\mathrm{S}}+|H_{\mathrm{NL}}|A_{\mathrm{S}}^{3/2})
, is sufficiently small such that the Gaussian component of the energy-density fraction spectrum,
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,0,0)}
, dominates among all categories of the scalar-induced gravitational wave energy-density fraction spectrum.
In Eq. (
4.1
), the leading-order term of
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}
in
A_{\mathrm{S}}
is
8F_{\mathrm{NL}}\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,0,0)}
, leading to an approximate expression for the frequency dependence of the reduced angular power spectrum as

\ell(\ell+1)\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)=2\pi A_{\mathrm{L}}\left[8F_{\mathrm{NL}}+\frac{3}{5}\left(6-n_{\mathrm{gw}}(\nu)\right)\right]^{2}\ .

(65)

As discussed with reference to
Fig.

8
, since
n_{\mathrm{gw}}
approximately varies from
3
to
-9
in the frequency range
(10^{-4}\nu_{\ast},10^{2}\nu_{\ast})
, the second term in the square brackets of this formula roughly changes from
3
to
15
.
Therefore, if
F_{\mathrm{NL}}
is of order one, the first term could become comparable to the second term, resulting in notable alterations in the profile of
\ell(\ell+1)\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)
when the sign of
F_{\mathrm{NL}}
is changed.
Moreover, the sum of the powers of
F_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
is no longer an even integer in
\ell(\ell+1)\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)
.
This implies that the sign degeneracy between
F_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
in the energy-density fraction spectrum, as discussed in Subsection
2.3
and Subsection
3.3
, is broken by the reduced angular power spectrum.
Although the energy-density spectrum closely resembles the Gaussian case, the reduced angular power spectrum for the non-Gaussian case exhibits significant deviations from its Gaussian counterpart.
Consequently, analyzing the scalar-induced gravitational wave angular power spectrum is crucial in the pursuit of primordial non-Gaussianity.
For large primordial non-Gaussianity, the first term,
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}(q)/\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)
, varies with different values of the primordial non-Gaussianity parameters, potentially resulting in distinct features in the reduced angular power spectrum.
This will be elaborated upon in the numerical results presented subsequently.
It is possible to infer the model parameters by combining insights from the energy-density fraction spectrum and the reduced angular power spectrum.

The reduced angular power spectrum
\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)
quantifies the covariance of the relative anisotropies in the scalar-induced gravitational wave background.
To evaluate the detectability of these anisotropies in scalar-induced gravitational waves, we can de-normalize
\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)
by multiplying it by the square of the average energy-density spectrum, thereby yielding the angular power spectrum as follows

C_{\ell}(\nu)=\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}^{2}(\nu)\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)\ .

(66)

This angular power spectrum is defined in such a manner that it characterizes the covariance of the absolute anisotropies in the scalar-induced gravitational wave background.
It can be directly compared to the noise spectrum of upcoming gravitational wave detectors, facilitating the assessment of the detectability of the corresponding scalar-induced gravitational waves signals.

Figure 12
:
Angular power spectrum of SIGWs.

Based on the numerical results for the 19 categories of the scalar-induced gravitational wave energy-density fraction spectrum illustrated in
Fig.

7
, we compute the 1st-order large-scale modulation through Eq. (
4.1
) and the corresponding (reduced) angular power spectrum of scalar-induced gravitational waves via Eq. (
62
).
The results for
\ell(\ell+1)\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)
(left panel) and
\ell(\ell+1)C_{\ell}(\nu)
(right panel) concerning frequency
\nu
are presented in
Fig.

12
.
For consistency, we utilize the same sets of model parameters as those in
Fig.

8
, while also considering variations in the signs of
F_{\mathrm{NL}}
.
This represents scenarios where primordial non-Gaussianity is pronounced.
By combining this figure with Eq. (
65
), which pertains to cases of small primordial non-Gaussianity, we investigate the effects of varying magnitudes of primordial non-Gaussianity on the anisotropies in the scalar-induced gravitational wave background.
Specifically, we depict the (reduced) angular power spectrum for the Gaussian scenario using solid black curves for comparison.
Given that the Sachs-Wolfe effect and the initial inhomogeneity arising from non-adiabaticity remain relatively unchanged in non-Gaussian scenarios due to the similar profiles of energy-density fraction spectra shown in
Fig.

8
, the black solid curve can also be regarded as these effects term for other scenarios.
Comparing it with the colored curves representing non-Gaussian scenarios, we observe that the presence of primordial non-Gaussianity typically leads to a significant enhancement in the spectral amplitude of
C_{\ell}(\nu)
(
\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)
).
Specifically, unless there is a balance between the contributions of the initial inhomogeneities terms and the propagation term across specific frequency bands, the anisotropies in scalar-induced gravitational waves are usually more prominent in non-Gaussian scenarios compared to Gaussian scenarios, regardless of the signs of the primordial non-Gaussianity parameters.
In the scenarios depicted in the figure, when either
G_{\mathrm{NL}}<0
or
F_{\mathrm{NL}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}<0
, the magnitudes of the (reduced) angular power spectra in non-Gaussian scenarios are lower than in the Gaussian scenario across specific frequency bands.
The angular power spectra
\ell(\ell+1)C_{\ell}(\nu)
exhibit characteristic dips in the profiles of these scenarios.
Moreover, around the pivot frequency, the black solid curve in the left panel of
Fig.

12
reveals that
\ell(\ell+1)\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)
in the Gaussian case displays a blue-tilted shape.
In contrast, the colored curves and Eq. (
65
) demonstrate a different slope for
\ell(\ell+1)\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)
in non-Gaussian scenarios, as long as the primordial non-Gaussianity is not negligible.
This finding suggests that the presence of primordial non-Gaussianity can be determined by measuring the anisotropies in the scalar-induced gravitational wave background.
Additionally, comparing the right panel of
Fig.

12
with
Fig.

8
, it is noteworthy that large magnitudes of
h^{2}\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\nu)
do not necessarily result in large magnitudes of
\ell(\ell+1)C_{\ell}(\nu)
.
For instance, in the case of
(|F_{\mathrm{NL}}|=5,G_{\mathrm{NL}}=H_{\mathrm{NL}}=0)
, the angular power spectrum is significantly enhanced, surpassing the magnitudes observed in the cases of
(F_{\mathrm{NL}}=5,G_{\mathrm{NL}}=25,H_{\mathrm{NL}}=125)
and
(F_{\mathrm{NL}}=-5,G_{\mathrm{NL}}=25,H_{\mathrm{NL}}=-125)
.
This property may prove useful for determining model parameters and breaking parameter degeneracies, as shown in
Fig.

10
.
The sign degeneracy between
F_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
is disrupted by the second term in the square brackets of Eq. (
62
), because it is independent of the presence of primordial non-Gaussianity.
In other words, if the primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneity dominates and the other effects are negligible, the reduced angular power spectra for different signs of
F_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
remain similar and nearly flat.

at the frequency

and

.

F_{\mathrm{NL}}\sqrt{A_{\mathrm{S}}}
across the three rows of contour plots as

across the four columns as

from left to right.
One can identify areas that satisfy the perturbativity conditions from the regions demarcated by dotted and dashed lines in each contour plot, with the conventions for these lines aligning with those in
