), the large-scale modulation at
\mathcal{O}(\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}^{3})
,
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}
, is given by

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}(q)=\sum_{c=0}^{4}\sum_{b=0}^{\lfloor 4-c\rfloor}\sum_{a=0}^{\lfloor 4-b-c\rfloor}\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(a,b,c)}(q)\Bigl(\mathscr{T}_{1}^{(3)}+\mathscr{T}_{2}^{(3)}+\mathscr{T}_{3}^{(3)}+\mathscr{T}_{4}^{(3)}\Bigr)\ ,

(87a)

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{1}^{(3)}=\sum_{l=1}^{3}\sum_{n=1}^{3}\sum_{i=1}^{o-l}\left(\frac{(i+l)!}{i!}\frac{V_{l}^{[i+l]}}{V_{0}^{[i]}}\right)^{n}\binom{N_{i}}{n}\delta_{ln,3}\ ,

(87b)

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{2}^{(3)}=\sum_{i=1}^{o-2}\left(\frac{(i+2)!}{i!}\frac{V_{2}^{[i+2]}}{V_{0}^{[i]}}\right)^{1}\binom{N_{i}}{1}\left(\frac{(i+1)!}{i!}\frac{V_{1}^{[i+1]}}{V_{0}^{[i]}}\right)^{1}\binom{N_{i}-1}{1}\ ,

(87c)

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{3}^{(3)}=\sum_{l_{1}=1}^{2}\sum_{l_{2}=1}^{2}\sum_{n_{1}=1}^{2}\sum_{n_{2}=1}^{2}\sum_{i=2}^{o-l_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{\min(i-1,o-l_{2})}\left(\frac{(i+l_{1})!}{i!}\frac{V_{l_{1}}^{[i+l_{1}]}}{V_{0}^{[i]}}\right)^{n_{1}}\binom{N_{i}}{n_{1}}

(87d)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathscr{T}_3^{(3)} = \sum_{l_1=1}^2 \sum_{l_2=1}^2 \sum_{n_1=1}^2 \sum_{n_2=1}^2 \sum_{i=2}^{o-l_1} \sum_{j=1}^{\min(i-1,o-l_2)}}\times\left(\frac{(j+l_{2})!}{j!}\frac{V_{l_{2}}^{[j+l_{2}]}}{V_{0}^{[j]}}\right)^{n_{2}}\binom{N_{j}}{n_{2}}\delta_{l_{1}n_{1}+l_{2}n_{2},3}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{4}^{(3)}=\sum_{i=3}^{o-1}\sum_{j=2}^{\min(i-1,o-1)}\sum_{k=1}^{\min(j-1,o-1)}\left(\frac{(i+1)!}{i!}\frac{V_{1}^{[i+1]}}{V_{0}^{[i]}}\right)^{1}\binom{N_{i}}{1}\left(\frac{(j+1)!}{j!}\frac{V_{1}^{[j+1]}}{V_{0}^{[j]}}\right)^{1}\binom{N_{j}}{1}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathscr{T}_4^{(3)} = \sum_{i=3}^{o-1} \sum_{j=2}^{\min(i-1,o-1)} \sum_{k=1}^{\min(j-1,o-1)}}\times\left(\frac{(k+1)!}{k!}\frac{V_{1}^{[k+1]}}{V_{0}^{[k]}}\right)^{1}\binom{N_{k}}{1}\ .

(87e)

Similar to Eq. (
63
), this formulation is readily extensible to include higher-order primordial non-Gaussianity.
In particular, for scale-independent primordial non-Gaussianity parameters, we have

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8F_{\mathrm{NL}}^{3}\Bigl(4\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,0,0)}+\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,1,0)}\Bigr)+12F_{\mathrm{NL}}G_{\mathrm{NL}}\Bigl(12\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,0,0)}+4\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,0,0)}+6\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,1,0)}+2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,2,0)}

(88)

\displaystyle\quad+3\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1,0,1)}+2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,0,2)}\Bigr)+48H_{\mathrm{NL}}\Bigl(2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,0,0)}+3\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,0,0)}+3\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,1,0)}+3\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,1,0)}

\displaystyle\quad+3\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,2,0)}+2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1,0,1)}+2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,3,0)}+2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1,1,1)}+\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,0,2)}+\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,1,2)}\Bigr)

\displaystyle+\frac{18G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}}{F_{\mathrm{NL}}}\Bigl(6\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,0,0)}+3\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,1,0)}+2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1,0,1)}+\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1,1,1)}\Bigr)

\displaystyle+\frac{36G_{\mathrm{NL}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}}{F_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}}\Bigl(2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,0,0)}+12\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(4,0,0)}+3\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,1,0)}+4\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,2,0)}+6\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(3,0,1)}+2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,0,2)}\Bigr)

\displaystyle+\frac{27G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{3}}{F_{\mathrm{NL}}^{3}}\Bigl(4\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(4,0,0)}+\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(3,0,1)}\Bigr)+\frac{48H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}}{F_{\mathrm{NL}}G_{\mathrm{NL}}}\Bigl(2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,1,0)}+6\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2,2,0)}+\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1,1,1)}+3\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1,2,1)}\Bigr)

\displaystyle+\frac{16F_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}H_{\mathrm{NL}}}{G_{\mathrm{NL}}}\Bigl(3\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,1,0)}+2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,2,0)}\Bigr)+\frac{32F_{\mathrm{NL}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}}{G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}}\Bigl(2\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,2,0)}+3\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,3,0)}\Bigr)

\displaystyle+\frac{64H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{3}}{G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{3}}\Bigl(\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,3,0)}+4\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,4,0)}\Bigr)\ .

Substituting the expression for
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}
along with Eqs. (
4.1
,
75
) into Eqs. (
84
,
85
), we derive the explicit formula for the reduced angular trispectrum
t^{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}_{\ell_{3}\ell_{4}}(L,\nu)
presented in Eq. (
83
).
We set aside the discussion of the multipole dependence of
t^{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}_{\ell_{3}\ell_{4}}(L,\nu)
, which exhibits a behavior similar to that of the cosmic microwave background in the low-multipole regime, as previously reported in Ref.
Li et al. (2024a)
.
Instead, the following analysis focuses on its frequency dependence, as described by Eqs. (
84
,
85
).

Figure 19
:
Frequency dependence of the reduced angular trispectrum of SIGWs, with the upper and lower panels displaying numerical results on linear and logarithmic vertical scales, respectively.
The dotted lines in the lower left panels indicate negative sign regions.

As done in the analysis of the angular bispectrum, we analyze the frequency dependence of the reduced angular trispectrum across various scenarios characterized by different primordial non-Gaussianity parameters in a similar manner.
First, for Gaussian
\zeta
, it is evident that
t_{1}(\nu)=t_{2}(\nu)=0
, as anticipated, indicating that a Gaussian
\zeta
yields a zero angular trispectrum of scalar-induced gravitational waves.
Second, in scenarios with minimal primordial non-Gaussianity, where the Gaussian component
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,0,0)}
is dominant among the 19 categories of
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
in Eq. (
32
), we consider only the leading-order terms of
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(2)}
and
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}
.
The former has been discussed in the analysis of
b(\nu)
in the previous subsection, while the latter yields
16(2F_{\mathrm{NL}}^{3}+9F_{\mathrm{NL}}G_{\mathrm{NL}}+6H_{\mathrm{NL}})\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(0,0,0)}
.
According to Eq. (
86
),
t_{1}(\nu)
is determined by
\ell(\ell+1)\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)
and
b(\nu)
, which are described by Eqs. (
65
,
76
) for these scenarios.
Therefore, we primarily focus on the behavior of
t_{2}(\nu)
, whose approximate expression reads

\displaystyle t_{2}(\nu)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\bigl(2\pi\bigr)^{2}A_{\mathrm{L}}^{3}\biggl[8F_{\mathrm{NL}}+\frac{3}{5}\bigl(6-n_{\mathrm{gw}}(\nu)\bigr)\biggr]^{3}

(89)

\displaystyle\times\biggl[16(2F_{\mathrm{NL}}^{3}+9F_{\mathrm{NL}}G_{\mathrm{NL}}+6H_{\mathrm{NL}})+\frac{3}{5}G_{\mathrm{NL}}\bigl(6-n_{\mathrm{gw}}(\nu)\bigr)\biggr]\ .

Under this approximation,
t_{2}(\nu)
exhibits various characteristics for different magnitudes of the primordial non-Gaussianity parameters.
As discussed below Eq. (
65
),
\bigl(6-n_{\mathrm{gw}}(\nu)\bigr)
ranges from
3
to
15
as the frequency varies from
10^{-4}\nu_{\ast}
to
10^{2}\nu_{\ast}
.
Similar to
b(\nu)
and
\ell(\ell+1)\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)
,
t_{2}(\nu)
can be used to disrupt the sign degeneracy between
F_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
when
|F_{\mathrm{NL}}|
is not significantly larger than
\bigl(6-n_{\mathrm{gw}}(\nu)\bigr)
.
The expressions of Eqs. (
65
,
76
,
89
) demonstrate that the primordial non-Gaussianity parameter,
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
, can only be discerned through the angular trispectrum in this approximation.
In an extreme case, if the second terms in the square brackets dominate over the first term,
t_{2}(\nu)
is found to be nearly proportional to
G_{\mathrm{NL}}\bigl[\ell(\ell+1)\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)\bigr]^{2}
.
However, this condition is challenging to meet for an observable angular trispectrum since the contributions from the primordial non-Gaussianity parameters in the first term of the second square brackets are significantly enhanced by the constant coefficients at
\mathcal{O}(10^{2})
order, implying the second term dominates only when primordial non-Gaussianity is very small or when the primordial non-Gaussianity parameters cancel each other out.
From Eq. (
65
) to Eq. (
89
)), the first terms in the square brackets, which represent the primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneity, become increasingly significant, suggesting that the angular trispectrum may improve the accuracy of the inferred model parameters.
Last but not least, we illustrate several instances of substantial primordial non-Gaussianity with numerical results depicted in
Fig.

19
.
The panels on the upper and lower sides of this figure demonstrate the same trispectra with model parameters identical to those in
Fig.

12
, where the vertical scales are linear (upper) and logarithmic (lower), respectively.
The convention of the dotted lines in the lower right panel is also the same as in
Fig.

16
, indicating where the sign is negative.
Although
t_{1}(\nu)
is determined by
\ell(\ell+1)\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)
and
b(\nu)
, the distinct behaviors of
t_{2}(\nu)
suggest that the analysis of the angular trispectrum would be useful for a deeper investigation of scalar-induced gravitational waves.
Comparing the left and right panels, it is apparent that the magnitude of
t_{1}(\nu)
is usually larger than that of
t_{2}(\nu)
.
However, given that the contributions from
t_{1}(\nu)
and
t_{2}(\nu)
to
t^{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}_{\ell_{3}\ell_{4}}(L,\nu)
are modulated by different multipoles, the distinct behaviors between
t_{1}(\nu)
and
t_{2}(\nu)
indicate that one might distinguish them using various sets of multipoles.
Thus, we can acquire a wealth of information from the reduced angular trispectrum, which will serve as an important complement to the information obtained from the energy-density fraction spectrum, reduced angular power spectrum, and angular bispectrum.

Figure 20
:
Reduced angular trispectrum of SIGWs with respect to the
F_{\mathrm{NL}}
,
G_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
. The conventions for the dotted and dashed lines are the same as those in
Fig.

13
.

We also present two arrays of contour plots for
t_{1}(\nu)
and
|t_{2}(\nu)|
in
Fig.

20
, arranged identically to that of
b(\nu)
.
By combining these two arrays with Eqs. (
83
,
84
,
85
), we can easily discern the dependence of the scalar-induced gravitational wave angular trispectrum on
G_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
.
The magnitudes of both
t_{1}(\nu)
and
|t_{2}(\nu)|
decrease as
A_{\mathrm{S}}
increases.
While the contour plots of
t_{1}(\nu)
are similar to those of
|b(\nu)|
except for differences in magnitudes and signs, the contour plots of
|t_{2}(\nu)|
exhibit distinct features.
Notably, an irregular region located at the center, biased toward
G_{\mathrm{NL}}<0
and
F_{\mathrm{NL}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}<0
, indicates where the sign of
t_{2}(\nu)
is negative.
This region extends a considerable distance into the
G_{\mathrm{NL}}>0
domain, suggesting that when
F_{\mathrm{NL}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}
is negative and small,
t_{2}(\nu)
remains negative regardless of the magnitude of the positive
G_{\mathrm{NL}}
.
This implies that
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}/\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
typically dominates over
3G_{\mathrm{NL}}\bigl(6-n_{\mathrm{gw}}(\nu)\bigr)/5
at this parameter space.
The comparison of the contour plots of
t_{2}(\nu)
with those of
t_{1}(\nu)
demonstrates that
t_{2}(\nu)
is generally an order of magnitude smaller than
t_{1}(\nu)
, particularly when primordial non-Gaussianity is significant.
Since the multipoles are constrained by the triangular inequalities, the reduced angular trispectrum expressed by Eq. (
82
) is unlikely to be negative, despite the possible negative values of
t_{2}(\nu)
at certain frequencies.
The differing dependencies on frequency, multipoles, and model parameters imply that the reduced angular trispectrum contains a wealth of information about the early Universe.

6
Extended Study

We have analyzed the statistics of the scalar-induced gravitational wave background, considering the presence of primordial non-Gaussianity up to
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
order in the aforementioned study.
This analysis encompasses the energy density fraction spectrum detailed in Section
3
, the angular power spectrum outlined in Section
4
, and the angular bispectrum and trispectrum discussed in Section
5
.
In our analyses, we employed a diagrammatic approach and neglected the scale dependence of primordial non-Gaussianity for convenience.
This section aims to extend and supplement the investigation by focusing on three key aspects.
First, we will review the similar infrared scaling across all categories of the scalar-induced gravitational wave energy-density spectrum, which results in the general behaviors of the energy density fraction spectrum and angular correlation functions.
Next, we will elaborate on the impacts of the primordial trispectrum on the scalar-induced gravitational wave background in the first subsection, which serves as an important supplement to the preceding investigation.
Then, the following subsection will demonstrate how to extrapolate our study to address the scale dependence of primordial non-Gaussianity.
Finally, in the last subsection, a simplified example will illustrate the relationship between primordial black hole formation and the accompanying scalar-induced gravitational wave background in the context of primordial non-Gaussianity.

6.1
Infrared scaling of the SIGW energy-density spectra

Figure 21
:
The spectral index for
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(a,b,c)}
.

The generic infrared behavior of the scalar-induced gravitational wave energy density spectrum has attracted considerable interest
Yuan et al. (2020)
;
Yuan et al. (2023)
;
Cai et al. (2019a)
;
Cai et al. (2020)
;
Adshead et al. (2021b)
;
Luo et al. (2025)
;
Han et al. (2025)
.
As illustrated in
Fig.

7
, the energy density spectra across all categories exhibit a common logarithmic slope in the infrared region.
Specifically, this shared feature can be characterized by

\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(a,b,c)}(q)\Big|_{\mathrm{IR}}\propto\left(\frac{q}{q_{\ast}}\right)^{3}\ln^{2}\left(\frac{4q_{\ast}^{2}}{3q^{2}}\right)\ ,

(90)

where the subscript
\mathrm{IR}
denotes the infrared region.
Furthermore, akin to the spectral index for the energy density fraction spectrum
\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}
defined in Eqs. (
25
), we can introduce the spectral index for each category
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(a,b,c)}
, as given by

n_{\mathrm{gw}}^{(a,b,c)}(q)\Big|_{\mathrm{IR}}=\frac{\partial\ln\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(a,b,c)}(q)\Big|_{\mathrm{IR}}}{\partial\ln q}=3-\frac{4}{\ln^{2}\left(\frac{4q_{\ast}^{2}}{3q^{2}}\right)}\ .

(91)

The detailed verification of Eqs. (
90
,
91
) is provided in the Appendix
E
.
Naturally, the total energy density fraction spectrum,
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
, which is the sum of
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(a,b,c)}
for all categories in Eq. (
32
), exhibits the same logarithmic slope as that of Eq. (
91
).
In particular, in the extreme infrared region, we find that
n_{\mathrm{gw}}(q\rightarrow 0)=3
.
Traditionally, this suggests that the source term of scalar-induced gravitational waves,
S_{\lambda}(\eta,q)
in Eq. (
135
), exhibits a scale-invariant power spectrum for super-horizon scales, which corresponds to white noise due to the absence of causal correlations
Maggiore (2018)
.
This feature is believed to generally exist in gravitational waves sourced from cosmological mechanisms
Maggiore (2018)
;
Cai et al. (2020)
.

This general infrared feature is crucial for investigating the infrared behaviors of the anisotropies in the scalar-induced gravitational wave background.
According to the analyses in
Section

4
and
Section

5
, the large-scale modulations,
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}
,
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(2)}
, and
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}
, can be expressed as linear combinations of the 19 categories
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(a,b,c)}
.
Therefore, in the low-frequency limit, the common feature across all categories described by Eqs. (
90
,
91
) indicates that the PNG-induced inhomogeneities
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}/\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
,
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(2)}/\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
, and
\omega_{\mathrm{ng},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}/\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
are independent of
q
.
Since
6-n_{\mathrm{gw}}(q\rightarrow 0)=3
is also a constant, the reduced angular power spectrum expressed by Eq. (
62
), the angular bispectrum in Eqs. (
72
,
73
), and the reduced angular trispectrum in Eqs. (
83
,
84
,
85
) are nearly scale-invariant in the infrared region, regardless of the magnitude of primordial non-Gaussianity.
Different scale-independent primordial non-Gaussianity parameters only alter the magnitude of the angular correlation functions but do not affect the features of the spectra.
In other words, these characteristics may be beneficial for reducing noise in future gravitational wave detection.

6.2
Impacts of primordial trispectrum on the SIGW background

In Section
2
and Section
3
, we emphasized the significance of the four-point correlator of the primordial curvature perturbations
\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}
in calculating the energy-density fraction spectrum of scalar-induced gravitational waves in the presence of primordial non-Gaussianity.
Based on Eqs. (
7
–
9
), where
\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}
is expanded in a power series of its Gaussian component
\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}
, we employ the diagrammatic approach to compute this correlator using Wick’s theorem.
The contraction terms are further decomposed into nine families in Subsection
3.1
.
Alternatively, as emphasized in Refs.
Adshead et al. (2021b)
;
Garcia-Saenz et al. (2023)
;
Gammal and others (2025)
, it is also conventional to separate this four-point correlator into the disconnected piece and the connected piece, which are expressed in terms of the primordial power spectrum and trispectrum of
\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}
, respectively.
In comparison to the nine families introduced in Subsection
3.1
, the disconnected piece corresponds to the
G
-like family, while the connected piece encompasses all other families.
In other words, we utilize
\mathcal{P}^{C-\mathrm{like}}
,
\mathcal{P}^{Z-\mathrm{like}}
, etc., to represent the contribution of the primordial trispectrum to the energy-density fraction spectrum of scalar-induced gravitational waves in Section
3
.
Traditionally, the primordial trispectrum of
\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}
is expressed in terms of
g_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
\tau_{\mathrm{NL}}
, differing from the form we use.
Thus, in this subsection, we first compare the primordial trispectrum represented by
\mathcal{P}^{X-\mathrm{like}}
(
X=C,Z,P,N,CZ,PZ,NC
) with that expressed in terms of
g_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
\tau_{\mathrm{NL}}
, specifically focusing on their respective contributions to the energy-density full spectrum
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
.
Furthermore, we discuss the pure impacts of the primordial trispectrum on the scalar-induced gravitational wave background.

As is customarily done, the primordial trispectrum for local-type primordial non-Gaussianity is expressed in the form outlined by
Okamoto and Hu (2002)
;
Boubekeur and Lyth (2006)
;
Suyama and Yamaguchi (2008)
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(\mathbf{k}_{1},\mathbf{k}_{2},\mathbf{k}_{3},\mathbf{k}_{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau_{\mathrm{NL}}\left(P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(|\mathbf{k}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{3}|)P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(k_{3})P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(k_{4})+11\mathrm{perms}\right)

(92)

\displaystyle+\frac{54g_{\mathrm{NL}}}{25}\left(P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(k_{2})P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(k_{3})P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(k_{4})+3\mathrm{perms}\right)\ ,

where
\tau_{\mathrm{NL}}
satisfies the condition
\tau_{\mathrm{NL}}\geq\left(6f_{\mathrm{NL}}/5\right)^{2}

Suyama and Yamaguchi (2008)
;
Huang (2009)
.
This condition, derived from the
\delta N
formalism
Sasaki and Stewart (1996)
;
Sasaki and Tanaka (1998)
;
Lyth et al. (2005)
;
Lyth and Rodriguez (2005)
, holds as long as the primordial non-Gaussianity arises from super-horizon evolution.
The equality in this condition holds for single-field inflation models and certain special multi-field inflation models
Byrnes et al. (2006)
;
Wands (2010)
.
In fact, the power series expansion of
\zeta
in terms of
\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}
, as presented in Eqs. (
7
,
8
), is a direct consequence of the
\delta N
formalism for single-field and specific multi-field inflation models
Byrnes et al. (2006)
;
Wands (2010)
.
Therefore, we can regard
\tau_{\mathrm{NL}}=\left(6f_{\mathrm{NL}}/5\right)^{2}
in the following discussion.
According to Eqs. (
7
-
10
), the total power spectrum of
\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}
, denoted as
P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}
in Eq. (
92
), can be expressed as follows

P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(q)=(1+3A_{\mathrm{S}}G_{\mathrm{NL}})^{2}P^{[1]}(q)+2(F_{\mathrm{NL}}+6A_{\mathrm{S}}H_{\mathrm{NL}})^{2}P^{[2]}(q)+6G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}P^{[3]}(q)+24H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}P^{[4]}(q)\ .

(93)

Similar to Eq. (
10
), we can define its dimensionless form as follows

\Delta_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}^{2}(q)=\frac{q^{3}}{2\pi^{2}}P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(q)\ .

(94)

Notably, the dimensionless power spectrum of
\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}
, denoted as
\Delta_{\mathrm{S}}^{2}(q)
in Eqs. (
10
,
12
), corresponds exactly to the tree-level of
\Delta_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}^{2}(q)
, just as
P^{[1]}(q)
is the tree-level of
P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}
.
In scenarios where
A_{\mathrm{S}}
, representing the spectral amplitude of
\Delta_{\mathrm{S}}^{2}(q)
, is sufficiently small,
P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}
can be approximated as the tree-level, i.e.,
P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(q)\simeq P^{[1]}(q)
.
Ref.
Garcia-Saenz et al. (2023)
argued that only the effects of the primordial trispectrum on the scalar-induced gravitational wave background can be attributed to primordial non-Gaussianity, since only the total primordial power spectrum is observable, while its tree-level contribution remains unobservable.
Although the loop corrections in the power spectrum result from primordial non-Gaussianity, we will discuss the impacts of the primordial trispectrum on the scalar-induced gravitational wave background in the following paragraphs.

We are particularly interested in the contribution of the primordial trispectrum to the energy-density full spectrum
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
of scalar-induced gravitational waves.
Derived from Eqs. (
4
,
6
,
15
), this contribution can be expressed as
\mathcal{T}_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(\mathbf{q}_{1},\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{1},-\mathbf{q}_{2},-\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{q}_{2})
in the integrand for
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
, where the terms involving
g_{\mathrm{NL}}
in Eq. (
92
) vanish.
The remaining terms that are proportional to
\tau_{\mathrm{NL}}
can be divided into two components, written in the form of

\tilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(\mathbf{q}_{1},\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{1},-\mathbf{q}_{2},-\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{q}_{2})=P^{C}+P^{Z}\ ,

(95)

where
P^{C}
and
P^{Z}
are defined as

\displaystyle P^{C}=4\tau_{\mathrm{NL}}P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(q_{2})P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{2}|)P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(|\mathbf{q}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{2}|)\ ,

(96)

\displaystyle P^{Z}=4\tau_{\mathrm{NL}}P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(q_{1})P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{2}|)P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(|\mathbf{q}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{2}|)\ .

(97)

These two components remind us
P^{C-\mathrm{like}}
defined in Eq. (
42
) and
P^{Z-\mathrm{like}}
defined in Eq. (
44
).
Furthermore, by substituting
\tau_{\mathrm{NL}}=\left(6f_{\mathrm{NL}}/5\right)^{2}
into Eqs. (
96
), we compare Eqs. (
43
,
45
) with Eqs. (
96
) and find that the leading-order terms are identical.
Specifically, these leading-order contributions correspond to the
C
- and
Z
-diagrams shown in
Fig.

27
and
Fig.

28
, respectively.
However, the contributions of higher orders in
A_{\mathrm{S}}
differ.
From this perspective, Eq. (
95
) appears to be an approximation of the connected piece of the four-point correlator
\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{1})\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}(\mathbf{q}_{1})\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{2})\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}(\mathbf{q}_{2})\rangle
, which is accurate at leading order but not at higher orders.

We thus utilize the sum of
\mathcal{P}^{X-\mathrm{like}}
(
X=C,Z,P,N,CZ,PZ,NC
) to represent the impacts of the primordial trispectrum on the scalar-induced gravitational wave background.
To explore these impacts without the influence of the disconnected component of the four-point correlator of
\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}
, it is essential to reconstruct
\mathcal{P}^{G-\mathrm{like}}
to ensure equivalence across various sets of primordial non-Gaussianity parameters.
Notably,
\mathcal{P}^{G-\mathrm{like}}
, as expressed by Eq. (
41
), can be reformulated into

\mathcal{P}^{G-\mathrm{like}}=2P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(q_{1})P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{1}|)\ .

(98)

This expression indicates that the disconnected component characterizes the Gaussian statistics and loop corrections resulting from primordial non-Gaussianity, while being irrelevant to the non-Gaussian statistics.
This inspires us to construct the same
\mathcal{P}^{G-\mathrm{like}}
by eliminating the influence of loop corrections on
P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(q)
.
In other words, we should construct identical
P_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(q)
, or equivalently
\Delta_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}^{2}(q)
, for different sets of primordial non-Gaussianity parameters to investigate the pure impacts of the primordial trispectrum on the scalar-induced gravitational wave background.

Figure 22
:
Dimensionless power spectra of
\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}
for various primordial non-Gaussianity parameters.

Figure 23
:
Imprints of the primordial trispectrum on the SIGW background, with
\Delta_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}^{2}(q)
adjusted to nearly overlap in the upper left panel.

Here, we illustrate the impacts of the primordial trispectrum on the scalar-induced gravitational wave background through figures derived from the same
\Delta_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}^{2}(q)
.
The previous numerical results, including
Fig.

6
,
Fig.

8
,
Fig.

12
,
Fig.

16
, and
Fig.

19
, are based on a consistent
\Delta_{\mathrm{S}}^{2}(q)
across the same sets of primordial non-Gaussianity parameters.
However, the corresponding dimensionless power spectra of
\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}
, denoted as
\Delta_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}^{2}(q)
, differ across various non-Gaussian scenarios.
As illustrated in
Fig.

22
, the primary differences among these
\Delta_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}^{2}(q)
are found in their magnitudes and peak frequencies.
Therefore, we adjust
A_{\mathrm{S}}
and
\nu_{\ast}
for each
\Delta_{\mathrm{S}}^{2}(q)
to ensure that the spectral amplitudes and peak frequencies of
\Delta_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}^{2}(q)
are consistent across all sets of primordial non-Gaussianity parameters.
These modified power spectra are depicted in the upper left panel of
Fig.

23
, demonstrating that all the
\Delta_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}^{2}(q)
nearly overlap while exhibiting slight differences in their slopes.
In comparison to the black curve, which signifies the Gaussian scenario
\Delta_{\mathrm{S}}(q)
, the non-Gaussian scenarios depicted by colored curves are skewed due to the convolutions introduced in Eq. (
35
).
Despite these discrepancies, it is evident that the power spectra
\Delta_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}^{2}(q)
can be regarded as nearly identical in the vicinity of the peak frequency (
10^{4}\mathrm{Hz}-5\times 10^{-3}\mathrm{Hz}
).
By utilizing these modified
\Delta_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}^{2}(q)
, we recompute the corresponding
h^{2}\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\nu)
,
\ell(\ell+1)\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)
,
b(\nu)
,
t_{1}(\nu)
, and
t_{2}(\nu)
for the respective non-Gaussian scenarios.
All of these are additionally presented in the five other panels of
Fig.

23
.
In each panel, the comparison between the Gaussian scenario, represented by the black curve, and the non-Gaussian scenarios illustrates the contributions of
T_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}
to the corresponding spectra of scalar-induced gravitational waves for various primordial non-Gaussianity.
For the energy-density fraction spectra
h^{2}\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\nu)
, the upper right panel reveals that they remain largely unchanged by the primordial trispectrum in most scenarios, except when both
G_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
F_{\mathrm{NL}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}
are negative.
In these cases,
h^{2}\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\nu)
is significantly enhanced due to an increased
A_{\mathrm{S}}
, but is instead suppressed in
Fig.

8
when
A_{\mathrm{S}}
is held constant.
Consequently, the contribution of
T_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}
to the isotropic component of the scalar-induced gravitational wave background is not always negligible, potentially augmenting the magnitude of
h^{2}\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\nu)
for significant negative primordial non-Gaussianity.
Among the angular spectra, the varying behaviors across different primordial non-Gaussianity suggest that
T_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}
exerts notable effects on the anisotropies and non-Gaussianity in the scalar-induced gravitational wave background.
Thus, detecting the statistical properties of the scalar-induced gravitational wave background holds promise as a probe of the primordial trispectrum in the future.
Additionally, a common feature emerges: a dip appears around the peak frequency of
\Delta_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}^{2}(q)
in scenarios characterized by negative
G_{\mathrm{NL}}
and non-negative
F_{\mathrm{NL}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}
.
Based on the analyses of Eqs. (
62
,
73
,
84
,
85
), this feature suggests that
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}(q)/\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)
cancels
3(6-n_{\mathrm{gw}}(\nu))/5
in these scenarios.

6.3
Effects of scale-dependent PNG on the SIGW background

In this study, we have proposed that all primordial non-Gaussianity parameters be scale-independent for the sake of simplicity.
Despite this assumption, the framework can be readily extended to accommodate a scale-dependent case.
In this subsection, we aim to provide a brief discussion of this generalization concerning the analyses of the imprints of primordial non-Gaussianity on the scalar-induced gravitational wave background.

In the most general scenario, we reformulate Eq. (
8
) in the Fourier modes as follows

\displaystyle\zeta(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{q})+\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}F_{\mathrm{NL}}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{k})\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{k})\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{k})

(99)

\displaystyle+\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}G_{\mathrm{NL}}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{p},\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{k})\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{p})\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{p})\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle+\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{p}\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{l}}{(2\pi)^{9/2}}H_{\mathrm{NL}}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{l},\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{l},\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{k})\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{l})\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{l})\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{p})\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{k})\ ,

where the primordial non-Gaussianity parameters are no longer constants, but rather functions of the wave vectors.
Therefore, when substituting this formula into Eq. (
6
) for the subsequent calculation of the energy-density full spectrum of scalar-induced gravitational waves, these primordial non-Gaussianity parameters cannot be extracted from the integrands.
The incorporation of these scale-dependent primordial non-Gaussianity parameters into the integrands will complicate the integrals.
Despite this increased complexity, the diagrammatic approach remains partially applicable in this scenario.
We can still evaluate the symmetric factors for the diagrams of
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)
using the Feynman-like rules in
Fig.

26
and the “renormalized” Feynman-like rules in
Fig.

4
, which is the primary objective of the diagrammatic approach.
Specifically, the Feynman-like diagrams presented in
Fig.

26
-
Fig.

34
and their corresponding symmetric factors remain unchanged in the scale-dependent scenario, allowing us to infer the symmetric factors based on Eqs. (
41
,
43
,
45
,
47
,
49
,
51
,
53
,
55
,
57
).
However, the specific rules for the vertices and propagators must be modified.
Minor adjustments should be made to the Feynman-like diagram rules in
Fig.

26
, as the vertices are modified to be scale-dependent while the propagators remain unchanged.
For the “renormalized” Feynman-like rules in
Fig.

4
, the loop integrals in Eqs. (
35
,
37
) are no longer valid, since the scale-dependent primordial non-Gaussianity parameters incorporated into the integrands involve other undetermined momenta in addition to the loop momentum.
In this case, the convolved propagators defined in Eq. (
36
) and the “renormalized” vertices described in Eqs. (
38
) can be regarded as operators, necessitating that all involved integrations be carried out ultimately.
Thus, the “renormalized” Feynman-like rules can only be utilized to simplify the evaluation of the symmetric factors, but are not helpful for the integral calculations of each diagram of
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(q)
.
One could reformulate the integrals according to the modified Feynman-like rules in
Fig.

1
.
Nevertheless, our method for calculating the large-scale modulations in Eqs. (
63
,
74
,
87
) also proves ineffective, making the calculation of the multi-point correlations of
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
significantly more complicated.
In this case, by constructing all the diagrams of the primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneities based on the modified Feynman-like rules and
Fig.

3
, it becomes necessary to compute the large-scale modulations for each diagram individually, rather than leveraging the entire categories of
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
in this work.
As a result, we have to perform thousands of integrals for primordial non-Gaussianity up to quartic approximation.
Even more challenging, since the loop integrals cannot be computed in advance, the expressions involve higher-dimensional integrals, presenting obstacles for accurate numerical calculations.

To address these difficulties, a compromise between scale-independent and scale-dependent primordial non-Gaussianity may be viable.
As discussed in Section
2
, we decompose the scalar perturbations into short- and long-wavelength modes in Eq. (
9
), where the short-wavelength mode is responsible for the production of scalar-induced gravitational waves, while the long-wavelength mode modulates the distribution of their energy density on the cosmic microwave background scale.
These two scales are distinctly different, while each scale region is relatively small in contrast.
Thus, it is plausible to postulate that the primordial non-Gaussianity parameters vary slowly within each scale region, allowing them to be regarded as graded across different scale regions
Yu and Wang (2024)
.
The Fourier mode of Eq. (
8
) is revised to be

\displaystyle\zeta(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{q})+F_{\mathrm{NL}}^{(0)}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}(\mathbf{k})\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{k})

(100)

\displaystyle+2F_{\mathrm{NL}}^{(1)}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{k})\zeta_{\mathrm{gS}}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{k})+F_{\mathrm{NL}}^{(2)}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{k})\zeta_{\mathrm{gL}}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{k})+\cdots\ .

Similarly, we introduce
G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{(1)}
,
G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{(2)}
,
G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{(3)}
,
H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{(1)}
,
H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{(2)}
,
H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{(3)}
,
H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{(4)}
, and so forth for the couplings of the different wavelength modes, while the primordial non-Gaussianity parameters representing the couplings among the short-wavelength modes are denoted as
G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{(0)}
and
H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{(0)}
.
The vertices in
Fig.

1
denote
V^{[2]}_{j}=F_{\mathrm{NL}}^{(j)}
,
V^{[3]}_{j}=G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{(j)}
, and
V^{[4]}_{j}=H_{\mathrm{NL}}^{(j)}
in this context.
In particular, the
Planck
2018 results provided the latest constraints on local-type primordial non-Gaussianity, specifically
f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CMB}}=-0.9\pm 5.1
and
g_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CMB}}=(-5.8\pm 6.5)\times 10^{4}
at a
68\%
confidence level
Akrami and others (2020)
, which correspond to
F_{\mathrm{NL}}^{(2)}=-0.54\pm 3
and
G_{\mathrm{NL}}^{(3)}=(-2.1\pm 2.3)\times 10^{4}
.
Moreover, the consistency relation for single-field inflation indicates that
F_{\mathrm{NL}}^{(1)}=3(n_{s}-1)/5
, where
n_{s}
is the power index of
\Delta^{2}_{\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}}(k)

Creminelli and Zaldarriaga (2004)
.
In this compromise, our diagrammatic approach, including both the Feynman-like rules in
Fig.

26
and the “renormalized” Feynman-like rules in
Fig.

4
, remains effective.
The energy-density fraction spectrum of scalar-induced gravitational waves remains unchanged compared to the results of this work, while the modified large-scale modulations can be readily obtained via Eqs. (
63
,
74
,
87
).
These modified large-scale modulations may differ from Eqs. (
4.1
,
75
,
88
), because these expressions are applicable to the specific results for the scale-independent case.
Thus, it is anticipated that the angular spectra could be enhanced or suppressed for different primordial non-Gaussianity parameters.
Additionally, as mentioned in Subsection
2.1
, the production of scalar-induced gravitational waves is linked to
\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}
, while the initial inhomogeneities arise from the coupling between short- and long-wavelength modes, as well as from large-scale scalar perturbations.
It appears that only the local-type primordial non-Gaussianity could yield large anisotropies in scalar-induced gravitational waves.
However, primordial non-Gaussianity of other shapes on small scales will alter the primordial trispectrum shown in Eq. (
92
), which may leave significant imprints on the isotropic component of the scalar-induced gravitational wave background.
Further analysis of this topic is reserved for future investigations.

6.4
SIGW Background and PBH Formation

In this study, we focus on the scalar-induced gravitational waves associated with an enhanced power spectrum and the non-Gaussianity of primordial curvature perturbations during the radiation-dominated era, which is linked to the formation of primordial black holes.
In particular, the primordial black holes within mass ranges where the accompanying scalar-induced gravitational waves may be potentially detectable by space-borne detectors
Saito and Yokoyama (2010)
;
Inomata and Nakama (2019)
;
Zhao and Wang (2023)
could constitute some or all of dark matter
Carr and Kuhnel (2020)
;
Carr et al. (2024)
.
Therefore, it is crucial to investigate the relationship between primordial black hole formation and the scalar-induced gravitational wave background while considering the presence of primordial non-Gaussianity.
Future detection of the scalar-induced gravitational wave background will constrain primordial black hole abundance and primordial non-Gaussianity, while the search for primordial black holes can also contribute to studying the statistics of primordial curvature perturbations
Young et al. (2014)
;
Yoo et al. (2018)
;
Yoo et al. (2021)
;
Abe et al. (2023)
;
Pi et al. (2024)
;
Pi (2024)
;
Kalaja et al. (2019)
;
Gammal and others (2025)
;
Pritchard et al. (2025)
;
Zhou et al. (2025)
.
A substantial amount of research
Byrnes et al. (2012)
;
Young and Byrnes (2013)
;
Franciolini et al. (2018)
;
Yoo et al. (2019)
;
Inomata et al. (2021)
;
Nakama et al. (2017)
;
Abe et al. (2023)
;
Perna et al. (2024)
;
Ferrante et al. (2023)
;
Gow et al. (2023a)
;
van Laak and Young (2023)
;
Franciolini et al. (2023)
;
Iovino et al. (2024b)
;
Kitajima et al. (2021)
;
Inui et al. (2025)
;
Young (2022)
;
Franciolini et al. (2024)
has investigated the impact of primordial non-Gaussianity on primordial black hole formation.
Based on these studies, we will provide a brief discussion of this important topic.

To determine whether the cosmological perturbations collapse to form a primordial black hole, we utilize the compaction function
Shibata and Sasaki (1999)
, which can characterize the average density contrast of cosmological fluids over the Hubble horizon.
During the radiation-dominated era, we consider a spherically symmetric region with an areal radius of
R(r,t)=a(t)re^{\zeta(t)}
, where
r
is the radial coordinate.
The compaction function can be expressed as

\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{x},r)=\mathcal{C}_{1}(\mathbf{x},r)-\frac{3}{8}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{2}(\mathbf{x},r)\ ,

(101)

where
\mathcal{C}_{1}(\mathbf{x},r)
is represented as

\mathcal{C}_{1}(\mathbf{x},r)=-\frac{4}{3}r\partial_{r}\zeta(r)=\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{x},r)\frac{\delta\zeta}{\delta\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}}\ ,

(102)

with the Gaussian component defined as
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{x},r)=-4r\partial_{r}\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}(r)/3
.
For local-type primordial non-Gaussianity in the form of Eq. (
8
), the variational derivative of
\zeta
with respect to
\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}
, denoted as
\delta\zeta/\delta\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}
, is given by

\frac{\delta\zeta}{\delta\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}}=1+2F_{\mathrm{NL}}\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}+3G_{\mathrm{NL}}\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}+4H_{\mathrm{NL}}\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}^{3}+\cdots\ .

(103)

We evaluate the compaction function at the time of horizon reentry, i.e.,
R(r_{m},t)\mathcal{H}=1
, where
r_{m}
denotes the scale at which
\mathcal{C}
reaches its maximum value.
As shown in Ref.
Musco et al. (2021)
,
r_{m}
depends on the shape of the power spectrum of
\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}
.
For
\Delta^{2}_{\mathrm{S}}(q)
as assumed in Eq. (
12
), we have
r_{m}k_{\ast}\simeq 1.75
according to Ref.
Musco et al. (2021)
.

When the compaction function exceeds the threshold, a primordial black hole forms from primordial overdensity collapse, with its mass given by
Niemeyer and Jedamzik (1998)
;
Niemeyer and Jedamzik (1999)

M_{\mathrm{PBH}}=\mathcal{K}M_{H}\left(\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{th}}\right)^{\gamma}\ ,

(104)

where
M_{H}
is the mass contained within a Hubble horizon in an unperturbed background, i.e.,

M_{H}\simeq 17M_{\odot}\left(\frac{r_{m}}{10^{-6}{\rm Mpc}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{g_{\ast}}{10.75}\right)^{-1/6}\ ,

(105)

with
M_{\odot}
denoting the solar mass.
For simplicity, we take the prefactor to be
\mathcal{K}\simeq 4
and the critical exponent to be
\gamma\simeq 0.36
.
Since the threshold
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{th}}
is robust against primordial non-Gaussianity
Kehagias et al. (2019)
and depends on the spectral width
\sigma

Musco (2019)
;
Musco et al. (2021)
;
Escrivà et al. (2020)
;
Musco et al. (2024)
, we utilize the result from Ref.
Musco et al. (2021)
, which indicates that
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{th}}\simeq 0.46
for
\sigma=1
.

The mass fraction of primordial black holes at the formation epoch is characterized by
\beta
, expressed as
Ferrante et al. (2023)
;
Gow et al. (2023a)
;
Young (2022)
;
van Laak and Young (2023)
;
Franciolini et al. (2023)
;
Iovino et al. (2024b)
;
Perna et al. (2024)

\beta=\int_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{th}})^{\gamma}\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{g}},\zeta_{\mathrm{g}})\mathrm{d}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{g}}\mathrm{d}\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}\ ,

(106)

where the integration domain is given by
\mathcal{D}=\{\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{g}},\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}\in\mathbb{R};\mathcal{C}>\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{th}}\wedge\mathcal{C}_{1}<\frac{4}{3}\}
, and the probability distribution function reads

\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{g}},\zeta_{\mathrm{g}})=\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2(1-\gamma_{cr}^{2})}\left(\frac{\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{g}}}{\sigma_{c}}-\frac{\gamma_{cr}\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}}{\sigma_{r}}\right)^{2}-\frac{\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}}{2\sigma_{r}^{2}}}}{2\pi\sigma_{c}\sigma_{r}\sqrt{1-\gamma_{cr}^{2}}}\ .

(107)

In this probability distribution function,
\sigma_{c}
and
\sigma_{r}
represent the standard deviations of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{g}}
and
\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}
, respectively, while
\gamma_{cr}=\sigma_{cr}^{2}/(\sigma_{c}\sigma_{r})
denotes their coefficient of association.
Using these three parameters, we can construct the covariance matrix between
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{g}}
and
\zeta_{\mathrm{g}}
.
Considering the corrections from the non-linear radiation transfer function
De Luca et al. (2023)
;
Ferrante et al. (2023)
;
Iovino et al. (2024b)
, we can calculate the corresponding variances as follows

\displaystyle\sigma_{c}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{16}{81}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{k}\,(kr_{m})^{4}W^{2}(k,r_{m})T^{2}(kr_{m})\Delta^{2}_{\mathrm{S}}(k)\ ,

(108)

\displaystyle\sigma_{cr}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{9}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{k}\,(kr_{m})^{2}W(k,r_{m})W_{s}(k,r_{m})T^{2}(kr_{m})\Delta^{2}_{\mathrm{S}}(k)\ ,

(109)

\displaystyle\sigma_{r}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{k}\,W_{s}^{2}(k,r_{m})T^{2}(kr_{m})\Delta^{2}_{\mathrm{S}}(k)\ ,

(110)

where the transfer function of the scalar perturbations, denoted as
T(kr_{m})
, is given by Eq. (
129
), and the top-hat window function
W(k,r)
and the spherical-shell window function
W_{s}(k,r)
are defined as

\displaystyle W(k,r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3\frac{\sin(kr)-kr\cos(kr)}{(kr)^{3}}\ ,

(111)

\displaystyle W_{s}(k,r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sin(kr)}{kr}\ .

(112)

Specifically, with
\Delta^{2}_{\mathrm{S}}(q)
as assumed in Eq. (
12
) and the spectral width set to
\sigma=1
in this study, numerical integration yields values of
\sigma_{c}^{2}=0.386A_{\mathrm{S}}
,
\sigma_{cr}^{2}=0.187A_{\mathrm{S}}
, and
\sigma_{r}^{2}=0.365A_{\mathrm{S}}
.
By substituting these variances into the integral in Eq. (
106
), we can perform the integral for
\beta
numerically for specific primordial non-Gaussianity parameters.

Figure 24
:
Dependence of the total fraction of primordial black holes and the angular power spectrum of SIGWs at the pivot frequency on PNG with the assumption
|H_{\mathrm{NL}}|A_{\mathrm{S}}^{3/2}=\upsilon|G_{\mathrm{NL}}|A_{\mathrm{S}}=\upsilon^{2}|F_{\mathrm{NL}}|\sqrt{A_{\mathrm{S}}}=\upsilon^{3}
.

In Fig.
24
, we present a numerical example to illustrate the relationship between
\beta
and
\ell(\ell+1)C_{\ell}(\nu_{\ast})
, assuming the spectral amplitude of
\Delta^{2}_{\mathrm{S}}(q)
is
A_{\mathrm{S}}=0.01
.
In this example, we define a quantity
\upsilon
to characterize the ratios among the primordial non-Gaussianity parameters.
Specifically, we assume
|H_{\mathrm{NL}}|A_{\mathrm{S}}^{3/2}=\upsilon|G_{\mathrm{NL}}|A_{\mathrm{S}}=\upsilon^{2}|F_{\mathrm{NL}}|\sqrt{A_{\mathrm{S}}}=\upsilon^{3}
, such that
\upsilon(1+\upsilon+\upsilon^{2})
quantifies the total amplitude of primordial non-Gaussianity.
Therefore, the perturbativity conditions require that
0\leq\upsilon<1
and
\upsilon(1+\upsilon+\upsilon^{2})<1
.
As demonstrated in this figure,
\beta
is significantly enhanced by substantial primordial non-Gaussianity, regardless of the signs of the primordial non-Gaussianity parameters.
This figure indicates that the search of primordial black holes will aid in addressing the sign-degeneracy problem in scalar-induced gravitational waves since
\beta
varies with the sign of any primordial non-Gaussianity parameter.
Despite exhibiting a similar trend in the large
\upsilon
regime,
\beta
displays distinct behaviors for different signs of the primordial non-Gaussianity parameters when primordial non-Gaussianity is small.
Notably, the formation of primordial black holes is suppressed when
F_{\mathrm{NL}}
is negative with a small absolute value.
Observing the two panels of this figure, we can find that the anisotropies in scalar-induced gravitational waves are altered in a remarkable way when primordial non-Gaussianity increases in small
\upsilon
regime, where primordial black holes only constitute a small fraction of the total energy density.
As demonstrated in Section
3
, the isotropic components of scalar-induced gravitational waves remains nearly unchanged in this regime, suggesting that the detection of the anisotropies in the scalar-induced gravitational wave background could provide crucial insights into primordial black hole formation in the early Universe.

7
Conclusion and Discussion

The imprints of primordial non-Gaussianity on the scalar-induced gravitational wave background present a promising avenue for understanding the physics of the early Universe.
In this study, we conduct a thorough analysis of the scalar-induced gravitational wave background in the presence of primordial non-Gaussianity, including its isotropic component, anisotropies, and non-Gaussianity.
These properties are characterized by the energy-density fraction spectrum in Eq. (
39
), the reduced angular power spectrum in Eq. (
62
), the angular bispectrum in Eqs. (
72
,
73
), and the angular trispectrum in Eqs. (
83
,
84
,
85
), which constitute the main results of this work.
In the derivation process, we develop a
“renormalized” diagrammatic approach, extending prior methods
Adshead et al. (2021b)
;
Ragavendra (2022)
;
Li et al. (2023a)
;
Li et al. (2024b)
;
Ruiz and Rey (2025)
;
Abe et al. (2023)
;
Perna et al. (2024)
to simplify calculations of the scalar-induced gravitational wave energy density spectrum with high-order primordial non-Gaussianity.
Our approach is applicable for perturbative local-type primordial non-Gaussianity up to all orders and can be extended to graded scale-dependent primordial non-Gaussianity.
In the case of scale-independent primordial non-Gaussianity up to
h_{\mathrm{NL}}
order, this approach significantly simplifies the calculation of the energy-density fraction spectrum, allowing us to derive the contractions of the multi-point correlators of
\zeta_{S}
directly using Eqs. (
40
,
42
,
44
,
46
,
48
,
50
,
52
,
54
,
56
) without the need for constructing specific diagrams.
Moreover, we extend the computational technique of Ref.
Li et al. (2024a)
to calculate the primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneity up to
\mathcal{O}(A_{\mathrm{L}}^{3})
in Eqs. (
63
,
74
,
87
), making it readily generalizable to primordial non-Gaussianity of all orders.
Consequently, for local-type primordial non-Gaussianity expressed as a power-series expansion up to all orders, one can easily derive the aforementioned spectra of scalar-induced gravitational waves, greatly simplifying the lengthy calculation process.

For primordial non-Gaussianity up to
h_{\mathrm{NL}}
order, we have analyzed the behaviors of the energy-density fraction spectrum
h^{2}\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\nu)
, the reduced angular power spectrum
\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)
, the angular bispectrum
B_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}(\nu)
, and the angular trispectrum
t^{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}_{\ell_{3}\ell_{4}}(L,\nu)
across various scenarios characterized by different primordial non-Gaussianity parameters.
Our analyses indicate that primordial non-Gaussianity may leave significant imprints on the scalar-induced gravitational wave background, particularly regarding its anisotropies and non-Gaussianity.
For
h^{2}\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\nu)
, the numerical results show that the
G
-like family, representing the disconnected component of the four-point correlator of
\zeta
, dominates the total spectrum.
Despite this finding, other families, which represent the contributions from the primordial trispectrum, should not be neglected, especially in the ultraviolet regime.
Even if we eliminate the contributions from loop corrections to the power spectrum of
\zeta
, the primordial trispectrum of
\zeta
can exert non-negligible impacts on
h^{2}\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\nu)
when both
f_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
h_{\mathrm{NL}}
are negative and their modes are large.
For the anisotropies and non-Gaussianity in the scalar-induced gravitational wave background, we have derived explicit expressions for
\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)
,
B_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}(\nu)
, and
t^{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}_{\ell_{3}\ell_{4}}(L,\nu)
at low multipoles, taking into account the Sachs-Wolfe effect and the initial inhomogeneities induced by both the non-adiabaticity and primordial non-Gaussianity.
Although their multipole dependencies parallel those of the cosmic microwave background at low multipoles, they are useful for distinguishing scalar-induced gravitational waves from gravitational waves sourced from other cosmological or astrophysical phenomena.
In contrast, their frequency dependencies exhibit different and non-trivial features, suggesting that the anisotropies and non-Gaussianity in the scalar-induced gravitational wave background encompass valuable information about scalar-induced gravitational waves beyond the isotropic component of the scalar-induced gravitational wave background in their frequency dependencies.
These angular spectra are more sensitive to primordial non-Gaussianity than the energy-density fraction spectrum.
Notably, Gaussian primordial curvature perturbations always yield a Gaussian scalar-induced gravitational wave background, implying that the observation of a non-zero angular bispectrum of scalar-induced gravitational waves can serve as compelling evidence for the presence of primordial non-Gaussianity.
The magnitude of
\ell(\ell+1)C_{\ell}(\nu)
is of order
\mathcal{O}(10^{-7})
at the pivot frequency for the Gaussian case, while the presence of primordial non-Gaussianity may significantly enhance this magnitude.
Additionally, while a larger amplitude of the power spectrum of
\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}
consistently yields an enhanced
h^{2}\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\nu)
, it commonly results in a suppressed
\tilde{C}_{\ell}(\nu)
,
B_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}(\nu)
, and
t^{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}_{\ell_{3}\ell_{4}}(L,\nu)
.
Although the primordial trispectrum of
\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}
leaves a minor imprint on
h^{2}\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\nu)
in most of cases, it has significant impacts on the angular spectra of scalar-induced gravitational waves.
The different behaviors of these angular spectra indicate that they contain a wealth of valuable information about the early Universe, which can be utilized to improve the accuracy of the inferred values of model parameters from
h^{2}\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\nu)
and to resolve the sign degeneracy between
f_{\mathrm{NL}}h_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
g_{\mathrm{NL}}
.

This study has significant implications for the search for primordial black holes, which are associated with the production of scalar-induced gravitational waves and are considered potential candidates for cold dark matter.
The model parameters inferred from the anisotropies in the future detected scalar-induced gravitational wave background can impose constraints on the primordial black hole formation, particularly within the mass ranges corresponding to the frequency bands of the sensitivity regimes of space-borne interferometers
Baker and others (2019)
;
Smith et al. (2019)
;
Hu and Wu (2017)
;
Wang and Han (2021)
;
Ren et al. (2023)
;
Luo and others (2016)
;
Mei and others (2021)
;
Zhou et al. (2023)
;
Seto et al. (2001)
;
Kawamura and others (2021)
;
Crowder and Cornish (2005)
;
Smith and Caldwell (2017)
;
Capurri et al. (2023)
and pulsar timing array programs
Hobbs and others (2010)
;
Demorest and others (2013)
;
Kramer and Champion (2013)
;
Manchester and others (2013)
;
Sesana et al. (2008)
;
Thrane and Romano (2013)
;
Janssen and others (2015)
;
Dewdney et al. (2009)
;
Weltman and others (2020)
;
Moore et al. (2015)
.
Given that the presence of primordial non-Gaussianity has a substantial impact on the primordial black hole formation, this work provides a brief discussion on the relationship between primordial black hole formation and the scalar-induced gravitational wave anisotropies through a simplified example.
Through this example, we demonstrate that the angular power spectrum of scalar-induced gravitational waves is closely related to the primordial black hole mass fraction at the formation epoch.
Notably, although the sign degeneracy between
f_{\mathrm{NL}}h_{\mathrm{NL}}
and
g_{\mathrm{NL}}
cannot be resolved for large
f_{\mathrm{NL}}
in any of the aforementioned spectra of scalar-induced gravitational waves, primordial non-Gaussianity parameters with differing signs result in distinct primordial black hole mass fractions, suggesting that the exploration of primordial black holes can serve as a complementary tool in the search for primordial non-Gaussianity.
In summary, future observations of either the scalar-induced gravitational wave background or the primordial black hole abundance can inform the detection of the other.

We anticipate that future advancements in gravitational wave detectors will enable sensitivity levels at which scalar-induced gravitational waves can be differentiated from other stochastic gravitational wave backgrounds.
Our theoretical predictions for the scalar-induced gravitational wave background can be tested by networks of space-borne gravitational wave detectors

In conclusion, this comprehensive analysis of the imprints of primordial non-Gaussianity on the scalar-induced gravitational wave background is crucial not only for distinguishing scalar-induced gravitational wave signals from the stochastic gravitational wave background but also holds significant implications for exploring the early Universe, particularly regarding inflation models, the nature of cosmological fluids, and the primordial black hole mass fraction at the formation epoch.
The incorporation of primordial non-Gaussianity up to higher orders enables us to search for primordial non-Gaussianity in a more generalized form, and the investigation of higher statistics of the scalar-induced gravitational wave background aids in extracting additional information about the early Universe.

Appendix A
Wigner symbols

Following Ref.
Komatsu (2001)
, we provide a brief summary of the basic properties of the Wigner 3-
j
symbol and the Wigner 6-
j
symbol in this appendix, which is useful in the derivation process of the angular bispectrum and trispectrum of scalar-induced gravitational waves presented in Section
5
.

The Wigner 3-
j
symbol is used to describe the coupling of angular momenta.
In this context, we use
\ell
to denote the eigenvalue of the angular momentum operator, satisfying the equation
\mathbf{L}^{2}Y_{\ell m}=\ell(\ell+1)Y_{\ell m}
, and
m
to represent the eigenvalue of the
z
-component of angular momentum, given by
L_{z}Y_{\ell m}=mY_{\ell m}
.
Thus,
\ell
characterizes the magnitude of the angular momentum, while
m
denotes its direction.
The Wigner 3-
j
symbol,

\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{2}&\ell_{3}\\
m_{1}&m_{2}&m_{3}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(113)

specifically describes a triangle formed by three angular momenta,
\mathbf{L}_{1}+\mathbf{L}_{2}+\mathbf{L}_{3}=0
, indicating that the Wigner 3-
j
symbol is meaningful only if the triples
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})
satisfy the triangle inequalities and
m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}=0
.

The Wigner 3-
j
symbol changes sign under the interchange of any two columns when
\Sigma_{\ell}=\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}
is odd, while it remains invariant under even permutations, such as

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{2}&\ell_{3}\\
m_{1}&m_{2}&m_{3}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{2}&\ell_{3}&\ell_{1}\\
m_{2}&m_{3}&m_{1}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(114)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{2}&\ell_{3}\\
m_{1}&m_{2}&m_{3}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{\Sigma_{\ell}}\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{2}&\ell_{1}&\ell_{3}\\
m_{2}&m_{1}&m_{3}\end{pmatrix}\ .

(115)

Moreover, the sign also changes under the transformation
(m_{1},m_{2},m_{3})\rightarrow(-m_{1},-m_{2},-m_{3})
if
\Sigma_{\ell}
is odd, i.e.,

\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{2}&\ell_{3}\\
m_{1}&m_{2}&m_{3}\end{pmatrix}=(-1)^{\Sigma_{\ell}}\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{2}&\ell_{3}\\
-m_{1}&-m_{2}&-m_{3}\end{pmatrix}\ .

(116)

Therefore, if
m_{1}=m_{2}=m_{3}=0
and
\Sigma_{\ell}
is odd, the Wigner 3-
j
symbol is zero, indicating that
h_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}=0
for odd parity.
In addition, the orthogonality properties of the Wigner 3-
j
symbol reads

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{3}m_{3}}(2\ell_{3}+1)\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{2}&\ell_{3}\\
m_{1}&m_{2}&m_{3}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{2}&\ell_{3}\\
m_{1}^{\prime}&m_{2}^{\prime}&m_{3}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\delta_{m_{1}m_{1}^{\prime}}\delta_{m_{2}m_{2}^{\prime}}\ ,

(117)

\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1}m_{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{2}&\ell_{3}\\
m_{1}&m_{2}&m_{3}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{2}&\ell_{3}^{\prime}\\
m_{1}&m_{2}&m_{3}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{\delta_{\ell_{3}\ell_{3}^{\prime}}\delta_{m_{3}m_{3}^{\prime}}}{2\ell_{3}+1}\ ,

(118)

\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}}\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{2}&\ell_{3}\\
m_{1}&m_{2}&m_{3}\end{pmatrix}^{2}=1\ .

(119)

In our investigation, the Wigner 3-
j
symbols appear in the definition of the scalar-induced gravitational wave angular bispectrum and trispectrum, as discussed in Section
5
, related to the Gaunt integral defined in Eq. (
69
).
The Gaunt integral, denoted as
\mathcal{G}_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}^{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}}
, represents the integration of three spherical harmonics over the same direction, resulting in a real number.
Furthermore, by utilizing the completeness of spherical harmonics, we can express the integral of four spherical harmonics in terms of the Gaunt integrals as follows

\int\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{n}\,\prod_{i=1}^{4}Y_{\ell_{i}m_{i}}(\mathbf{n})=\sum_{LM}(-1)^{M}\mathcal{G}^{m_{1}m_{2}-M}_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}L}\mathcal{G}^{m_{3}m_{4}M}_{\ell_{3}\ell_{4}L}\ .

(120)

This relation is beneficial for deriving the angular trispectrum associated with
\langle\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}^{(3)}\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}^{(1)}\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}^{(1)}\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}^{(1)}\rangle
.

The Wigner 6-
j
symbol, related to the Wigner 3-
j
symbol through

\displaystyle\begin{Bmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{2}&\ell_{3}\\
\ell_{1}^{\prime}&\ell_{2}^{\prime}&\ell_{3}^{\prime}\end{Bmatrix}=(-1)^{\ell_{1}^{\prime}+\ell_{2}^{\prime}+\ell_{3}^{\prime}}\sum_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}}

\displaystyle(-1)^{m_{1}^{\prime}+m_{2}^{\prime}+m_{3}^{\prime}}\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{2}&\ell_{3}\\
m_{1}&m_{2}&m_{3}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{2}^{\prime}&\ell_{3}^{\prime}\\
m_{1}&m_{2}^{\prime}&-m_{3}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

(121)

\displaystyle\times\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{1}^{\prime}&\ell_{2}&\ell_{3}^{\prime}\\
-m_{1}^{\prime}&m_{2}&m_{3}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{2}^{\prime}&\ell_{3}^{\prime}\\
m_{1}&-m_{2}^{\prime}&m_{3}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

expresses the relationship between two distinct couplings of three angular momenta.
Geometrically, the Wigner 6-
j
symbol represents a tetrahedron with the triangle
{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}
as its base and edges
\ell_{i}^{\prime}
opposite to
\ell
.
It is meaningful only if all four triplets satisfy the triangle inequalities.
Moreover, the symbol is invariant under the interchange of any two columns and under the interchange of the upper and lower arguments in any two columns.
Additionally, it obeys

\displaystyle\sum_{L_{1}}(2L_{1}+1)\begin{Bmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{2}&L_{1}\\
\ell_{3}&\ell_{4}&L_{2}\end{Bmatrix}\begin{Bmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{2}&L_{1}\\
\ell_{3}&\ell_{4}&L_{3}\end{Bmatrix}=\frac{\delta_{L_{2}L_{3}}}{2L_{2}+1}\ ,

(122)

\displaystyle\sum_{L_{1}}(-1)^{L_{1}+L_{2}+L_{3}}(2L_{1}+1)\begin{Bmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{2}&L_{1}\\
\ell_{3}&\ell_{4}&L_{2}\end{Bmatrix}\begin{Bmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{2}&L_{1}\\
\ell_{4}&\ell_{3}&L_{3}\end{Bmatrix}=\begin{Bmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{3}&L_{3}\\
\ell_{2}&\ell_{4}&L_{2}\end{Bmatrix}\ .

(123)

One can employ these symmetric properties, as well as the properties of the Wigner 3-
j
symbol, to determine that

\displaystyle\sum_{LM}(2L+1)(-1)^{\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}+M}\begin{Bmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{3}&L^{\prime}\\
\ell_{4}&\ell_{2}&L\end{Bmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{2}&L\\
m_{1}&m_{2}&-M\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{3}&\ell_{4}&L\\
m_{3}&m_{4}&M\end{pmatrix}

(124)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{M^{\prime}}(-1)^{M^{\prime}}\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{1}&\ell_{3}&L^{\prime}\\
m_{1}&m_{3}&-M^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{2}&\ell_{4}&L^{\prime}\\
m_{2}&m_{4}&M^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\ .

This expression is useful for deriving Eq. (
81
).

Appendix B
Equation of motion for SIGWs

In this appendix, we briefly review the derivation of the solution to the equation of motion for scalar-induced gravitational waves, as presented in Eq. (
6
)
Espinosa et al. (2018)
;
Kohri and Terada (2018)
;
Adshead et al. (2021b)
;
Li et al. (2023a)
;
Li et al. (2024b)
.
For convenience, we omit the subscript
in
, which denotes the time of scalar-induced gravitational wave emission, where its omission does not cause confusion.
The equation of motion for scalar-induced gravitational waves is derived from the metric of a perturbed spatially-flat Friedmann-Robertson-Walker spacetime in the conformal Newtonian gauge, which is given by

\mathrm{d}s^{2}=a^{2}\left[-e^{2\Phi}\mathrm{d}\eta^{2}+\left(e^{-2\Phi}\delta_{ij}+\frac{h_{ij}}{2}\right)\mathrm{d}x^{i}\mathrm{d}x^{j}\right]\ .

(125)

Here,
a(\eta)
is the scale factor of the universe at conformal time
\eta
,
\Phi(\eta,\mathbf{x})
denotes the linear scalar perturbations, and the transverse-traceless tensor perturbations
h_{ij}(\eta,\mathbf{x})
represent scalar-induced gravitational waves.
We neglect possible stress anisotropy and other perturbations, as they are negligible in a standard radiation-dominated era filled with perfect and adiabatic cosmological fluids.

For the spatially flat Friedmann-Robertson-Walker spacetime, we introduce the conformal Hubble parameter
\mathcal{H}(\eta)
by defining
\mathcal{H}(\eta)=\partial_{\eta}a/a
, which characterizes the expansion rate of the Universe.
The time evolution of
\mathcal{H}(\eta)
is governed by the zeroth-order Einstein equation, also known as the Friedmann equation.
Assuming a perfect fluid and a fixed equation of state,
w=\bar{P}/\bar{\rho}
, we solve this equation and obtain

\mathcal{H}(\eta)=\frac{2}{(1+3w)\eta}\ ,

(126)

where
w
is the equation-of-state parameter of the Universe, and
\bar{P}
and
\bar{\rho}
denote the background pressure and energy density, respectively.
Given the initial condition, the time evolution of
a(\eta)
can be immediately obtained by solving Eq. (
126
).

Under the perturbed metric in Eq. (
125
), we use the Einstein equation to derive the evolution of the perturbations.
At first order, the Einstein equation yields a master equation that governs the evolution of
\Phi(\eta,\mathbf{q})
.
In particular, in adiabatic scenarios, this master equation can be written as

\partial_{\eta}^{2}\Phi+3\mathcal{H}\left(1+c_{s}^{2}\right)\partial_{\eta}\Phi+3\left(c_{s}^{2}-w\right)\mathcal{H}^{2}\Phi+c_{s}^{2}q^{2}\Phi=0\ ,

(127)

where
c_{s}^{2}
denotes the squared sound speed.
Furthermore,
\Phi(\eta,\mathbf{q})
is commonly expressed in terms of the primordial comoving curvature perturbation
\zeta(\mathbf{q})
and the scalar transfer function
T(q\eta)
, i.e.,

\Phi(\eta,\mathbf{q})=\left(\frac{3+3w}{5+3w}\right)T(q\eta)\zeta(\mathbf{q})\ .

(128)

By substituting Eq. (
128
) into Eq. (
127
), we can solve the differential equation with respect to
q\eta
to obtain the transfer function
T(q\eta)
.
Specifically, in the case of a radiation-dominated era with
w=c_{s}^{2}=1/3
, the transfer function
T(q\eta)
takes the form

T(x)=\frac{9}{x^{2}}\left(\frac{\sin(x/\sqrt{3})}{x/\sqrt{3}}-\cos(x/\sqrt{3})\right)\ ,

(129)

where we use
x=q\eta
for brevity.

For the second-order tensor perturbation
h_{ij}(\eta,\mathbf{q})
, the spatial components of the second-order Einstein equation yield its equation of motion
Ananda et al. (2007)
;
Baumann et al. (2007)
, namely,

\partial_{\eta}^{2}h_{\lambda}(\eta,\mathbf{q})+2\mathcal{H}\partial_{\eta}h_{\lambda}(\eta,\mathbf{q})+q^{2}h_{\lambda}(\eta,\mathbf{q})=4S_{\lambda}(\eta,\mathbf{q})\ ,

(130)

where the source term
S_{\lambda}(\eta,\mathbf{q})
can be expressed in terms of quadratic combinations of the scalar perturbations by combining the lower-order Einstein equation, as given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}(\eta,\mathbf{q})=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{q}_{a}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}

\displaystyle\epsilon_{ij}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{q})q_{a}^{i}q_{a}^{j}\bigg[2\Phi(\eta,\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{a})\Phi(\eta,\mathbf{q}_{a})

(131)

\displaystyle+\frac{4\left(\partial_{\eta}\Phi(\eta,\mathbf{q}_{a})+\mathcal{H}\Phi(\eta,\mathbf{q}_{a})\right)\left(\partial_{\eta}\Phi(\eta,\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{a})+\mathcal{H}\Phi(\eta,\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{a})\right)}{3(1+w)\mathcal{H}^{2}}\bigg]\ ,

where the polarization tensor
\epsilon_{ij}^{\lambda}
is introduced below Eq. (
3
).
We use the Green function method
Espinosa et al. (2018)
;
Kohri and Terada (2018)
to solve Eq. (
130
), i.e.,

a(\eta)h_{\lambda}(\eta,\mathbf{q})=4\int^{\eta}\mathrm{d}\eta^{\prime}\,G_{\mathbf{q}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})a(\eta^{\prime})\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}(\eta^{\prime},\mathbf{q})\ ,

(132)

where the Green function
G_{\mathbf{q}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
satisfies the following differential equation

\partial_{\eta}^{2}G_{\mathbf{q}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})+\left(q^{2}-\frac{\partial_{\eta}^{2}a(\eta)}{a(\eta)}\right)G_{\mathbf{q}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=\delta(\eta-\eta^{\prime})\ ,

(133)

Since Eq. (
126
) yields
\partial_{\eta}^{2}a(\eta)/a(\eta)=2(1-3w)/[(1+3w)\eta]^{2}
, the solution during the radiation-dominated era is given by

G_{\mathbf{q}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=\Theta(\eta-\eta^{\prime})\frac{\sin(q(\eta-\eta^{\prime}))}{q}\ ,

(134)

where
\Theta(x)
is the Heaviside function.
Subsequently, we substitute this solution into Eq. (
132
) to obtain an integral for
h_{\lambda}(\eta,\mathbf{q})
.
Furthermore, as the linear scalar perturbation
\Phi(\eta,\mathbf{q})
is expressed in terms of
\zeta(\mathbf{q})
and
T(q\eta)
in Eq. (
128
), we can reformulate
\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}(\eta,\mathbf{q})
to simplify the integrand.
By substituting Eq. (
128
) into Eq. (
131
), we obtain

\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}(\eta,\mathbf{q})=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{q}_{a}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}q^{2}Q_{\lambda}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{q}_{a})F({\left|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{a}\right|},q_{a},\eta)\zeta(\mathbf{q}_{a})\zeta(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{a})\ ,

(135)

where
Q_{\lambda}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{q}_{a})
denotes the projection factor, defined as
where
Q_{\lambda}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{q}_{a})
denotes the projection factor, defined as

Q_{\lambda}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{q}_{a})=\epsilon_{ij}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{q})\frac{q_{a}^{i}q_{a}^{j}}{q^{2}}=\frac{\sin^{2}\theta}{\sqrt{2}}\times\begin{cases}\cos(2\phi_{a})&\lambda=+\\
\sin(2\phi_{a})&\lambda=\times\end{cases}\ ,

(136)

and the function
F(p_{a},q_{a},\eta)
describes the time evolution of
\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}(\eta,\mathbf{q})
, given by

\displaystyle F(p_{a},q_{a},\eta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3(1+w)}{(5+3w)^{2}}\biggl[2(5+3w)T(p_{a}\eta)T(q_{a}\eta)+\frac{4}{\mathcal{H}^{2}}\partial_{\eta}T(p_{a}\eta)\partial_{\eta}T(q_{a}\eta)

(137)

\displaystyle\hphantom{ \quad\frac{3 (1 + w)}{(5 + 3 w)^{2}} \biggl[ }+\frac{4}{\mathcal{H}}\left(T(p_{a}\eta)\partial_{\eta}T(q_{a}\eta)+\partial_{\eta}T(p_{a}\eta)T(q_{a}\eta)\right)\biggr]\ .

Here,
\theta
denotes the separation angle between
\mathbf{q}
and
\mathbf{q}_{i}
,
\phi_{i}
is the azimuthal angle of
\mathbf{q}_{i}
when
\mathbf{q}
is aligned with the
\mathbf{z}
-axis, and we use
p_{a}=|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{a}|
for brevity.
By substituting Eq. (
135
) into Eq. (
132
), we obtain an expression for
h_{\lambda}
with integration variables
\eta^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{q}_{a}
.
We first integrate over
\eta^{\prime}
, obtaining a kernel function of the form

\displaystyle\hat{I}({\left|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{a}\right|},q_{a},\eta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int^{\eta}\mathrm{d}\eta^{\prime}\,q^{2}G_{\mathbf{q}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})\frac{a(\eta^{\prime})}{a(\eta)}F({\left|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{a}\right|},q_{a},\eta^{\prime})\ .

(138)

Finally, we can express
h_{\lambda}
in terms of
Q_{\lambda}
,
\hat{I}
, and
\zeta
, namely,

\displaystyle h_{\lambda}(\eta,\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{q}_{a}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}\zeta(\mathbf{q}_{a})\zeta(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{a})Q_{\lambda}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{q}_{a})\hat{I}({\left|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{a}\right|},q,\eta)\ .

(139)

This is exactly the expression given in Eq. (
6
).

Appendix C
Feynman-like diagrams

In this appendix, we present specific Feynman-like diagrams of the average energy-density spectrum of scalar-induced gravitational waves for local-type primordial non-Gaussianity up to
H_{\mathrm{NL}}
order.
Based on the discussion in Section
2
, we construct all connections as colored squares within each family, with the figures displayed in
Fig.

26
-
Fig.

34
.
The shaded square in
Fig.

2
can be substituted with any of the colored squares in
Fig.

26
-
Fig.

34
to produce the corresponding complete Feynman-like diagrams, while the background colors of the squares indicate the categories to which the diagrams belong, as illustrated in
Fig.

25
.
Hence, each distinct square corresponds to an independent Feynman-like diagram for Wick’s contractions of
\langle\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}^{4}\rangle
, accounting for all equivalent contractions.
As noted in Section
2
, only some of the categories contribute to the primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneities denoted by
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}
,
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(2)}
and
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(3)}
, respectively.
Diagrammatically, squares with specific background colors in
Fig.

26
to
Fig.

34
can replace the shaded squares in
Fig.

3
to represent specific diagrams for primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneities.
These diagrams can be further combined to generate all specific diagrams shown in
Fig.

11
,
Fig.

15
, and
Fig.

18
.
To provide clarity, we have created a table outlining the average energy-density spectrum for any specific category,
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(a,b,c)}
, in
Table

1
.
This table includes information on the model parameters to which it is proportional, a list of all included diagrams, the background colors within these squares, and the total number of diagrams in this category.
It also specifies whether each category contributes to the primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneities outlined in
Fig.

3
, using a checkmark (✓) to denote contribution and a cross (
\times
) to indicate non-contribution. Importantly, although the red solid lines in the diagrams of
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},1}^{(2)}
and
\omega_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in},2}^{(3)}
depicted in
Fig.

3
connect two vertices on one side, any two vertices are permissible as long as they collectively connect no more than four solid lines.
