Appendix D
Numerical integrals

In Subsection
3.2
, we presented the integrals for all contributions to the scalar-induced gravitational wave average energy density spectrum
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
.
Although multiple integration over three-momenta is natural in theoretical analysis, it is inconvenient for numerical computation.
Therefore, in this appendix, we provide methods to transform the integration variables, recasting the integrals into a form more suitable for numerical evaluation.
The integrands, which consist of two projection factors, the oscillation average of two kernel functions, and several convolved propagators, are transformed accordingly.

Since the expressions for
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{X-\mathrm{like}}
in Eq. (
39
) involve integration over at most five three-momenta, we first introduce five sets of variables
(u_{i},v_{i},\phi_{i})
with
i=1,2,3,4,5
to replace
\mathbf{q}_{i}
, i.e.,

v_{i}=\frac{q_{i}}{q}\ ,\qquad\qquad\quad u_{i}=\frac{{\left|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{i}\right|}}{q}\ ,

(140)

and
\phi_{i}
is the azimuthal angle of
\mathbf{q}_{i}
, as defined below Eq. (
136
).
We further transform
(u_{i},v_{i})
to
(s_{i},t_{i})
as follows,

s_{i}=u_{i}-v_{i}\ ,\qquad\qquad\quad t_{i}=u_{i}+v_{i}-1\ ,

(141)

ensuring that the integration limits for different variables are independent of each other.
Hence, the integration measure transforms to

\prod_{i}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{q}_{i}}{(2\pi)^{3}}=\frac{q^{3}}{2(2\pi)^{3}}\prod_{i}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t_{i}\int_{-1}^{1}\mathrm{d}s_{i}\,v_{i}u_{i}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}\phi_{i}\right)\ .

(142)

Using the new variables, the
(\xi-1)
-fold convolved propagator
P^{[\xi]}(q)
, introduced in Eqs. (
34
,
35
), becomes

\displaystyle P^{[1]}(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi^{2}}{q^{3}}\frac{A_{\mathrm{S}}}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{\ln^{2}(q/q_{\ast})}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)\ ,

(143)

\displaystyle P^{[\xi]}(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{q^{3}}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\tilde{t}\int_{-1}^{1}\mathrm{d}\tilde{s}\,\tilde{v}\tilde{u}P^{[\xi-1]}(\tilde{v}q)P^{[1]}(\tilde{u}q)\ ,\ \text{where }\xi\geq 2,\xi\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\ ,

(144)

where we have transformed
\tilde{\mathbf{q}}
into
(\tilde{v},\tilde{u})
in a manner similar to Eq. (
140
), and the transformations from
(\tilde{u},\tilde{v})
to
(\tilde{s},\tilde{t})
are analogous to Eq. (
141
).
While
P^{[1]}(q)
is analytic, the numerical results for
P^{[\xi]}(q)
can be obtained directly by performing the numerical integration in Eq. (
144
).
Next, we analyze the arguments involved in these convolved propagators.
According to
Fig.

23
, the integral for
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{G-\mathrm{like}}
involves only
P^{[\xi]}(\mathbf{q}_{1})
and
P^{[\xi]}(|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{1}|)
, which can be expressed as
P^{[\xi]}(v_{1}q)
and
P^{[\xi]}(u_{1}q)
using Eqs. (
140
).
In other words, the integrand for
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{G-\mathrm{like}}
is independent of the azimuthal angles, resulting in a
2\pi
factor after integrating over
\phi_{1}
.
However, the integrals for other families in Eq. (
39
) also involve
P^{[\xi]}(|\mathbf{q}_{i}-\mathbf{q}_{j}|)
and
P^{[\xi]}(|\mathbf{q}_{i}+\mathbf{q}_{j}-\mathbf{q}_{k}|)
.
Thus, we introduce a new variable
y_{ij}
to characterize the inner product of
\mathbf{q}_{i}
and
\mathbf{q}_{j}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle y_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{q}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{q}_{j}}{q^{2}}

(145)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\cos\varphi_{ij}}{4}\sqrt{t_{i}(t_{i}+2)(1-s_{i}^{2})t_{j}(t_{j}+2)(1-s_{j}^{2})}+\frac{1}{4}[1-s_{i}(t_{i}+1)][1-s_{j}(t_{j}+1)]\ ,

where we represent
\varphi_{ij}=\phi_{i}-\phi_{j}
as the difference between the two azimuthal angles.
Furthermore, we introduce quantities of the form

\displaystyle w_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{{\left|\mathbf{q}_{i}-\mathbf{q}_{j}\right|}}{q}=\sqrt{v_{i}^{2}+v_{j}^{2}-2y_{ij}}\ ,

(146)

\displaystyle w_{ijk}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{{\left|\mathbf{q}_{i}+\mathbf{q}_{j}-\mathbf{q}_{k}\right|}}{q}=\sqrt{v_{i}^{2}+v_{j}^{2}+v_{k}^{2}+2y_{ij}-2y_{ik}-2y_{jk}}\ ,

(147)

so that
P^{[\xi]}(|\mathbf{q}_{i}-\mathbf{q}_{j}|)
and
P^{[\xi]}(|\mathbf{q}_{i}+\mathbf{q}_{j}-\mathbf{q}_{k}|)
can be written as
P^{[\xi]}(w_{ij}q)
and
P^{[\xi]}(w_{ijk}q)
, respectively.
Consequently,
\mathcal{P}^{X-\mathrm{like}}
in Eq. (
39
) can be expressed as a function of
v_{i}
,
u_{i}
, and
\phi_{i}
for any family.

Ultimately, to rewrite the kernel function
\hat{I}\left(|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{i}|,q_{i},\eta\right)
during the radiation-dominated era in terms of the variables
u_{i}
,
v_{i}
, and
x=q\eta
, we recast it in the following form

\hat{I}\left({\left|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{i}\right|},q_{i},\eta_{\mathrm{in}}\right)=I_{\mathrm{RD}}\left(\frac{{\left|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{i}\right|}}{q},\frac{q_{i}}{q},q\eta_{\mathrm{in}}\right)=I_{\mathrm{RD}}(u_{i},v_{i},x)\ .

(148)

The integral for
\hat{I}\left(|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{i}|,q_{i},\eta\right)
in Eq. (
138
) can then be rewritten with respect to
u_{i}
and
v_{i}
accordingly.
As elaborated in Refs.
Kohri and Terada (2018)
;
Espinosa et al. (2018)
, this integral admits an analytical result on subhorizon scales (
x_{\mathrm{in}}\gg 1
), given by

\displaystyle I_{\mathrm{RD}}(u_{i},v_{i},x\gg 1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{x}I_{A}(u_{i},v_{i})\left[I_{B}(u_{i},v_{i})\sin x-\pi I_{C}(u_{i},v_{i})\cos x\right]\ ,

(149a)

\displaystyle I_{A}(u_{i},v_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3\left(u_{i}^{2}+v_{i}^{2}-3\right)}{4u_{i}^{3}v_{i}^{3}}\ ,

(149b)

\displaystyle I_{B}(u_{i},v_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4u_{i}v_{i}+\left(u_{i}^{2}+v_{i}^{2}-3\right)\ln{\left|\frac{3-(u_{i}+v_{i})^{2}}{3-(u_{i}-v_{i})^{2}}\right|}\ ,

(149c)

\displaystyle I_{C}(u_{i},v_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(u_{i}^{2}+v_{i}^{2}-3\right)\Theta\left(u_{i}+v_{i}-\sqrt{3}\right)\ .

(149d)

Furthermore, using this formula for
I_{\mathrm{RD}}(u_{i},v_{i},x\gg 1)
, we can express the oscillation average of two kernel functions with the same
x
as
Adshead et al. (2021b)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muI_{\mathrm{RD}}(u_{i},v_{i},x\gg 1)I_{\mathrm{RD}}(u_{j},v_{j},x\gg 1)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

(150)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{I_{A}(u_{i},v_{i})I_{A}(u_{j},v_{j})}{2x^{2}}\left[I_{B}(u_{i},v_{i})I_{B}(u_{j},v_{j})+\pi^{2}I_{C}(u_{i},v_{i})I_{C}(u_{j},v_{j})\right]\ .

As for the projection factor defined in Eq. (
136
), we can deduce
\cos\theta=(1+v_{i}^{2}-u_{i}^{2})/(2v_{i})
from Eq. (
140
) and rewrite
Q_{\lambda}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{q}_{i})
in terms of
(u_{i},v_{i},\phi_{i})
as follows

Q_{\lambda}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{q}_{i})=\frac{\bigl[(v_{i}+u_{i})^{2}-1\bigr]\bigl[1-(v_{i}-u_{i})^{2}\bigr]}{\sqrt{2}}\times\begin{cases}\cos(2\phi_{i})&\lambda=+\\
\sin(2\phi_{i})&\lambda=\times\end{cases}\ .

(151)

For brevity, we introduce a new function that combines the kernel function with the projection factor as follows

J(u_{i},v_{i},x)=\frac{x}{8}\bigl[(v_{i}+u_{i})^{2}-1\bigr]\bigl[1-(v_{i}-u_{i})^{2}\bigr]I_{\mathrm{RD}}(u_{i},v_{i},x)\ .

(152)

Using this function, the two projection factors and two kernel functions in the integrand of Eq. (
39
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda}Q_{\lambda}^{2}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{q}_{1})\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\hat{I}^{2}({\left|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{1}\right|},q_{1},\eta)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{x^{2}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muJ^{2}(u_{1},v_{1},x)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\ ,

(153)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda}Q_{\lambda}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{q}_{1})Q_{\lambda}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{q}_{2})\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\hat{I}({\left|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{1}\right|},q_{1},\eta)\hat{I}({\left|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}_{2}\right|},q_{2},\eta)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\cos(2\varphi_{12})}{x^{2}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muJ(u_{1},v_{1},x)J(u_{2},v_{2},x)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\ .

Consequently, the integrands in Eq. (
39
) can be written in terms of
P^{[\xi]}
and
J
, with the integration variables being
u_{i}
,
v_{i}
, and
\varphi_{ij}
(or equivalently
s_{i}
,
t_{i}
, and
\varphi_{ij}
), where
1\leq i,j\leq 5
.

Appendix E
Infrared scaling of the SIGW energy-density spectra

In this appendix, we present a verification of the infrared scaling of the scalar-induced gravitational wave energy density spectra discussed in Subsection
6.1
.
As shown in Appendix
D
, the integrand of the energy density fraction spectrum in Eq. (
39
) is primarily composed of two elements:
P^{[\xi]}(q)
, as defined in Eqs. (
143
,
144
), and
J(u_{i},v_{i},x)
, given in Eq. (
152
).
We first analyze the behavior of
P^{[\xi]}(q)
. Subsequently, we examine the behavior of the integrals associated with the various diagrams contributing to the scalar-induced gravitational wave energy density spectrum.

For the
(\xi-1)
-fold convolved propagator, while
P^{[1]}
in Eq. (
143
) is analytic, we focus on the infrared behavior of
P^{[\xi]}
for
\xi\geq 2
.
As indicated in Eq. (
144
),
P^{[\xi]}(q)
is represented by convolution integrals involving
\tilde{u}P^{[1]}(\tilde{u}q)
.
To examine the behavior of
P^{[\xi]}(q)
, we first introduce certain approximations in the integration involving
\tilde{u}P^{[1]}(\tilde{u}q)
. Since
\tilde{u}P^{[1]}(\tilde{u}q)
is predominantly localized around the pivot wavenumber
\tilde{u}q=q_{\ast}e^{-2\sigma^{2}}
, we introduce a parameter
\varepsilon
to delineate the peak, infrared, and ultraviolet regions of
P^{[1]}(q)
.
The region satisfying
q<q_{-}=q_{\ast}e^{-(2+\varepsilon)\sigma^{2}}
is defined as the infrared region, while
q>q_{+}=q_{\ast}e^{-(2-\varepsilon)\sigma^{2}}
corresponds to the ultraviolet region.
The intermediate region,
q_{-}\leq q\leq q_{+}
, is identified as the peak region.
For
P^{[1]}(q)
in the integrand of Eq. (
144
), we neglect the contributions from both the infrared and ultraviolet regions and retain only those from the peak region
q_{-}\leq q\leq q_{+}
, thereby adjusting the integration limits.
Furthermore, we assume
\varepsilon\geq 5
to ensure the accuracy of this approximation.
For
\sigma=1
and
\varepsilon=5
in this work, the infrared region is given by
q/q_{\ast}\lesssim 9.1\times 10^{-4}
.
Nevertheless, to maintain generality, we leave
\varepsilon
as a free parameter in the following analysis.

Figure 35
:
Approximation of the integration domain of Eq. (
155
), where the left panel showcases the case where
q\leq 2q_{-}
, while the right panel illustrates the scenario where
2q_{-}<q<2q_{+}
. The orange region represents the original integration domain and the green region indicates the approximated integration domain. The red point signifies the location where the integrand achieves its maximum value. The region between the two red dashed lines denotes the peak region
(2q_{-}/q_{\ast}<\tilde{v}_{q}+\tilde{u}_{q}<2q_{+}/q_{\ast})
for the integrand, with
\varepsilon
taken as a very small constant for clarification.

Using this approximation, the integral for
P^{[2]}(q)
can be written as

\displaystyle P^{[2]}(q)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\frac{q^{3}}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{\max(2q_{-}/q-1,0)}^{\max(2q_{+}/q-1,0)}\mathrm{d}\tilde{t}\int_{-1}^{1}\mathrm{d}\tilde{s}\,\tilde{v}\tilde{u}P^{[1]}(\tilde{v}q)P^{[1]}(\tilde{u}q)

(155)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{4\sigma^{2}q_{\ast}^{2}q}\int_{\max(2q_{-}/q_{\ast}-q/q_{\ast},0)}^{\max(2q_{+}/q_{\ast}-q/q_{\ast},0)}\mathrm{d}\tilde{t}_{q}\int_{-q/q_{\ast}}^{q/q_{\ast}}\mathrm{d}\tilde{s}_{q}\,\frac{1}{(\tilde{v}_{q}\tilde{u}_{q})^{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\ln^{2}\tilde{v}_{q}+\ln^{2}\tilde{u}_{q}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)\ ,

where
\tilde{u}_{q}=\tilde{u}q/q_{\ast}
,
\tilde{v}_{q}=\tilde{v}q/q_{\ast}
,
\tilde{s}_{q}=\tilde{s}q/q_{\ast}
, and
\tilde{t}_{q}=\tilde{t}q/q_{\ast}
, while the relationships between
(\tilde{u},\tilde{v})
and
(\tilde{s},\tilde{t})
are analogous to those between
(u_{i},v_{i})
and
(s_{i},t_{i})
in Eq. (
141
).
Since
\tilde{s}_{q}
and
\tilde{t}_{q}
are orthometric, the area of the approximated integration domain is

S(\varepsilon)=2\frac{q}{q_{\ast}}\left(\max(2q_{+}-q,0)-\max(2q_{-}-q,0)\right)=\begin{cases}4(q_{+}-q_{-})q\quad&q\leq 2q_{-}\\
2(2q_{+}-q)q&2q_{-}<q<2q_{+}\\
0&q\geq 2q_{+}\end{cases}\ .

(156)

In particular,
S(\varepsilon)=0
indicates that the value of the integral is negligible and can be approximated as zero for
q\geq 2q_{+}
.
We can then employ the first mean value theorem to estimate the value of the integral.
Before proceeding, we adopt
\tilde{u}_{q}
and
\tilde{v}_{q}
in the subsequent analysis for simplification, transforming the integration domain to
|\tilde{v}_{q}-q/q_{\ast}|<\tilde{u}_{q}<\tilde{v}_{q}+q/q_{\ast}
with
\tilde{v}_{q}>0
.
The approximated integration domain with respect to these variables is illustrated in
Fig.

35
, which visualizes the domain for different values of
q
in the following analysis.
Applying the first mean value theorem, there exists a specific point
(\tilde{v}_{q}^{*},\tilde{u}_{q}^{*})
within the integration domain that allows the integral to be simplified as

P^{[2]}(q)\simeq\frac{S(\varepsilon)}{2q}\frac{\pi}{2\sigma^{2}q_{\ast}^{2}}\frac{1}{(\tilde{v}_{q}^{\ast}\tilde{u}_{q}^{\ast})^{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\ln^{2}\tilde{v}_{q}^{\ast}+\ln^{2}\tilde{u}_{q}^{\ast}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)\ .

(157)

Here and hereinafter, we use the superscript
∗
to denote a particular value of the integration variable within the integration interval.
Moreover, as long as our approximation remains valid, the contributions from outside the peak region are negligible. Thus, we expect that this point
(\tilde{v}_{q}^{*},\tilde{u}_{q}^{*})
lies within the square region
(q_{-}/q_{\ast},q_{+}/q_{\ast})\times(q_{-}/q_{\ast},q_{+}/q_{\ast})
.
In this region, it is straightforward to see that the integrand attains its minimum at
\tilde{u}_{q}=\tilde{v}_{q}=q_{+}/q_{\ast}
for
q<2q_{+}
(or equivalently, at
\tilde{u}_{q}=\tilde{v}_{q}=q_{-}/q_{\ast}
for
q\leq 2q_{-}
), and achieves its maximum at
\tilde{u}_{q}=\tilde{v}_{q}=e^{-2\sigma^{2}}
.
Since the integrand is continuous in the domain, we further propose that
(\tilde{v}_{q}^{*},\tilde{u}_{q}^{*})
can be located on the line
\tilde{u}_{q}=\tilde{v}_{q}
within the approximated integration domain.
Along this line, as
\tilde{u}_{q}=\tilde{v}_{q}
decreases from
q_{+}/q_{\ast}
, the value of the integrand increases and reaches a maximum at
e^{-2\sigma^{2}}
.
Therefore, for a given
q
, we further postulate that

\displaystyle\tilde{v}_{q}^{\ast}=\tilde{u}_{q}^{\ast}=e^{-(2-c)\sigma^{2}}\ ,\qquad

(158)

where
c\in(0,\varepsilon)
.
Substituting Eqs. (
156
,
158
) into Eq. (
157
), we can rewrite
P^{[2]}(q)
as

P^{[2]}(q)\simeq\frac{\pi e^{(2-c^{2})\sigma^{2}}}{2\sigma^{2}q_{\ast}^{2}}\times\begin{cases}2q_{\ast}(e^{\varepsilon\sigma^{2}}-e^{-\varepsilon\sigma^{2}})\quad&q\leq 2q_{-}\\
(2q_{\ast}e^{\varepsilon\sigma^{2}}-q)&2q_{-}<q<2q_{+}\\
0&q\geq 2q_{+}\end{cases}\ .

(159)

Strictly speaking,
c
is a variable dependent on
q
, since the first mean value theorem is applied for a specific value of
q
.
According to Eq. (
157
), we may use
2qP^{[2]}(q)/S(\varepsilon)
, which can be interpreted as the average line density of
P^{[2]}(q)
with respect to
\tilde{t}_{q}
, to assess how
q
influences the choice of the specific point
(\tilde{v}_{q}^{*},\tilde{u}_{q}^{*})
.
In the region where
q<2q_{\ast}e^{-2\sigma^{2}}
, the approximated integration domain spans the entire peak region of the integrand, as illustrated in the left panel of
Fig.

35
.
Moreover, the integrand in Eq. (
155
) attains its maximum at
\tilde{s}_{q}=0
for any
\tilde{t}_{q}
and varies slowly with respect to
\tilde{s}_{q}
.
Hence,
2qP^{[2]}(q)/S(\varepsilon)
decreases only slightly as
q
increases.
However, this effect is negligible in the infrared region where
q
is very small.
In other words,
P^{[2]}(q)
is nearly scale-invariant for
q\leq 2q_{-}
.
For
2q_{-}<q<2q_{+}
, as shown in the right panel of
Fig.

35
, the region between the two red dashed lines exceeds the size of the green region, implying that the approximated integration domain covers only part of the peak region.
Furthermore, the occupied area decreases as
q
increases, indicating that
P^{[2]}(q)
decreases monotonically with increasing
q
.
Specifically,
P^{[2]}(q)
appears to decrease more rapidly with increasing
q
when
q<2q_{\ast}e^{-2\sigma^{2}}
.
Therefore, we may further approximate
P^{[2]}(q)
as scale-invariant for
q\leq 2\sqrt{q_{-}q_{+}}=2q_{\ast}e^{-2\sigma^{2}}
, and as zero for
q>2\sqrt{q_{-}q_{+}}=2q_{\ast}e^{-2\sigma^{2}}
, i.e.,

P^{[2]}(q)\simeq\begin{cases}P^{[2]}_{\mathrm{IR}}\quad&q\leq 2q_{\ast}e^{-2\sigma^{2}}\\
0&q>2q_{\ast}e^{-2\sigma^{2}}\end{cases}\ ,

(160)

where
P^{[2]}_{\mathrm{IR}}
is a constant representing the infrared limit of
P^{[2]}(q)
.

Figure 36
:
Numerical results for
P^{[\xi]}(q)
.

According to the above approximation in Eq. (
160
), we can express
P^{[3]}(q)
as

\displaystyle P^{[3]}(q)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\frac{q^{3}P^{[2]}_{\mathrm{IR}}}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{e^{-2\sigma^{2}}q_{\ast}/q}\tilde{v}\mathrm{d}\tilde{v}\int_{|1-\tilde{v}|}^{1+\tilde{v}}\tilde{u}\mathrm{d}\tilde{u}\,P^{[1]}(\tilde{u}q)

(161)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{\sigma^{2}/2}P^{[2]}_{\mathrm{IR}}}{2\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma}\frac{q_{\ast}}{q}\int_{0}^{2e^{-2\sigma^{2}}}\tilde{v}_{q}\mathrm{d}\tilde{v}_{q}\int_{\ln|q/q_{\ast}-\tilde{v}_{q}|)}^{\ln(q/q_{\ast}+\tilde{v}_{q})}\mathrm{d}a\exp\left[-\left(\frac{a+\sigma^{2}}{\sqrt{2}\sigma}\right)^{2}\right]\ .

The result of this integral can be written in terms of error functions.
Despite its lengthy form,
P^{[3]}(q)
remains nearly scale-invariant for
q/q_{\ast}<2e^{-2\sigma^{2}}
and decreases rapidly for
q/q_{\ast}\geq 2e^{-2\sigma^{2}}
.
Thus, similar to the simplification of
P^{[2]}(q)
in Eq. (
160
),
P^{[3]}(q)
can be approximated in the same way.
In particular, the infrared limit of
P^{[3]}(q)
can be written as

\frac{P^{[3]}_{\mathrm{IR}}}{P^{[2]}_{\mathrm{IR}}}\simeq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{0}^{\ln(2e^{-2\sigma^{2}})}e^{-\chi^{2}/(2\sigma^{2})}\mathrm{d}\chi\ .

(162)

For
\sigma=1
in this work, we have
P^{[3]}_{\mathrm{IR}}/P^{[2]}_{\mathrm{IR}}\simeq 0.1
.
By employing induction, we conclude that for
\xi\geq 2
, the behavior of
P^{[\xi]}(q)
given by Eq. (
144
) exhibits similar features.
The above analyses can be validated by the numerical results, as shown in
Fig.

36
.
In this figure, it is evident that
P^{[\xi]}(q)
for
\xi\geq 2
, as anticipated, is nearly scale-invariant in the infrared region.
Additionally, although our analysis predicts a step at
2e^{-2\sigma^{2}}
for
\xi\geq 2
, the figure indicates that
P^{[\xi]}(q)
begins to decrease rapidly at a higher wavenumber as
\xi
increases.
Hence,
P^{[\xi+1]}_{\mathrm{IR}}/P^{[\xi]}_{\mathrm{IR}}
for
\xi\geq 2
is expected to increase with increasing
\xi
.
In fact, the numerical results show that
P^{[3]}_{\mathrm{IR}}/P^{[2]}_{\mathrm{IR}}\simeq 0.24
and
P^{[4]}_{\mathrm{IR}}/P^{[3]}_{\mathrm{IR}}\simeq 0.41
.
Despite these differences, this approximation remains sufficiently accurate for the subsequent analysis.

Next, we analyze the behavior of the full integrand in the infrared region.
This analysis is analogous to those in previous works
Yuan et al. (2020)
;
Yuan et al. (2023)
;
Cai et al. (2019a)
;
Cai et al. (2020)
, which have discussed similar features in the infrared region for scalar-induced gravitational waves without primordial non-Gaussianity or with lower-order primordial non-Gaussianity.
As shown in Appendix
D
, in the infrared region where
v_{i}q,u_{i}q\gg q_{\ast}
,
v_{i}
and
u_{i}
are of the same order due to the relation
|u_{i}-v_{i}|<1
for any
i
.
Similarly,
w_{ij}
and
w_{ijk}
, defined in Eqs. (
146
), are also of
\mathcal{O}(v_{i})
.
Moreover, in the integral for the scalar-induced gravitational wave energy-density fraction spectrum, i.e., Eq. (
39
), each integration over a three-momentum contributes a factor of
\mathcal{O}(v_{i}^{2})
according to Eq. (
142
).
Since
P^{[\xi]}(q)
is nearly scale-invariant in the infrared region for
\xi\geq 2
, we define its dimensionless form as

\Delta^{2}_{[\xi]}(q)=\frac{q^{3}}{2\pi^{2}}P^{[\xi]}(q)\ ,

(163)

where
\Delta^{2}_{[\xi]}(q)=\Delta^{2}_{\mathrm{gS}}(q)
is given by Eq. (
12
).
For any
\xi
,
\Delta^{2}_{[\xi]}(q)
exhibits a single peak.
This peak is located at
q=q_{\ast}
for
\xi=1
, and is situated near
2q_{\ast}e^{-2\sigma^{2}}
for
\xi\geq 2
.
Therefore, for simplicity, we continue to use
q_{-}\leq v_{i}q\leq q_{+}
as the region that predominantly contributes to the integral from
\Delta^{2}_{[\xi]}(v_{i}q)
.
We do not further specify the values of
q_{-}
and
q_{+}
, treating them as fixed parameters.
With this approximation, the integration limit is set to
[q_{-}/q,q_{+}/q]
with respect to the variable
v_{i}
.
To eliminate
q
from the integration limit, we transform the variable
v_{i}
to
v_{q,i}=v_{i}q/q_{\ast}
, recasting the integration limit as
[q_{-}/q_{\ast},q_{+}/q_{\ast}]
.
Similarly,
u_{i}
is transformed to
u_{q,i}=u_{i}q/q_{\ast}
.
Both
u_{q,i}
and
v_{q,i}
are of order one when the integrand is sizable, regardless of
\xi
.

In the infrared region where
q\ll q_{-}
, we find that
v_{i}\geq q_{-}/q\gg 1
.
Consequently,
u_{i}
and all the relevant
w_{ij}
and
w_{ijk}
are much greater than one.
Therefore, the kernel functions introduced in Eq. (
149
) can be approximated as

\displaystyle I_{\mathrm{RD}}^{\mathrm{IR}}(u_{i},v_{i},x\gg 1)\simeq\frac{1}{x}\frac{3\left(u_{i}^{2}+v_{i}^{2}\right)^{2}}{4u_{i}^{3}v_{i}^{3}}\left[\sin x\ln\frac{(u_{i}+v_{i})^{2}}{3}-\pi\cos x\right]\ .

(164)

Then,
J(u_{i},v_{i},x)
can be expressed as

J_{\mathrm{IR}}(u_{i},v_{i},x)=\frac{x}{8}(v_{i}+u_{i})^{2}I_{\mathrm{RD}}^{\mathrm{IR}}(u_{i},v_{i},x)\ .

(165)

Notably, after the transformation of variables
(u_{i},v_{i})\rightarrow(u_{q,i},v_{q,i})
, we have
J_{\mathrm{IR}}(u_{i},v_{i},x)=J_{\mathrm{IR}}(u_{q,i},v_{q,i},x)
.
Therefore, by substituting Eqs. (
164
,
165
) into Eqs. (
153
,
), we find that for
u_{q,i}\sim v_{q,i}\sim 1
,

\displaystyle J_{\mathrm{IR}}(u_{q,i},v_{q,i},x)\propto\ln^{2}\frac{(u_{q,1}+v_{q,1})^{2}q_{\ast}^{2}}{3q^{2}}\simeq\ln^{2}\frac{4q_{\ast}^{2}}{3q^{2}}\ .

(166)

According to the above analysis, we can evaluate the infrared behavior of the scalar-induced gravitational wave average energy-density spectrum
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
in Eq. (
39
) for different diagram families.
For a diagram
X
belonging to the
G
-like family, we approximate
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{G-\mathrm{like}}
and employ the first mean value theorem, yielding

\displaystyle\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{X\in G-\mathrm{like}}\bigg|_{\mathrm{IR}}

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{F}^{X}q^{2}q_{\ast}^{4}}{24\pi}\int_{q_{-}/q_{\ast}}^{q_{+}/q_{\ast}}\mathrm{d}v_{q}\int_{v_{q}-q/q_{\ast}}^{v_{q}+q/q_{\ast}}\mathrm{d}u\,v_{q}u_{q}\frac{\Delta^{2}_{[\alpha]}(v_{q}q_{\ast})}{(v_{q}q_{\ast})^{3}}\frac{\Delta^{2}_{[\beta]}(u_{q}q_{\ast})}{(u_{q}q_{\ast})^{3}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muJ_{\mathrm{IR}}^{2}(u_{q},v_{q},x)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\ ,

(167)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{X}\frac{q_{+}-q_{-}}{12\pi q_{\ast}}\left(\frac{q}{q_{\ast}}\right)^{3}\frac{\Delta^{2}_{[\alpha]}(v_{q}^{\ast}q_{\ast})}{(v_{q}^{\ast})^{2}}\frac{\Delta^{2}_{[\beta]}(u_{q}^{\ast}q_{\ast})}{(u_{q}^{\ast})^{3}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muJ_{\mathrm{IR}}^{2}(u_{q}^{\ast},v_{q}^{\ast},x)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\ ,

where
\mathcal{F}^{X}
is the product of the primordial non-Gaussianity parameters multiplied by the symmetry factor of diagram
X
.
It is reasonable to assume that both
v_{q}^{*}
and
u_{q}^{*}
lie within the interval
(q_{-}/q_{\ast},q_{+}/q_{\ast})
.
Furthermore, by substituting Eq. (
166
) into Eq. (
167
), we obtain

\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{X\in G-\mathrm{like}}\bigg|_{\mathrm{IR}}\propto\left(\frac{q}{q_{\ast}}\right)^{3}\ln^{2}\frac{4q_{\ast}^{2}}{3q^{2}}\ .

(168)

Similarly, for a diagram
X
in the
C
-like family,

\displaystyle\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{X\in C-\mathrm{like}}\bigg|_{\mathrm{IR}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{F}^{X}qq_{\ast}^{8}}{96\pi^{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\left[\int_{q_{-}/q_{\ast}}^{q_{+}/q_{\ast}}\mathrm{d}v_{q,i}\int_{v_{q,i}-q/q_{\ast}}^{v_{q,i}+q/q_{\ast}}\mathrm{d}u_{q,i}\,v_{q,i}u_{q,i}\right]\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}\varphi_{12}\,\cos(2\varphi_{12})\frac{\Delta^{2}_{[\alpha]}(v_{q,2}q_{\ast})}{(v_{q,2}q_{\ast})^{3}}

(169)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\frac{q^{5}}{3(2\pi)^{3} \mathcal{H}^{2}}}\times\frac{\Delta^{2}_{[\beta]}(u_{q,2}q_{\ast})}{(u_{q,2}q_{\ast})^{3}}\frac{\Delta^{2}_{[\beta]}(w_{q,12}q_{\ast})}{(w_{q,12}q_{\ast})^{3}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muJ_{\mathrm{IR}}(u_{q,1},v_{q,1},x)J_{\mathrm{IR}}(u_{q,2},v_{q,2},x)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\ ,

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{F}^{X}}{12\pi}\left(\frac{q_{+}-q_{-}}{q_{\ast}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{q}{q_{\ast}}\right)^{3}\cos(2\varphi_{12}^{\ast})\frac{\Delta^{2}_{[\alpha]}(v_{q,2}^{\ast}q_{\ast})}{(v_{q,2}^{\ast})^{3}}\frac{\Delta^{2}_{[\beta]}(u_{q,2}^{\ast}q_{\ast})}{(u_{q,2}^{\ast})^{3}}\frac{\Delta^{2}_{[\beta]}(w_{q,12}^{\ast}q_{\ast})}{(w_{q,12}^{\ast})^{3}}

\displaystyle\times\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muJ_{\mathrm{IR}}(u_{q,1}^{\ast},v_{q,1}^{\ast},x)J_{\mathrm{IR}}(u_{q,2}^{\ast},v_{q,2}^{\ast},x)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\ .

By substituting Eq. (
166
) into this expression, we find

{\left|\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{X\in C-\mathrm{like}}\bigg|_{\mathrm{IR}}\right|}\propto\left(\frac{q}{q_{\ast}}\right)^{3}\ln^{2}\frac{4q_{\ast}^{2}}{3q^{2}}\ ,

(170)

where the absolute value sign arises from the sign ambiguity of
\cos(2\varphi_{12}^{*})
.
By the same method, we prove that
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{X}\big|_{\mathrm{IR}}
for diagrams in other families all share a common logarithmic slope, i.e.,

n_{\mathrm{gw}}^{X}(q)=\frac{\partial\ln{\left|\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{X}(q)\right|}}{\partial\ln q}=3-\frac{4}{\ln^{2}\left(\frac{4\nu_{\ast}^{2}}{3\nu^{2}}\right)}\ .

(171)

According to
Table

1
, we can sum
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{X}
over a certain category to obtain
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(a,b,c)}
. The above relation indicates that
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(a,b,c)}\big|_{\mathrm{IR}}
in all categories exhibits the same behavior in the infrared region, i.e.,

\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{(a,b,c)}\bigg|_{\mathrm{IR}}\propto\left(\frac{q}{q_{\ast}}\right)^{3}\ln^{2}\frac{4q_{\ast}^{2}}{3q^{2}}\ ,

(172)

which is precisely Eq. (
90
) in Subsection
6.1
.

Appendix F
Present-day density contrast of SIGWs

In this appendix, we present the derivation of the present-day scalar-induced gravitational wave energy density contrast
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}
, as described by Eq. (
22
).
This contrast consists of two components: the initial inhomogeneities and the propagation effects. Specifically, the initial inhomogeneities comprise contributions from both primordial non-Gaussianity-induced fluctuations and those induced by large-scale effects.
Since the main text focuses on the primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneity, we first provide additional details regarding the initial inhomogeneity arising from large-scale effects.
The graviton propagation, derived using the Boltzmann equation for gravitons, will be discussed later.

We begin by providing an overview of the initial inhomogeneity arising from non-adiabaticity.
In this study, we consider scalar-induced gravitational waves produced on subhorizon scales, and the gravitational wave energy density derived from Eq. (
2
) is indeed only valid on such scales.
However, when examining the initial density contrasts along two observational directions, we must account for the gravitational wave energy density modulated by large-scale perturbations.
Following the approach in Refs.
Mierna et al. (2024)
;
Valbusa Dall’Armi et al. (2023)
;
Valbusa Dall’Armi et al. (2024)
, the modulated gravitational wave energy density
\tilde{\rho}_{\mathrm{gw}}
is derived as follows.
As shown in Eq. (
9
), we have separated the primordial curvature perturbations
\zeta
into short- and long-wavelength modes.
The short-wavelength mode is responsible for the production of scalar-induced gravitational waves, while the long-wavelength mode modulates their energy-density distribution on large scales.
Consequently, the linear scalar perturbations
\Phi
, which are proportional to
\zeta
in Eq. (
128
), can also be decomposed into these two modes as follows

\Phi(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})=\Phi_{\mathrm{S}}(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})+\Phi_{\mathrm{L}}(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})\ .

(173)

Notably,
\Phi_{\mathrm{S}}(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})
(or
\Phi_{\mathrm{L}}(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})
) is related to
\zeta_{\mathrm{S}}
(or
\zeta_{\mathrm{L}}
) in a manner analogous to the relationship between
\Phi(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})
and
\zeta
in Eq. (
128
).
Moreover, because the wavelength of
\Phi_{\mathrm{L}}
is much larger than the horizon scale, we neglect its spatial derivatives.
In other words,
\Phi_{\mathrm{L}}
can be treated as independent of conformal time
\eta
during scalar-induced gravitational wave emission.
Therefore, we can absorb
\Phi_{\mathrm{L}}
into the scale factor and coordinates in the metric given by Eq. (
125
) through the redefinition

\displaystyle\tilde{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-\Phi_{\mathrm{L}}}a\ ,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\tilde{\eta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{2\Phi_{\mathrm{L}}}\mathrm{d}\eta\ ,

(174)

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{2\Phi_{\mathrm{L}}}h_{ij}\ .

The metric from Eq. (
125
) can then be written as

\mathrm{d}s^{2}=\tilde{a}^{2}\left[-e^{2\Phi_{\mathrm{S}}}\mathrm{d}\tilde{\eta}^{2}+\left(e^{-2\Phi_{\mathrm{S}}}\delta_{ij}+\frac{\tilde{h}_{ij}}{2}\right)\mathrm{d}x^{i}\mathrm{d}x^{j}\right]\ .

(175)

Thus, the large-scale effects are naturally incorporated if we evaluate scalar-induced gravitational wave production under this metric.
By substituting
\tilde{h}_{ij}
into Eq. (
2
), the modulated energy density of scalar-induced gravitational waves at the emission moment is given by

\tilde{\rho}_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})=\frac{m_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}{16\tilde{a}^{2}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\partial_{\tilde{\eta}_{\mathrm{in}}}\tilde{h}_{ij}(\tilde{\eta}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})\partial_{\tilde{\eta}_{\mathrm{in}}}\tilde{h}_{ij}(\tilde{\eta}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\frac{m_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}e^{2\Phi_{\mathrm{L}}}}{16a^{2}(\eta)}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\partial_{\eta_{\mathrm{in}}}h_{ij}(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})\partial_{\eta_{\mathrm{in}}}h_{ij}(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\ .

(176)

We use a tilde above these quantities to indicate the inclusion of the non-adiabaticity.
Using Eq. (
176
), the total density contrast can be obtained via Eqs. (
1
,
16
), respectively.
By excluding the primordial non-Gaussianity-induced inhomogeneity
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
, we define the initial inhomogeneity arising from non-adiabaticity (NAD) as follows

\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{NAD}}=\tilde{\delta}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}-\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}=e^{2\Phi_{\mathrm{L}}}-1\simeq 2\Phi_{\mathrm{L}}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})\ .

(177)

Additionally, large-scale effects exert no influence on the scalar-induced gravitational wave average energy-density spectrum
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
.
Since
\langle\Phi_{\mathrm{L}}\rangle=0
,
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}
remains unchanged when Eq. (
176
) is used to derive it using Eqs. (
15
).

Next, we analyze the propagation effects using the Boltzmann equation for gravitons, following Refs.
Contaldi (2017)
;
Bartolo et al. (2019)
;
Bartolo et al. (2020b)
.
The distribution function
f(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})
for gravitons is introduced in connection with the expression for the energy density
\rho_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,\mathbf{x})
, i.e.,

\rho_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{a^{4}}\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{q}\,qf(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})\ .

(178)

Comparing with Eq. (
1
), the relation between
f(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})
and
\omega_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})
is given directly by

f(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})=\rho_{\mathrm{c}}\left(\frac{a}{q}\right)^{4}\omega_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})\ .

(179)

As we have decomposed
\omega_{\mathrm{gw}}
into the average component
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw}}
and the fluctuation
\delta_{\mathrm{gw}}
in Eq. (
16
), we may similarly decompose
f(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})
into a background component
\bar{f}(\eta,q)
and perturbations
\Gamma(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})
, namely,

f(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})=\bar{f}(\eta,q)-q\frac{\partial\bar{f}}{\partial q}\Gamma(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})\ .

(180)

By comparing with Eq. (
16
), we obtain the relation between
\bar{f}
and
\bar{\omega}_{\mathrm{gw}}
, and between
\Gamma
and
\delta_{\mathrm{gw}}
.
Further employing Eq. (
19
), the former can be written as

\bar{f}(\eta,q)=\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{c}}}{4\pi}\left(\frac{a}{q}\right)^{4}\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,q)\ .

(181)

Using this formula and Eq. (
25
), one can express
\partial\bar{f}/\partial q
in relation to
n_{\mathrm{gw}}
.
This allows
\Gamma(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})
to be represented in terms of
\delta_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\Gamma(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(4-\frac{\partial\ln\bar{\Omega}_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,q)}{\partial\ln q}\right)^{-1}\delta_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})

(182)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(4-n_{\mathrm{gw}}(\nu)\right)^{-1}\delta_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})\ .

The distribution function
f(\eta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{q})
for any particle species evolves according to the Boltzmann equation, i.e.,

\frac{\mathrm{d}f}{\mathrm{d}\eta}=\mathcal{I}(f)+\mathcal{C}(f)\ ,

(183)

where
\mathcal{I}
denotes the emissivity term and
\mathcal{C}
stands for the collision term.
Collisions between gravitons are generally negligible, i.e.,
\mathcal{C}=0

Contaldi (2017)
;
Bartolo et al. (2018)
;
Flauger and Weinberg (2019)
.
Moreover, for gravitational waves sourced by cosmological mechanisms, the emissivity term can be regarded as the initial condition, implying
\mathcal{I}=0

Bartolo et al. (2019)
;
Bartolo et al. (2020b)
.
Therefore, gravitons propagate freely once produced, and the Boltzmann equation simplifies to

\frac{\mathrm{d}f}{\mathrm{d}\eta}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial\eta}+\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x^{i}}{\mathrm{d}\eta}+\frac{\partial f}{\partial q}\frac{\mathrm{d}q}{\mathrm{d}\eta}+\frac{\partial f}{\partial n^{i}}\frac{\mathrm{d}n^{i}}{\mathrm{d}\eta}=0\ .

(184)

To linear order, the perturbed geodesics of massless gravitons yield the following relations

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}x^{i}}{\mathrm{d}\eta}=n^{i}\ ,\quad\frac{\mathrm{d}q}{\mathrm{d}\eta}=\left(\partial_{\eta}\Phi-n^{i}\partial_{i}\Phi\right)q\ ,\quad\frac{\mathrm{d}n^{i}}{\mathrm{d}\eta}=0\ .

(185)

By substituting Eq. (
180
) and Eqs. (
185
) into Eq. (
184
), we obtain the evolution equations for the background and perturbation terms individually, i.e.,

\displaystyle\partial_{\eta}\bar{f}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\ ,

(186)

\displaystyle\partial_{\eta}\Gamma+n^{i}\partial_{i}\Gamma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\eta}\Phi-n^{i}\partial_{i}\Phi\ .

(187)

Eq. (
186
) implies that the background part
\bar{f}
remains invariant throughout cosmological evolution.
Regarding the perturbations, we can express Eq. (
187
) in Fourier space, denoted as

\partial_{\eta}\Gamma+ik\mu\Gamma=\partial_{\eta}\Phi-ik\mu\Phi\ ,

(188)

where we introduce
\mu=\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{n}/k
as the cosine of the angle between
\mathbf{k}
and
\mathbf{n}
.
The solution to this equation is given by
Contaldi (2017)
;
Bartolo et al. (2019)
;
Bartolo et al. (2020b)

\displaystyle\Gamma(\eta_{0},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{ik\mu(\eta_{\mathrm{in}}-\eta_{0})}\left(\Gamma(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})+\Phi(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{k})\right)+2\int_{\eta_{\mathrm{in}}}^{\eta_{0}}\mathrm{d}\eta\,e^{ik\mu(\eta-\eta_{0})}\partial_{\eta}\Phi(\eta,\mathbf{k})\ ,

(189)

where the monopole term
\Phi(\eta_{0},\mathbf{k})
has been neglected.
This formula describes the perturbations in the graviton distribution in the present-day Universe.
By employing the line-of-sight condition
\mathbf{x}_{0}-\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}}=(\eta_{0}-\eta_{\mathrm{in}})\mathbf{n}
, this result can be recast as the density contrast in real space as follows
Li et al. (2023a)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{\delta}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})+\left(4-n_{\mathrm{gw}}(\nu)\right)\Phi(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})

(190)

\displaystyle+2\left(4-n_{\mathrm{gw}}(\nu)\right)\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{0}}\int_{\eta_{\mathrm{in}}}^{\eta_{0}}\mathrm{d}\eta\,e^{ik\mu(\eta-\eta_{0})}\partial_{\eta}\Phi(\eta,\mathbf{k})\ .

The three terms on the right-hand side represent, from left to right, the initial density contrast, the Sachs-Wolfe effect
Sachs and Wolfe (1967)
, and the integrated Sachs-Wolfe effect
Sachs and Wolfe (1967)
.
In addition, since the Sachs-Wolfe and integrated Sachs-Wolfe effects are generated by long-wavelength scalar modes that re-entered the Hubble horizon during matter domination, the integrated Sachs-Wolfe effect is typically subdominant and can be neglected
Bartolo et al. (2020a)
;
Valbusa Dall’Armi et al. (2021)
.
Notably, we use
\tilde{\delta}_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})
rather than
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})
to represent the initial density contrast, as it more comprehensively incorporates the inhomogeneity arising from non-adiabaticity in Eq. (
177
).
By separating
\delta_{\mathrm{gw}}(\eta_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{q})
into
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})
and
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{NAD}}
and substituting Eq. (
177
) into Eq. (
190
), we have

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{gw},\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{q})+\left(6-n_{\mathrm{gw}}(\nu)\right)\Phi_{\mathrm{L}}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}})\ .

(191)

This result is precisely Eq. (
22
).
Additionally, since we are interested in low multipoles, we ignore the gravitational lensing effects, which are expected to mainly affect
\delta_{\mathrm{gw},0}
at higher multipoles
Dodelson (2003)
.

Appendix G
Comparison with previous works

The energy-density fraction spectrum of scalar-induced gravitational waves in the presence of local-type non-Gaussianity has attracted extensive interest in recent years, with many works
Adshead et al. (2021b)
;
Ragavendra (2022)
;
Abe et al. (2023)
;
Yuan et al. (2023)
;
Perna et al. (2024)
;
Li et al. (2023a)
;
Li et al. (2024b)
;
Ruiz and Rey (2025)
;
Wang et al. (2024a)
;
Yu and Wang (2024)
;
Iovino et al. (2024a)
;
Zeng et al. (2024)
;
Cai et al. (2019a)
;
Unal (2019)
;
Atal and Domènech (2021)
;
Yuan and Huang (2021)
;
Chang et al. (2024)
;
Nakama et al. (2017)
;
Garcia-Bellido et al. (2017)
;
Ragavendra et al. (2021)
;
Zhang (2022)
;
Lin et al. (2023)
;
Chen et al. (2022a)
;
Cai et al. (2019b)
;
Papanikolaou et al. (2024)
;
He et al. (2024)
;
Rey (2024)
;
Pi (2024)
;
Zhou et al. (2025)
dedicated to this topic.
In this appendix, we compare our work with previous studies
Unal (2019)
;
Atal and Domènech (2021)
;
Adshead et al. (2021b)
;
Ragavendra (2022)
;
Abe et al. (2023)
;
Li et al. (2023a)
;
Li et al. (2024b)
;
Perna et al. (2024)
;
Ruiz and Rey (2025)
that also employ a diagrammatic approach to compute
\Omega_{\mathrm{gw},0}
, highlighting the specific advances presented here.
